<meta>This page provides a biographical introduction to the commentator, Mahavir Prasad Srivastava, and outlines the publication history of this scientific commentary on the Surya Siddhanta.</meta>

# Introduction

The late Mahavir Prasad Srivastava was born on the second day of the bright fortnight of the month of Kartik in the year 1944 of the <term>Vikrama Samvat<note>The Vikrama Samvat is a historical Hindu calendar era that began in 57 BCE. To find the Common Era (CE) date, one generally subtracts 57 years from the Samvat year.</note></term>, corresponding to October 18, 1887, in the village of Bizhauli, Handia Tehsil, in the district of Allahabad. He passed his High School (1906) and Intermediate examinations (1908) at the Kayastha Pathshala in Allahabad, and earned his Bachelor of Science degree (1910) from Muir Central College. Due to financial difficulties, he took up employment. In the month of Kartik, Samvat 1976 (c. 1919 CE), he began writing the *Vijnana Bhashya* <note>original: "विज्ञानभाष्य"; literally, "Scientific Commentary"</note> on the <term>Surya Siddhanta</term>, which was published serially in the monthly magazine of the Science Council. This commentary was completed in Samvat 1987 (c. 1930 CE). The Science Council published it in book form in two volumes.

For this work, he was honored with the **Mangala Prasad Prize** <note>A prestigious literary award established in 1923 for excellence in Hindi literature and scholarship.</note> and the **Chunnilal Award**.

Since the first edition of this work has sold out, it is now being republished due to the constant requests of readers.

Accompanied by its peerless "Scientific Commentary," this edition of the *Surya Siddhanta* is a priceless treasure of ancient Indian literature and a source of great pride for the Hindi language. The *Surya Siddhanta* is useful and worthy of collection for all libraries and seekers of knowledge.

# Preface

Several volumes of ancient literature <note>original: "vangmaya"; refers to the collective body of classical or sacred texts</note> have already been published by the Dr. Ratnakumari Research Institute—including the works of Swami Satyaprakash and Dr. Usha Jyotishmati, such as the *Bakhshali Manuscript* and the *Sulba-Sutras* <note>Ancient Indian texts on mathematics and geometry</note> (in Sanskrit and English). In this same tradition, we are now publishing the *Scientific Commentary* <note>original: "Vigyan Bhashya"</note> on the *Surya-Siddhanta* <note>The "Sun-Treatise," a foundational text of Indian astronomy</note> by the late Shri Mahavir Prasad Srivastava. We have attained this honor through the generosity of the <term>Vigyan-Parishad<note>The Science Council</note></term>, Prayag, for which we are indebted to the Council’s officials.

Performing the expensive task of publishing this work would have been difficult for us had we not received financial assistance from the respected <term>Raisahab<note>A title of honor used during the British Raj and continued informally</note></term> Chaudhary Pratap Singh Ji of Karnal and the trust established by him. We are extremely grateful to Chaudhary Saheb. At present, we are publishing the first volume, which contains the <term>Madhyamadhikara<note>The chapter concerning the "Mean Motions" of the planets</note></term>, the <term>Spashtadhikara<note>The chapter concerning the "True Positions" of the planets</note></term>, and the <term>Triprashnadhikara<note>The chapter concerning the "Three Problems" of direction, place, and time</note></term>. This work will be completed in its entirety with the second volume.

S. Ranganayaki,
M.Sc., D.Phil, D.Sc.
Director

<term>Phalguni Purnima<note>The full moon day of the Hindu lunar month of Phalguna, which usually marks the festival of Holi</note></term>
6 March 1982.

<meta>This page continues the Introduction (Bhumika) following the administrative Preface. # Introduction

In his immortal work **The Light of Truth** <note>original: "Satyarth Prakash"</note>, published in 1874 AD, Swami Dayananda Saraswati provided a comprehensive curriculum for the study and teaching of school graduates. This curriculum holds historical significance because, prior to Rishi Dayananda's plan, there was no all-encompassing system of curricula in this country. In 1858 AD, three universities were opened in the country—those of Calcutta, Madras, and Bombay. In 1888 AD, two more universities (after the death of Maharishi Dayananda) were established in Prayag <note>modern-day Prayagraj</note> and Lahore. Sanskrit examinations of the kind held in Kashi <note>Varanasi</note> had also not yet begun. Therefore, we can say with certainty that Rishi Dayananda’s comprehensive curriculum method was the first effort in this direction. After the study of grammar and related subjects, the Rishi suggested teaching the <term>Manusmriti<note>The Laws of Manu; an ancient legal and social text</note></term>, Valmiki's <term>Ramayana<note>The epic poem detailing the life of Rama</note></term>, the <term>Mahabharata<note>The great epic of the Bharata dynasty</note></term>, the <term>Six Systems of Philosophy<note>original: "Shad Darshan"; the six orthodox schools of Hindu philosophy</note></term>, the <term>Brahmana Texts<note>Expository layers of the Vedas</note></term>, and the <term>Upavedas<note>Secondary Vedic sciences</note></term> (specifically <term>Ayurveda<note>The science of medicine</note></term>, <term>Dhanurveda<note>Military science/archery</note></term>, <term>Gandharvaveda<note>The science of music and dance</note></term>, and <term>Arthaveda-Shilpadi<note>Economics, mechanics, and the arts</note></term>). He then wrote that for two years, students should properly learn the <term>Science of Light<note>original: "Jyotish Shastra"; encompassing astronomy and astrology</note></term> and the <term>Sun-Treatise<note>original: "Surya-Siddhanta"</note></term>, which includes <term>Algebra<note>original: "Bijaganita"</note></term>, Arithmetic, <term>Geography<note>original: "Bhugol"</note></term>, <term>Astronomy<note>original: "Khagol"</note></term>, and <term>Geology<note>original: "Bhugarbha Vidya"</note></term>.

The original source of astronomical subjects is the verses of the Vedas. The 164th hymn of the first book of the <term>Rigveda<note>The oldest of the four Vedas</note></term> is named the **Asyavamaiya Hymn**. The seer <note>original: "mantradrashta rishi"; the sage who "saw" or realized the sacred verse</note> of this hymn is **Dirghatamas**. This hymn is an inspiring source for the science of astronomy—

> The wheel of Order <note>original: "Rta"; the cosmic law</note> with twelve spokes turns round and round the heavens; it never ages, it is never weary. Here, O Fire-God <note>Agni</note>, stand the seven hundred and twenty sons in pairs.
->(Rigveda 1/164/11)<-

The sun's wheel, which has twelve spokes <note>representing the twelve months</note>, turns continuously; it never tires, never decays, and never dies. It has 720 "sons" (representing 360 days and 360 nights). In the <term>Atharvaveda<note>The fourth Veda, dealing with daily life and rituals</note></term>, there are two hymns in the nineteenth book (hymns 7 and 8) whose seer is **Gargya**. In these hymns, a calculation of the 27 lunar mansions <note>original: "Nakshatras"</note> is given. Taking inspiration from the Vedas, the **Sage Lagadha** composed the <term>Vedanga Jyotish<note>The astronomical auxiliary to the Vedas</note></term>, whose verses are found under the names *Rig-Jyotish* and *Yajur-Jyotish*. Lagadha's era is considered to be before 600 BCE. Another term for astronomy is the **Science of Time Determination** <note>original: "Kala-Vidhana-Shastra"; see Yajur-Jyotish 3</note>. Lagadha also used the word "Mathematics" <note>original: "Ganita"</note> in this same sense, and expressed the majesty of this branch of the Vedic limbs in this way—

<summary>This page explains the historical significance of Swami Dayananda's 19th-century curriculum and traces the origins of Indian astronomy (Jyotish) back to the Rigveda and the works of Sage Lagadha.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Vedic curriculum by narrowing the focus to the science of astronomy (Jyotish). It transitions from the general educational reforms of Swami Dayananda to the specific historical texts of Indian mathematical astronomy, setting the stage for the current edition of the Surya Siddhanta.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>

->Just as the crest is to the peacocks, and the gems are to the serpents,<-
->So does Mathematics stand at the head of the auxiliary sciences of the Vedas.<-
->(Yajur Jyotish, Verse 4)<-

The beauty that the crest holds in a peacock’s body, and the majesty that the gem holds on a serpent’s head, is the same status that Mathematics—specifically mathematical astronomy—holds among the <term>Vedangas<note>The six auxiliary disciplines of the Vedas: phonetics, ritual, grammar, etymology, meter, and astronomy.</note></term>.

Following the era of the *Vedanga Jyotisha*, several astronomical doctrines known as <term>Siddhantas<note>A Sanskrit term for a formal treatise or settled doctrine within a science.</note></term> became prevalent in the field of astronomy. Among these, five doctrines hold special importance. Varahamihira <note>A 6th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note> conducted a comparative analysis of these in his famous work, the "**Panchasiddhantika**" <note>The Five Treatises</note>. The five doctrines are listed in the following verse:

->The Paulisha, Romaka, Vasishtha, Saura, and Paitamaha are the five doctrines.<-

Of these five, the first two (the *Paulisha* and the *Romaka*) were analyzed by Latadeva. From Varahamihira's perspective, the calculations in the *Paulisha* doctrine are accurate, and the *Romaka* doctrine is nearly the same. Even clearer than these two is the *Savitra* doctrine—that is, the *Saura* or **Surya Siddhanta**. The remaining two, the *Vasishtha* and *Paitamaha*, are considered corrupted or inaccurate. An edition of the *Panchasiddhantika* was published with an English translation by Professor G. Thibaut and Mahamahopadhyaya Sudhakara Dvivedi. The name of Sudhakara Ji's Sanskrit commentary is "**Panchasiddhantika Prakashika**" (Prayag, December 15, 1888).

In this same tradition, Shri Mahavir Prasad Srivastava, a veteran member of the Science Council (*Vijnana Parishad*) in Prayag, took up the arduous task of creating a scientific commentary (*Vijnana-Bhashya*) on the **Surya Siddhanta**. This monumental lifelong work was completed in 1940 AD. The work began in 1912; its first chapter (on mean motions) was published in 1914. The twelfth chapter was printed in 1931. Work then halted due to a lack of funds. The final two chapters were eventually printed in 1940. Thus, this commentary was completed on the festival of <term>Bhratri Dvitiya<note>Also known as Bhai Dooj; a Hindu festival celebrating the bond between brothers and sisters</note></term> in the Vikrami year 1967, which corresponds to 1940 AD. Shri Srivastava's work was published in installments in the *Vijnana* monthly magazine, and some separate copies were also prepared in book form. For nearly the last 20 years, this scientific commentary on the **Surya Siddhanta** has been unavailable.

The Dr. Ratnakumari Swadhyaya Sansthan is honored that, through the immense generosity of the officials of the Science Council of Prayag, permission for its publication was granted to this institute. The Swadhyaya Sansthan has taken upon itself the project of publishing priceless works of ancient literature. We are publishing this work in two volumes. The printing blocks for the illustrations within the book were [provided] by the Science Council...

The work was prepared and handed over to the Institute. Shri Shrikrishna Srivastava, a retired judge and son of the late Mahavir Prasad Srivastava, took a personal interest in this endeavor. Dr. Shivgopal Mishra, the General Secretary of the Science Council <note>Vigyan Parishad</note>, carried out the proof-correction of the entire text himself with great diligence and devotion. The original Sanskrit text of the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>The Sun Doctrine, a foundational astronomical treatise</note> is provided here based on the version edited by Professor Kripashankar Shukla of Lucknow University. We also received significant assistance in refining the text from our senior member, Pandit Omkarnath Sharma. We are grateful to all of them.

The late Shri Srivastava had written an extensive introduction (43 pages long) for the book, which was published with the final volume in 1940. Regrettably, this introduction could not be included in its entirety in the current edition. We are providing only some essential portions of it here. The late Shri Srivastava also provided extremely useful material on Indian astronomy in the book "**Saral Vigyansagar**" <note>The Simple Ocean of Science, 1946</note>, published by the Science Council of Prayag. It would be excellent if that material could be published separately.

The late Shri Mahavir Prasad Srivastava dedicated this scientific commentary <note>Vigyan-Bhashya</note> to his late, revered father. The words of the dedication were: "In holy memory of my respected father, at whose feet I learned my first lesson in mathematics."

<meta>This page continues from the general editorial history into the specific contents of the "Commentary Author’s Introduction" written by Mahavir Prasad Srivastava. # Introduction by the Commentator

## [ A brief excerpt from the introduction written by the late Shri Mahavir Prasad Srivastava ]

Astronomy <term>Jyotish Shastra; literally 'the science of light'</term> is the primary limb of the Veda because it is used to determine the correct time for performing sacrifices. Therefore, in ancient India, the study and teaching of astronomy was considered a meritorious and holy act. Its other names are the "Science of Time Regulation" <note>Kala-vidhana-shastra</note> or "Knowledge of Time" <note>Kala-gyana</note>. According to the **Kashyapa Samhita** <note>an ancient Sanskrit text attributed to the sage Kashyapa</note>, there were 18 founding teachers <term>Acharyas</term> of astronomy, whose names are:—

1—Surya <note>The Sun</note>, 2—Pitamaha <note>Brahma, the Creator</note>, 3—Vyasa, 4—Vasishtha, 5—Atri, 6—Parashara, 7—Kashyapa, 8—Narada, 9—Garga, 10—Marichi, 11—Manu, 12—Angira, 13—Lomasha, 14—Paulisha, 15—Chyavana, 16—Yavana, 17—Bhrigu, and 18—Shaunaka.

The original theoretical treatises <term>Siddhanta-grantha</term> of all these 18 names listed here are not currently known. Among these figures, several are authors of both Compendiums <term>Samhita</term> and Treatises <term>Siddhanta</term>; some deal with only one of these two subjects; and for some, no surviving text exists in either category under their name.

The ancient doctrines <term>Siddhantas</term> that have been discussed from the time of **Varahamihira** until today are: 1—**Paulisha**, 2—**Romaka**, 3—**Vasishtha**, 4—**Saura**, and 5—**Paitamaha** doctrines. These were collected by Varahamihira (around 550 AD) in his book titled **Pancha-Siddhantika** <note>The Five Treatises</note>. In that work, he also explained that the Paulisha doctrine is accurate, the Romaka doctrine is close to it, but the most accurate is the **Surya-Siddhanta** <note>The Sun Doctrine</note>; the remaining two are highly corrupted. On page 2 of the commentary *Pancha-siddhantika-prakashika*, **Mahamahopadhyaya Sudhakar Dwivedi** <note>a renowned 19th-century mathematician and astronomer</note> quotes the "Dialogue between the Sun and Aruna" <note>Suryaruna-Samvada</note> and states: the knowledge told by the Great Sage Pulisha (based on the wisdom of sages like Garga) is the **Paulisha Siddhanta**. The knowledge which the Sun God—having been born in the city of **Rome** due to a Brahmin's curse—explained to the **Yavana** <note>historically referring to Greeks or Westerners</note> people, is the **Romaka Siddhanta**. That which Vasishtha gave to his son Parashara is the **Vasishtha Siddhanta**. That which the Sun gave to the demon **Maya** <note>Maya Daitya, the celestial architect</note> is the **Saura Siddhanta**, and that which Brahma gave to his son Vasishtha is the **Paitamaha Siddhanta**.

It is not mentioned where this "Dialogue between the Sun and Aruna" was taken from. According to this dialogue, the Sun God is the instructor of both the Romaka and Saura doctrines; the first—

<meta>Text ends abruptly at bottom of page.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the spiritual and scientific importance of astronomy (Jyotish) in the Vedic tradition, listing the eighteen founding sages and discussing the five major historical astronomical systems (Siddhantas) compiled by Varahamihira.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotish Shastra, Eighteen Acharyas, Varahamihira, Pancha-Siddhantika, Surya Siddhanta, Romaka Siddhanta, Sudhakar Dwivedi, Indian Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the eighteen teachers of astronomy and the five foundational doctrines (Siddhantas). Having listed the systems, the author now addresses the origins of the Romaka and Surya Siddhantas and the historical independence of Indian astronomical thought.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

The City of Rome <term>Romaka-nagara</term> was explained to be the home of the Ionians <term>Yavanas; often used to refer to Greeks or westerners</term>. Another teacher mentioned is Maya the Daitya <note>an architect of the Asuras in mythology</note>, whose specific location is unknown, though Western scholars consider him a Greek (Yavana) and Indian scholars consider him a resident of Assyria or Babylonia. However, the astronomer Brahmagupta (628 AD) considered only the **Romaka-Siddhanta** to be foreign <note>literally "outside of the tradition/Smriti"</note>, but not the **Saura-Siddhanta** <note>the Solar Doctrine</note>. In verses 2-6 of the first chapter of the current **Surya-Siddhanta**, it is written that at the end of the Golden Age <term>Satya-yuga</term>, an Asura named Maya performed penance to the Sun God <term>Surya</term> to learn astronomy. The Sun God then appointed a part of himself <term>Amsha-purusha; an emanation or personified portion</term> for this task. This emanated person taught the entire **Surya-Siddhanta**—which the Sun God himself had taught to the Great Sages <term>Maharishis</term> in earlier ages—to Mayasura, from whom the Sages later received it again.

If the Vedic collections <term>Samhitas</term>, the ritual texts <term>Brahmanas</term>, and the philosophical <term>Upanishads</term> are studied with an impartial mind, it will be found that even in that prehistoric era, experiences of celestial observation are scattered throughout, and they were debated extensively. If this were not the case, where would the concept of the equinoctial points <term>Ayanavindu</term> being in different lunar mansions <term>Nakshatras</term> come from? How else could they have known the relationship between the seasons, months, lunar dates <term>Tithis</term>, and stars? Even in medieval India, there was no shortage of intelligent and eagle-eyed astronomers and mathematicians. Those who read the works of Aryabhata, Varahamihira, Brahmagupta, Munjala, Keshava, Ganesha, and others can see what subtle observations they wrote regarding celestial events. Now, scholars like Shankar Balkrishna Dixit and Prabodh Chandra Sengupta have also proven through comparative studies of Eastern and Western astronomers that Indian astronomy is entirely independent of the systems of Hipparchus or Ptolemy <note>famous Greek astronomers</note>.

It appears clearly from the **Aryabhatiya** <note>the seminal work of Aryabhata, c. 499 AD</note> that Aryabhata created his text by churning the ancient astronomy of India and by personally observing the Sun, Moon, planets, and stars.<gloss>1</gloss>

G. R. Kaye writes, "There is no doubt that Varaha—

---

<margin>1</margin> <gloss>By the grace of God, I have rescued the jewel of true knowledge from the ocean of both true and false knowledge, using the boat of my own intellect. (49)</gloss>
<gloss>That which was previously known as the doctrine of the self-born [Brahma] is now called the Aryabhatiya; whoever slanders this work destroys the merit of their good deeds and their life. (50)</gloss>
<gloss>The Sun is determined by the rotation of the Earth; the Moon is determined by its conjunction with the Sun; and all the stars and planets are determined by their conjunction with the Moon and the stars. (48)</gloss> <note>These verses are from the "Golapada" or spherical section of the Aryabhatiya, emphasizing the observational basis of Aryabhata's work.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the origins of the Romaka and Surya Siddhantas, focusing on the figure of Mayasura and the argument that Indian astronomy developed independently of Greek influence through indigenous observation and the Vedic tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Romaka-Siddhanta, Surya-Siddhanta, Mayasura, Aryabhata, Varahamihira, Indian Astronomy, Hipparchus, Ptolemy, Nakshatras</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical comparison between Indian astronomical systems and those of the West (specifically Greek/Yavana), focusing on the internal evidence of the precession of the equinoxes to determine if Indian scholars like Varahamihira were merely copying Greek sources or working from independent observation.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

"Mihira [Varahamihira] had the theoretical treatises of the Greeks (Yavanas) available to him, and from his style of interpretation, it appears that he was introducing new ideas—or at least ideas and words that were not common to both traditions—into his own works. It is certain that some of these ideas date to a time after Ptolemy." To refute this opinion, the strongest evidence seems to me to be the fact that <term>Varahamihira</term> <note>a 6th-century Indian astronomer and polymath</note> showed an ignorance of the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> (the <term>movement of the spring equinox</term>), even though he was aware that in his own time, the <term>southern solstice</term> <note>Dakshinayana; the sun's southernmost point</note> occurred at the beginning of the zodiac sign of Cancer or in the <term>Punarvasu lunar mansion</term> <note>Nakshatra</note>, whereas in ancient times it occurred at the midpoint of the <term>Ashlesha</term> constellation.1 If he had been familiar with the works of <term>Hipparchus</term> or <term>Ptolemy</term>, he would have certainly accepted the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> <note>the slow, circular shifting of Earth's axis that changes which stars are visible at the equinoxes</note> as they described it. This point was also not addressed by <term>Brahmagupta</term> <note>the 7th-century mathematician who first defined rules for computing with zero</note>, which reveals that until Brahmagupta’s time, the concept of the precession of the equinoxes had not troubled medieval Indian astronomers. This is despite the fact that through direct observation of the sky in ancient times, they had realized that the constellations associated with the <term>northern solstice</term> <note>Uttarayana</note> and <term>southern solstice</term> had changed. We know this because the <term>Maitrayani Upanishad</term> mentions that the southern solstice occurred when the sun was between the Magha constellation and the middle of <term>Dhanishta</term>, and the northern solstice occurred when the sun was between the <term>middle of Shravishtha</term> and Ashlesha. In the <term>Vedanga Jyotisha</term> <note>one of the oldest Indian texts on astronomy, used to time rituals</note> as well, it is clearly stated that the northern solstice began when the sun entered the beginning of Shravishtha. Discussing this very shift, Varahamihira explains that in our time, the southern solstice occurs in Punarvasu; if it should differ from this in the future, one must determine it through direct observation.2

From the figures mentioned above, it is clear that the Indian values for the <term>sidereal year</term> <note>the time it takes for the Sun to return to the same position relative to the stars</note> are no more than 3 minutes and 27 seconds longer than the actual physical sidereal year, and Brahmagupta’s calculation is the closest of all. In reality, this value is about one and a quarter minutes less than the value of the <term>anomalistic year</term> <note>manda-kendra varsha; the time between the Earth's successive closest approaches to the Sun</note>, meaning it almost perfectly matches it. This is the reason why the motion of the solar <term>apogee</term> <note>mandochcha; the point where the sun is farthest from the Earth</note> is considered almost negligible in our texts. In comparison, there is a greater degree of error in the sidereal year values used by <term>Meton</term> <note>a 5th-century BCE Greek astronomer</note> and in <term>Babylonia</term>. The values for the <term>tropical year</term> <note>sayan varsha; the year measured by the cycle of seasons</note> given by Ptolemy and <term>Kepler</term> are 6.5 or 7 minutes greater than the actual tropical year. Therefore—

---

1. <note>original: "आश्लेषार्द्धादासीद्यदा निवृत्तिः किलोष्णकिरणस्य । युक्तमयनं तदासीत् साम्प्रतमयनं पुनर्वसुतः ॥२१॥" translation: "When the return of the sun once occurred from the middle of Ashlesha, that solstice was then correct; currently, the solstice occurs from Punarvasu." — Brihat Samhita, Verse 21</note>
2. *Varahi Samhita*, chapter on the Sun’s motion, pages 16, 17; *Vigyanbhashya*, page 336.

<summary>The author argues for the independence of Indian astronomy by pointing out that Varahamihira and Brahmagupta were unaware of the Greek theories on the precession of equinoxes, despite making accurate independent observations of shifting solstices. He compares the precision of Indian calculations for the sidereal year favorably against those of Meton, Ptolemy, and Kepler.</summary>
<keywords>Varahamihira, Brahmagupta, precession of the equinoxes, sidereal year, tropical year, solstices, Punarvasu, Ashlesha, Vedanga Jyotisha, Ptolemy</keywords>

# Measurements of the Solar Year — According to Various Opinions

| Source | Days | Hours | Minutes | Seconds | Notes |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---|
| Modern <term>Surya Siddhanta</term> <note>a foundational Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy</note> | 365 | 6 | 12 | 36.56 | 3 min 27 sec more than the actual value |
| <term>Aryabhatiya</term> <note>written by the 5th-century mathematician Aryabhata</note> | 365 | 6 | 12 | 29.64 | 3 min 20 sec more than the actual value |
| <term>Brahmasphuta Siddhanta</term> <note>the 7th-century work by Brahmagupta</note> | 365 | 6 | 12 | 9 | 3 minutes more than the actual value |
| Mean <term>Sidereal Year</term> <note>the time it takes for the Sun to return to the same position relative to the fixed stars</note> according to Lockyer | 365 | 6 | 9 | 6.3 | Actual Sidereal Year |
| <term>Anomalistic Year</term> <note>original: "Manda-kendra-varsha"; the time between the Earth's closest approaches to the Sun</note> | 365 | 6 | 13 | 48.3 | |
| <term>Tropical Year</term> <note>original: "Sayana-varsha"; the year measured by the return to an equinox, which dictates the seasons</note> | 365 | 5 | 48 | 46.054 | 6 min 26 sec [more] than the actual Tropical Year |
| Tropical Year according to <term>Ptolemy</term>* | 365 | 5 | 55 | 12 | More than the actual Tropical Year |
| Tropical Year according to <term>Copernicus</term> X | 365 | 5 | 55 | 58 | 7 min 6 sec [more] |
| Sidereal Year according to <term>Meton</term> <note>a 5th-century BCE Greek astronomer</note> § | 365 | 6 | 18 | 57 | 6 min 48 sec more than the actual Sidereal Year |
| Sidereal Year, <term>Babylonian</term> § | 365 | 6 | 13 | 36.3 | 4 min 30 sec more than the actual Sidereal Year |

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific numerical comparisons of solar year lengths found on page 12 to a broader argument regarding the independence and chronology of Indian astronomy, specifically focusing on the Surya Siddhanta and its revisions over the centuries.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

It is proven that the astronomical observations of our scholars are more accurate than the observations of Ptolemy or Kepler, and are thus independent from them.

Similarly, if one compares the <term>apogees</term> <note>original: "mandochchas"; the points in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note>, the <term>nodes</term> <note>original: "patas"; the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>, and other astronomical constants, it becomes evident that the constants accepted by our ancient masters are, for the most part, closer to reality. Therefore, it is established that our system of <term>Jyotish</term> <note>traditional Indian astronomy and astrology</note> is not a shadow of Ptolemy or any other foreign astronomy, but is instead independent. These points are illustrated throughout the *Vijnana-Bhashya* <note>a scientific commentary on the text</note>.

->**The Composition Period of the Surya Siddhanta**<-

Regarding this matter, there is great disagreement among scholars. The composition date stated in this book is not logically sound. For if it had continued in this same form from such an ancient time—two million, one hundred and sixty-five thousand years ago—then why would the question have arisen five thousand years ago during the <term>Mahabharata</term> era <note>the period of the great Indian epic war</note>? In the court of the Emperor Duryodhana, where the greatest scholars of the time were present, why would the question have been raised as to when the Pandavas’ fourteen-year exile would be completed? Based on the answer given by Bhishma <note>the grand patriarch of the Kuru dynasty</note> regarding this matter, it does not appear that they were familiar with astronomical calculations from a text like the *Surya Siddhanta* at that time.

However, there is no doubt that the original form of this text predates Varahamihira <note>a 6th-century polymath</note> by a long time, and it is possible that the version seen in the *Panchasiddhantika* <note>Varahamihira's "Five Treatises" on astronomy</note> is not the original version, but rather a revision made by Varahamihira. Yet it is surprising that Aryabhata (476 CE) has not mentioned it anywhere in his own work; from this, one might infer that it was composed around the same time as the *Aryabhatiya* <note>Aryabhata's primary astronomical work</note>.

The form of the *Surya Siddhanta* presented by Varahamihira differs from the current *Surya Siddhanta*, in which details about the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> <note>original: "ayana-chalana"; the slow change in the orientation of Earth's rotational axis</note> were added later. If the precession of the equinoxes had been discussed in the *Surya Siddhanta* before Varahamihira, he would surely have included it somewhere in the *Panchasiddhantika*, and would not have been satisfied with merely writing that in ancient times the <term>solstitial point</term> was in the *Ashlesha* lunar mansion and is now in *Punarvasu*. Even in the version of the *Surya Siddhanta* existing during the time of Brahmagupta (628–665 CE), there was no mention of the precession of the equinoxes, because if there had been, Brahmagupta would certainly have discussed it. 

This matter must have been added shortly before the time of Bhaskaracharya <note>the 12th-century mathematician, also known as Bhaskara II</note>, but even then, not in the form we see today, as Bhaskaracharya himself expressed doubts about its accuracy. Therefore, it is proven that the original form of the current *Surya Siddhanta* is indeed the one given in the *Panchasiddhantika*, which existed even before Varahamihira, or that the version of the *Surya Siddhanta* by Varahamihira is its revised form, and later, astronomers made corrections to it from time to time... <margin>corrections were made to keep calculations aligned with observed reality</margin>

<summary>The author argues for the independence of Indian astronomy from Western influences and discusses the historical evolution and chronology of the Surya Siddhanta, suggesting it was revised multiple times between the 6th and 12th centuries.</summary>
<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, Varahamihira, Aryabhata, Brahmagupta, Bhaskaracharya, precession of the equinoxes, Indian astronomy, chronology, Mahabharata, Panchasiddhantika</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 14 regarding the dating of the Surya Siddhanta. While the previous page provided a data table of planetary errors, this page analyzes the conclusions drawn by Western scholars like John Bentley and offers a critique based on the work of Ebenezer Burgess.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

...and brought it to its present form, for which help must have been taken from the works or observations of Aryabhata, Brahmagupta, etc., as Mr. Sengupta <note>Prabodh Chandra Sengupta, a 20th-century historian of Indian astronomy</note> proves in his introduction to the *Khandakhadyaka* <note>A significant astronomical treatise by Brahmagupta, 7th century CE</note>.

### **Opinions of Western Scholars** — 

Regarding its period of composition, Western scholars have engaged in a great deal of deliberation, for which it is our supreme duty to thank them. Mr. Bentley <note>John Bentley (1750–1824), an English scholar who applied mathematical analysis to date Indian texts</note> has put in a lot of effort on this subject. He calculated the positions of the Sun, Moon, and other planets according to the *Surya Siddhanta* and modern observations for various eras. He then fixed the composition period of the *Surya Siddhanta* as the specific time when the planetary longitudes <note>original: "bhogansh"; the angular position of a planet along the ecliptic</note> from both calculations coincided. This will be clear from the table on page 14, in which the errors of the planets were shown.

From this, Bentley concludes that the *Surya Siddhanta* was written in the last quarter of the 11th century. On the surface, this seems very logical, and there is no doubt that the author has shown great innovation in this approach. However, in reality, this method is not worthy of acceptance without thorough examination. I find it sufficient to state this in the words of the Publication Committee for Burgess’s translation <note>referring to the 1860 translation of the Surya Siddhanta by Rev. Ebenezer Burgess</note>:—

> “The times Bentley calculated for zero error regarding other planets are very similar to the times we calculated by working backward from the errors of 1860 CE, which are given with the commentary on the 67th verse.”

> “Upon comparing these two tables, it will immediately be seen that Bentley did not derive his decision from the errors in the absolute positions of the planets, but rather in comparison to the position of the Sun. However, he did not even consider the error in the Sun's position. The Hindus' zodiac is <term>Sidereal</term> <note>original: "Nakshatrika"; a system of tracking planets against the fixed stars rather than the seasons</note> and is in no way dependent on the movement of the Sun. Like other planets, the Sun was also not at that position in 3102 BCE <note>The traditional start of the Kali Yuga</note> where it was assumed to be, and therefore the speed of the Sun assumed according to this method differs from reality because the sidereal year was assumed to be 3.5 minutes longer than it actually is. Therefore, why should the error of the Sun not be considered, and why should the sidereal motion of the planets be considered only in comparison to this erroneous motion? It is evident that Bentley should have also fully considered the Sun's position and shown whether the same result follows from it as from the calculation of other planets; and if not, what was the reason for the inconsistency? Such...”

<summary>This page critiques John Bentley's 11th-century dating of the Surya Siddhanta, arguing that his calculations were flawed because they relied on the Sun's relative position rather than absolute sidereal positions.</summary>
<keywords>John Bentley, Surya Siddhanta, Khandakhadyaka, Ebenezer Burgess, Sidereal zodiac, planetary errors, Kali Yuga, Nakshatrika</keywords>

->*An "absolute" position is that which is measured from the fixed starting point of the zodiac <note>The first point of Aries in the sidereal system</note>, and a "relative" position is that which is measured in relation to the Sun, the position of which is determined according to the solar sidereal year. This year is approximately 3 minutes longer than the actual <note>tropical</note> year; therefore, the position of the Sun is also changing accordingly.<-

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the table from page 17, which examined the chronological errors in the planetary positions given by the Surya Siddhanta. The author is now interpreting these findings to suggest that while the original text is ancient (circa 250 CE based on the Sun's position), many of its planetary constants were updated by later astronomers around the 10th or 11th century CE using Beeja (correction) factors.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

...by not doing so, in our understanding, a very significant portion of the entire calculation has been omitted, which leads to a very important conclusion.”......

> “But what is the actual truth regarding our theory? We find that it provides us with the <term>Dhruvankas</term> <note>Astronomical constants or mean longitudes at a specific epoch</note> of the planets. When the errors in their positions are examined using the method described above, it becomes clear that these constants were not determined with the intention of knowing their exact <term>sidereal</term> <note>original: "naxtrika"; positions measured relative to the fixed stars</note> positions at any given time. On the contrary, they provide evidence that in the 10th or 11th century CE, an attempt was made to accurately determine the position of the planets specifically in relation to the position of the Sun. The exact timing of this is also doubtful because there is great variation in the times when the error is considered zero. In the history of Sanskrit literature, it is as certain as anything can be that the <term>Surya-Siddhanta</term> <note>The "Sun Treatise," one of the most important ancient Indian texts on astronomy</note> existed long before that time. References and excerpts from other astronomical texts also reveal that there were several variations of the text by this name. We have already seen above (in verse 6)—which is not a very vague piece of information—that the current text does not contain exactly the same constants that were originally attributed to the first *Surya-Siddhanta*. Therefore, what could be closer to the truth than the inference that the <term>Beeja</term> <note>original: "Beej"; literally "seed," referring to the correction factors applied to traditional astronomical formulas to align them with observed reality</note> calculations made for corrections in the 10th or 11th century were incorporated into the original work by replacing only four or five verses? Thus, while the relative errors of the other planets point to the time when this *Beeja* refinement was made, the absolute error of the Sun reveals the nearly true age of the original book.”

> “In our table, the time of the Sun's zero error is 250 CE. There is no need to place too much emphasis on the precision of this date, as it depends on the accuracy of the observation with which the Sun's position was first determined and then referenced from that point which is called the starting point of the celestial cycle. It was impossible to observe with the naked eye exactly when the center of the Sun, according to its mean motion, was ten <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "kala"; a minute of arc, or 1/60th of a degree</note> east of the junction star of <term>Revati</term> <note>The 27th and final lunar mansion (Nakshatra) in the Indian zodiac</note>, <term>Zeta Piscium</term>. It is clear that the points of the zodiac determined by the Hindus from this point contain significant errors. If even a one-degree error is made in determining the Sun's position, it can cause a difference of 425 years in the calculated time of zero error.”

<vocab>Surya-Siddhanta, Dhruvanka (Constants), Nakshatrika (Sidereal), Beeja Samskara (Refinement/Correction), Revati (Lunar Mansion), Zeta Piscium (Star), Rashichakra (Zodiac), Vedha (Observation), Kala (Minute of arc)</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that the Surya Siddhanta's current form is a composite, featuring an ancient core (dating to roughly 250 CE) with planetary constants updated via "Beeja" corrections around the 10th-11th century CE. It also cautions that dating the text based on astronomical positions is difficult due to the high margin of error inherent in ancient naked-eye observations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the chronological investigation into the Surya-Siddhanta. While the previous page discussed potential updates to the text made in the 10th or 11th century CE, this section focuses on establishing the original composition's timeframe (the "upper limit") by comparing its astronomical coordinates with those provided by the later astronomer Brahmagupta.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

->( 16 )<-

A comparison of the <term>Dhruvankas</term> <note>Polar longitudes; the position of a star measured along the ecliptic</note> of the <term>Yoga Taras</term> <note>The principal "junction stars" used to identify the 27 or 28 lunar mansions of the Indian zodiac</note> as given by <term>Brahmagupta</term> <note>A 7th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> and others also places the date of the *Surya-Siddhanta* around this same period. Some of these coordinates have been passed down through tradition—these are the ones that remain identical across three different major texts—while others have been corrected by the authors themselves. Through a comparative study of these, we can determine the superior and inferior limits <note>The earliest and latest possible dates</note> of the *Surya-Siddhanta's* era. According to Thibaut, <term>Varaha’s</term> <note>Varahamihira, the 6th-century author of the Panchasiddhantika</note> *Panchasiddhantika* also provides the coordinates for seven junction stars, which we consider to be the coordinates of that ancient *Surya-Siddhanta* which existed before Varaha (see pages 20-21).*

To determine the timing of the ancient *Surya-Siddhanta*, the following <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>Lunar mansions or star clusters</note> have been selected. The amounts by which Brahmagupta's coordinates exceed those of the *Surya-Siddhanta* are listed below:

| Star Name | Degrees | Minutes |
|----------|----------|----------|
| Krittika | 4 | 48 |
| Rohini | 1 | 18 |
| Punarvasu | 5 | 3 |
| Magha | 3 | 0 |
| Purva Phalguni | 3 | 0 |
| Chitra | 3 | 0 |
| **Sum** | **20** | **16** |
| **Mean Value** | **3** | **23** |

The average excess of the coordinates of these six stars is 3 degrees and 23 minutes. If we assume that due to <term>Ayan Chalan</term> <note>Precession of the equinoxes; the slow shift in the orientation of Earth's rotational axis</note> the coordinates increase by one degree every 72 years, then this increase would have occurred over a period of approximately 244 years. We know with certainty that Brahmagupta's time was 628 CE. Therefore, the date of the *Surya-Siddhanta* comes to 244 years prior to this, or 384 CE. This can be broadly understood as 400 CE, which serves as the "Upper Limit" for this period. To find the "Lower Limit," we must examine the coordinates of the stars listed below, whose coordinates exceed Brahmagupta's by a mean value of 1 degree 15 [minutes]...

<meta>This page continues the chronological analysis of the Surya-Siddhanta’s composition. <page-num>22</page-num>

...arc-minutes, which must have occurred 60 years after Brahmagupta. In this way, this later limit <note>original: अपरा सीमा (Apara Sima); the upper bound of the date range</note> becomes 628 + 60 = 718 AD.

| Lunar Mansion <note>Nakshatra</note> | Degrees | Arc-minutes |
| :--- | :---: | :---: |
| Ashlesha | 1 | 0 |
| Vishakha | 1 | 0 |
| Abhijit | 1 | 0 |
| Shravana | 2 | 0 |
| **Total** | 5 | 0 |
| **Mean** | 1 | 15 |

In this manner, it can be concluded that the composition of the *Surya-Siddhanta* took place between 400 AD and 725 AD. Bentley and Burgess also determined the timing of the *Surya-Siddhanta* in a similar way by comparing the polar longitudes <note>original: ध्रुवाङ्क (Dhruvamka); the position of a star measured along the ecliptic</note> of the lunar mansions given in the *Surya-Siddhanta* with coordinates established by modern astronomy. However, that does not seem entirely correct, because the same types of errors in observation <note>original: वेध (Vedha)</note> that the author of the *Surya-Siddhanta* might have made, Brahmagupta and others would have likely made as well. Thus, the earlier limit <note>original: परा सीमा (Para Sima)</note> of the composition period could be extended further back. However, the later limit could reach as far as 1100 AD, as Bentley has demonstrated.

The matter of the precession of the equinoxes <note>original: अयन-चलन (Ayana-chalana); the slow wobbling of Earth's axis that changes the positions of stars over time</note> also supports Bentley’s view. During the time of Bhaskaracharya, the rate of precession mentioned in the *Surya-Siddhanta* was not as high as it is in the current version of the *Surya-Siddhanta*. This suggests that the revision of the rate of precession was carried out after Bhaskaracharya’s time. However, Bhaskaracharya’s era is known to be 1150 AD.

**The Author of the Surya-Siddhanta**—It has already been shown that the celestial coordinates in the *Surya-Siddhanta* do not match those of the Greek astronomers Hipparchus, Ptolemy, or astronomers from other Western countries. Therefore, this book was not written based on their observations. Its lineage <note>original: परम्परा (Parampara)</note> is attributed to Vivasvan, that is, the Sun God (see the Chapter on Mean Motions <note>original: मध्यमाधिकार (Madhyamadhikara)</note>, verses 8-9), just as the lineage of the path of Yoga is described in the *Bhagavad Gita* (see *Bhagavad Gita*, Chapter 4, Verses 1-2). Mayasura <note>The figure to whom the Sun God reveals the science in the text</note> is not a foreigner. Being a resident of India, he acquired this knowledge, and from him, the sages also learned it later. This appears to be the secret behind the legend provided in the *Surya-Siddhanta*. The story regarding the Sun God being born in the city of Romaka <note>Often associated with the West or Rome/Alexandria in ancient Indian texts</note> due to a curse from Brahma is fabricated and is found in only one or two manuscripts.

<summary>This page estimates the composition of the Surya-Siddhanta to be between 400 and 1100 AD based on star coordinates and precession. It also argues that the text's author, Mayasura, was of Indian origin rather than a foreigner, dismissing legends of Western origins as later fabrications.</summary>
<keywords>Surya-Siddhanta, Brahmagupta, Bentley, Burgess, Bhaskaracharya, precession of the equinoxes, Mayasura, Vivasvan, celestial coordinates, Indian astronomy history</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the dating and authorship of the Surya-Siddhanta. It transitions from calculating dates based on star positions to evaluating historical claims made by various commentators and astronomers such as Brahmagupta, Aryabhata, and Al-Biruni.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

...is, therefore it is an interpolation <note>original: प्रक्षिप्त (prakshipta); a later addition to a text that wasn't in the original</note> which no one has accepted. If the *Surya-Siddhanta* had been received through a foreigner, Brahmagupta would certainly have written about it, because he clearly stated so for the *Romaka-Siddhanta*, which is undoubtedly foreign. The origin of the *Suryaruna-Samvada* <note>original: सूर्यारुण-संवाद; a dialogue between the Sun God and his charioteer Aruna</note> provided by Mahamahopadhyaya Pandit Sudhakar Dwivedi in his commentary *Panchasiddhantika-Prakashika* is unknown; therefore, it cannot be said who wrote it or on what basis it was written. Nityananda’s^1 writing that this was composed after 3600 years of the Kaliyuga <note>The fourth and current age in the Hindu cycle of world ages</note> had passed—that is, in Saka 421 or 499 AD, the same time that Aryabhata wrote his *Aryabhatiya*—is a delusion. Perhaps because of this delusion, Munishvara also accepted Aryabhata as the author of the *Surya-Siddhanta*. Al-Biruni’s claim that Latacharya or Latasimha created it is also an error^2, because neither Varahamihira nor Brahmagupta accepted Latacharya as the author of the *Surya-Siddhanta*. According to Varahamihira, Latacharya is a commentator on the *Romaka* and *Paulisha Siddhantas*. Lata's *Ahargana*^3 <term>Ahargana|A mathematical calculation of the total number of elapsed days from a fixed starting epoch to a specific date</term> belong to the time of sunset at Yavanapura <note>literal: 'City of the Greeks/Ionians'; often referring to Alexandria or another western city influenced by Greek astronomy</note>. 

Thus, the opinion is clear that all the conjectures regarding the period of composition and the author of the *Surya-Siddhanta* remain unproven. Based on internal evidence, it can only be said that its development <note>original: वृद्धि (vriddhi); growth or expansion</note> began around 400 AD and concluded by 1200 AD. Initially, its expansion or revision might have been done by Varahamihira. Then, other revisers tried to bring it up-to-date <note>The author uses the English phrase 'Up-to-date' transcribed into Hindi script</note> by taking advantage of the observations <note>original: वेध (Vedha); astronomical sightings or measurements</note> of Aryabhata, Brahmagupta, Manjula, and others. This revision process continued until Ranganatha fixed its verses by writing a commentary on it. In Madhava Purohita’s commentary, a few additional verses are found; it is unknown whether they were written based on some ancient text or were simply added by him.

->Prayag<-
->Bhratri-Dvitiya; Samvat 1967 Vikrami<- <note>Nov 4, 1910 AD; Bhratri-Dvitiya is the festival of Bhai Dooj</note> **Mahavir Prasad Srivastava**

^1 The time of composition of the *Surya-Siddhanta* is stated by Nityananda, the author of *Siddhanta-Raja*, as having occurred when thirty-six hundred years of the Kaliyuga had passed—*Panchasiddhantika Prakashika*, page 2.
^2 "Said by Latacharya, when the sun was half-set at Yavanapura"—*Panchasiddhantika*, Chapter 1, Verse 3.

<summary>The author concludes that the Surya-Siddhanta was not the work of a single person or a foreign source, but a developing text revised by multiple Indian astronomers between 400 and 1200 AD. He refutes claims by Al-Biruni and Nityananda regarding its authorship and dating.</summary>

# Chapter One

## The Mean Motions of the Planets
<note>original: मध्यमाधिकार (Madhyamadhikara); this section details the fundamental mathematical rules for calculating the average (mean) positions of celestial bodies.</note>

### (Brief Description)

[ Verse 1—Invocation of God. Verses 2-7—The story of Mayasura <note>A legendary figure and architect of the asuras (demons/titans) in Hindu mythology</note> performing penance to the Sun God to acquire the science of astronomy (*Jyotish-shastra*); the Sun God being pleased, granting a boon, and manifesting a man from his own body to teach him. Verses 8-9—The man (a personified portion of the Sun) telling Mayasura that the science which the Sun God previously revealed to the Great Sages is now being retaught with slight modifications for the current era. Verse 10—Two divisions of Time: (1) Eternal time without beginning or end, and (2) Measurable or calculable time. Verses 11-20—Units of time ranging from a *nimesha* <note>the blink of an eye</note> to a *kalpa* <term>Kalpa|A cosmic cycle or 'Day of Brahma,' totaling 4.32 billion years</term>. Verses 21-23—The current age of Brahma <note>the creator deity in the Hindu triad</note>. Verse 24—The duration of time spent in the process of creation since the start of the current *kalpa*. Verses 25-27—The underlying causes of the movement of stars and planets. Verse 28—The units used for measuring angles. Verses 29-34—The number of revolutions performed by the planets, their "fast peaks" <note>original: शीघ्रोच्च (shighrochcha); a term related to the epicycle theory, similar to the center of the epicycle in Ptolemaic astronomy</note>, the moon's apogee and nodes, and the stars within one *Mahayuga* <term>Mahayuga|A Great Age consisting of 4,320,000 years</term>. Verses 35-36—The relationship between lunar and solar months. Verses 37-39—The total number of civil days (*savana* days), intercalary months, and lunar days (*tithis*) in a *Mahayuga*. Verse 40—The number of civil days and lunar days in a *kalpa*. Verses 41-44—The number of revolutions of the planetary "slow peaks" <note>original: मन्दोच्च (mandhochcha); the planetary apogee where the planet's motion appears slowest</note> and their nodes during a *kalpa*. Verses 45-47—The time elapsed from the start of the *kalpa* to the end of the *Satyayuga* <note>The first and most perfect of the four world ages</note>. Verses 48-50—The method for calculating the total number of days passed since the beginning of creation. Verses 51-52—The method for determining the "Lord of the Day," "Lord of the Year," and "Lord of the Month." Verses 53-54—The method for calculating the mean positions of the planets. Verse 55—The method for determining the year of Jupiter (*Samvatsara*). Verses 56-58—The specific positions of the planets at the end of the *Satyayuga*. Verse 59—The relationship between diameter and circumference, and the measurement of the Earth's circumference. Verses 60-61—Determining the latitude circle of a specific location and using it to calculate a planet’s mean position. Verse 62—Listing the famous cities located on the central meridian <note>The prime meridian of ancient Indian astronomy, passing through Ujjain</note> of India. Verses 63-65—Using lunar eclipses to determine how far east or west a location is from the central meridian. Verse 66—When the solar trend or motion begins. Verse 67—Determining the position of planets at any desired time. Verses 68-70—The maximum distance the Moon and other planets can travel north or south of the ecliptic <note>original: कान्ति-वृत्ति (kanti-vritti); the apparent path of the Sun</note>. ]

<summary>This page provides a detailed table of contents for the first chapter of the Surya-Siddhanta, covering the mythological origins of the text and the fundamental units and cycles used in ancient Indian astronomical calculations.</summary>

<page-num>2</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta

->To the One whose form is unthinkable and unmanifest, who is without attributes yet the soul of all attributes;<-
->to the Supreme Spirit, the foundation and embodiment of the entire universe, be salutation. || 1 ||<-

**Translation —** Salutations to that Supreme Spirit (Parabrahma) whose form can neither be conceived in meditation nor manifested, who is devoid of qualities (nirguna) yet from whom all qualities arise, and who is the base of the entire world. || 1 ||

->When but a small portion of the Golden Age remained, a great titan named Maya,<-
->desiring to know the supreme, holy, and most excellent knowledge—<-
->the foremost of the auxiliary sciences of the Veda, astronomy, the cause of all celestial motions—<-
->worshipped the Sun God and performed most difficult penance. || 2-3 ||<-

**Translation —** Toward the end of the *Krita Yuga* <note>The first of the four world ages, also known as Satyayuga or the Golden Age</note>, a great *Asura* <note>Translated here as 'titan' or 'being of great power'; in this context, Maya is a legendary figure and architect</note> named Maya, wishing to know the supreme, sacred, and best knowledge—astronomy—which is the foremost among the limbs of the Veda and the cause of the movements of all celestial bodies, worshipped the Sun God through severe penance. || 2-3 ||

**Scientific Commentary —** The explanation of the four ages—*Satyayuga, Treta, Dvapara,* and *Kaliyuga*—is provided in the 16th verse of this same chapter.

There are six <term>Vedangas|Literally 'limbs of the Veda'; these are auxiliary disciplines required for the proper understanding and application of the Vedas</term>: Phonetics (*Shiksha*), Meter (*Chhandas*), Grammar (*Vyakarana*), Etymology (*Nirukta*), Rituals (*Kalpa*), and Astronomy (*Jyotisha*). These help in understanding and explaining the Vedas, hence they are called *Vedangas*. The excellence of Astronomy among these limbs was illustrated by the great mathematician Bhaskaracharya as follows: "The science of words (Grammar) is the mouth of the Veda, Astronomy is the eye, Etymology is the ear, Rituals are the hands, Phonetics is the nose, and Meter is the feet. Therefore, just as the eye is the most important organ among all parts of the body, so is the science of Astronomy the most excellent among all the limbs of the Veda."

->Gratified by that penance and pleased with the seeker of the boon,<-
->the Sun God himself taught Maya the movements of the planets. || 4 ||<-

**Translation —** Satisfied and pleased with his penance, the Sun God himself instructed Maya, who sought a boon, on the movements of the planets—that is, the science of astronomy. || 4 ||

**Scientific Commentary —** Western astronomers define "planets" as those celestial bodies that revolve around the Sun. According to this definition, there are eight planets: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. Of these, the latter two were discovered... <note>The text continues on the next page regarding the discovery of the outer planets.</note>

<summary>This page contains the opening invocation of the Surya Siddhanta and recounts the legendary origin of the text, where the titan Maya receives divine astronomical knowledge from the Sun God. It also establishes the status of astronomy (Jyotisha) as the most vital "limb" of the Vedas.</summary>

<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, Maya, Sun God, Vedanga, Jyotisha, Krita Yuga, Bhaskaracharya, Astronomy origins</keywords>

On the previous page, the titan Maya began a severe penance to please the Sun God in order to receive astronomical knowledge. This page details the Sun God’s response and the introduction of a surrogate teacher.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
## Chapter on the Mean Motions of the Planets

<note>This chapter, titled 'Madhyamadhikara,' focuses on calculating the average positions of celestial bodies.</note>

...discovered within the last two hundred years and these are not visible to the naked eye <note>Referring to Uranus and Neptune, which were unknown to ancient Indian astronomers</note>. The Moon revolves around the Earth, therefore it is a satellite. Satellites of other planets have also been seen through the telescope. However, our astronomical texts do not consider the Earth as a planet, but rather the Sun. The Moon is also placed in the category of planets. Uranus and Neptune are not mentioned anywhere. Therefore, in our tradition, the Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Guru (Jupiter), Venus, and Saturn are considered the seven "gross" or visible planets, while Rahu and Ketu <note>The north and south nodes of the moon</note> are considered two "subtle" planets. A full description of these two subtle planets will be provided in this very chapter while describing the nodes of the Moon. Astronomy/Astrology describes the events that occur in the sky due to the movements of these planets; therefore, in this verse, another name for Astronomy is given as "The Conduct of the Planets" <note>original Sanskrit: 'Grahanam Charitam'</note>.

> "I have known your intention; I have been worshipped through your penance. 
> I shall give you the knowledge based on time—the great conduct of the celestial bodies." || 5 || 
> "No one can withstand my radiance; nor do I have a moment to spare. 
> This person, who is a portion of myself, will explain everything to you in full." || 6 ||

**Translation—** The Sun God said: "I have become aware of your intention and I am very satisfied with your penance. I grant you the instruction on the great conduct of the planets, through which the exact knowledge of time can be obtained. However, no one can endure my brilliance, and I do not have the time to provide the instruction myself. Therefore, this person, who is an incarnation of a part of me, will provide the full instruction to you." || 5-6 ||

> Having said this, the God disappeared after commanding his own partial incarnation. 
> That person spoke this to Maya, who stood prostrate with palms joined in reverence. || 7 ||
> "Listen with a focused mind to that supreme knowledge which was spoken long ago, 
> age after age, to the great sages by the Sun God himself." || 8 ||

---

* Before this verse, in a manuscript of the *Surya Siddhanta* from the Anandashram in Pune (one lacking a commentary), this verse is also found:

-> "Therefore, go to your own city; there I shall give you knowledge. <-
-> In the city of Romaka, due to a curse from a Brahmin, assuming the form of a barbarian." <-

However, this is not found in any other copy of the *Surya Siddhanta*. No connection can be seen between this verse and those that come before or after it; therefore, it is an <term>interpolation|Kshepaka: a verse inserted into a text by a later hand</term>. <note>The mention of 'Romaka'—likely referring to the Roman or Greek world—suggests a historical awareness of Western astronomical traditions, though this specific verse is considered an unofficial addition.</note>

<vocab>Surya Siddhanta (The Sun Treatise), Jyotishastra (Astronomy/Astrology), Graha (Planet/Celestial body), Madhyamadhikara (Chapter on Mean Motions), Vivasvata (The Sun), Romaka Nagare (In the city of Rome/the West), Kshepaka (Interpolation)</vocab>

<summary>The Sun God acknowledges Maya's penance but, unable to teach directly due to his overwhelming brilliance, appoints a surrogate (his "portion") to impart the sacred astronomical knowledge. It also notes an interpolated verse mentioning the city of Romaka.</summary>
<keywords>Sun God, Maya the Asura, celestial conduct, Rahu and Ketu, Romaka, interpolation, divine revelation</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the introduction to the Surya Siddhanta. On the previous page, the Sun God responded to the titan Maya's penance by explaining that he could not teach Maya directly due to his intense radiance and lack of time, and thus appointed a surrogate being—a portion of himself—to deliver the astronomical wisdom.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta

->This is that same original science which the Sun spoke of old;<-
->only through the revolution of the ages has there occurred a difference in time. || 6 ||<-

**Translation—** After saying this, the Sun God disappeared. The being who was a portion of the Sun, following the God's command, spoke to Maya, who was bowing in deep humility with hands joined in prayer: "Listen with a focused mind to this supreme knowledge, which the Lord Sun himself spoke to the great sages at various times. This is the very same primal science that the Sun first taught; however, due to the passing of the great ages <note>The 'Yugas' or massive cycles of time in Indian cosmology</note>, a slight difference in the measurement of time has arisen." || 7-8 ||

**Scientific Commentary—** Regarding the second part of the ninth verse <note>The commentator appears to be using a different verse numbering system than the text above, which labels these as 6-8</note>, some interpret it to mean that this science is exactly what the Sun taught the sages without any change, and only the time of delivery is different. However, if that were the case, why bother saying there is a "difference in time"? The first part of the verse would have been enough. Therefore, a more logical meaning is: if there is any discrepancy between the astronomy taught in ancient times and that being taught now, it has been caused by the passage of time; in essence, there is no difference. 

How can time cause such a difference? This is because astronomy is an experimental science, and in any experiment, some minute errors inevitably remain—what we call an **experimental error**. In the study of the stars, this error accumulates year after year. After hundreds of years, it grows into a significant discrepancy. Therefore, it must be corrected periodically, a process known as <term>Beeja-Samskara</term> <note>The rectification of astronomical parameters to ensure calculations match observed reality</note>. 

It is from this perspective that the Sun’s surrogate speaks. As proof, the final chapter of the *Surya Siddhanta* contains twenty-one verses titled "Beejopanayana" <note>The presentation of the 'Beeja' or correction factors</note>. The commentators Ranganatha and Pandit Indranarayana Dwivedi treated these as "interpolations" <note>later additions not part of the original text</note> and did not comment on them. Their reason was: why would the Sun God need to correct his own science? However, in his own commentary *Gudartha-Prakashika* (The Light of Hidden Meanings), while explaining the 6th verse, Ranganatha admits that differences do occur over time. His exact words are as follows:

> "And so, by the force of time, a certain variation occurs in the movement of the planets. In another age, having established the planetary motions for that specific period, the Compassionate One spoke as if it were a new science for the sake of the daily life and rituals of the people living then; thus, the various versions of these sciences are not useless. The intent is: 'I, accepting the planetary motions established in the science spoken by the Sun for the current age, and setting aside the planetary motions established in the science of the first Sun as they are no longer practical, am teaching you that very same essential science.' Thus, even within a single age, when a difference is seen in the planetary motions at different times..."

<summary>This page explains that while the astronomical science is eternal, the movement of time requires periodic corrections (Beeja-Samskara) to maintain accuracy. The Sun's surrogate begins teaching the titan Maya, acknowledging that calculations must be updated for the current era.</summary>

<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, Maya, Beeja-Samskara, astronomical correction, Ranganatha, experimental error, Gudartha-Prakashika, Yuga cycles</keywords>

# Chapter on the Mean Motions of the Planets
<note>original: Madhyamādhikāra</note>

"...experts of the present time carry out the calculations after verifying the results. This difference <note>the discrepancy between observed and calculated positions</note> is called the 'Seed' <note>original: *bīja*</note> in ancient texts. Because the older texts have been lost, the dialogue between the Sun and the Sages is no longer seen today. However, its lack of popularity should not be doubted, given the authority of traditional scripture." †

There are many examples of differences occurring due to the passage of time, which will be pointed out in their proper places within this commentary.

->Time is the destroyer of worlds; another type of Time is for calculation.<-
->It is twofold: Gross and Subtle, known as Formed and Formless. || 10 ||<-

**Translation—** One type of Time destroys the world, and the second type is "calculable" <term>kalatnaka: that which can be reckoned or known</term>. This is also of two kinds: (1) Gross and (2) Subtle. Gross time can be measured, hence it is called Formed <term>mūrta</term>, while subtle time cannot be measured and is thus called Formless <term>amūrta</term>. || 10 ||

**Scientific Commentary—** The first type of Time <note>Time as the eternal destroyer</note> cannot even be imagined, because we do not know when it began nor when it will end. It is indivisible and all-encompassing; yet, within its presence, the worlds come to an end. The creator Brahma is born, creates the universe, and dissolves it, but Time remains. Therefore, it is called the "Ender" or "Destroyer" of worlds. This is why Death is also referred to as Time <note>the Sanskrit word *Kāla* carries both meanings</note>.

Of the small middle portion of Time that can be known, that which is extremely minute cannot be measured and is called "Formless." As the precision of our measurement increases, the definition of Formless Time will continue to be updated, as will be shown in the explanation of the next verse.

->Time beginning with a "breath" is called Formed; that beginning with a "speck" is Formless.<-
->Six breaths make a *vināḍī*; sixty of those are known as a *nāḍī*. || 11 ||<-
->Sixty *nāḍīs* make a sidereal day and night.<-
->Thirty of those make a month; a "civil" month is measured by sunrises. || 12 ||<-
->A lunar month is by lunar days; likewise, a solar month is by the Sun's transit.<-
->Twelve months make a year, which is also called a single day of the gods. || 13 ||<-

**Translation—** From the "breath" <term>prāṇa: the time taken for one inhalation/exhalation</term> upwards, all units of time are considered Formed...

<page-num>6</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta
<note>The Sun’s Treatise</note>

...are called as such, and units from a *truti* <term>the smallest infinitesimal unit of time</term> up to those below a *prana* <term>a breath</term> are classified as Formless. Six *pranas* make one *vinadi* <note>also called a *pala*</note>, and sixty *vinadis* make one *nadi* <note>also called a *ghadi* or Indian hour</note>. || 11 || Sixty *nadis* make one Sidereal Day and Night <note>one full rotation of the Earth relative to the fixed stars</note>, and thirty Sidereal Days make one Sidereal Month. In the same manner, thirty civil days <term>savana: measured from sunrise to sunrise</term> make one Civil Month. || 12 || Likewise, thirty lunar dates <term>tithis</term> make one Lunar Month, and the time it takes for the Sun to move from one zodiac sign to the next <term>sankranti</term> is called a Solar Month. Twelve months make one year; this is known as a Divine Day, or the "Day of the Gods."

**Scientific Commentary—** When a healthy person is sitting comfortably, the time it takes to naturally inhale and exhale (the breath of life) is called a *prana*. This was the smallest unit of time that could be practically measured in the ancient era. Because there were no instruments available then to measure intervals shorter than this, those smaller units were called Formless. Today, clocks are manufactured that easily measure those units once considered Formless. 

In one sidereal day, there are 60 *ghadis* = 60 × 60 *palas* = 60 × 60 × 60 *pranas*, which equals 21,600 *pranas*. By comparison, in one modern day there are 24 hours = 24 × 60 minutes = 24 × 60 × 60 seconds, which equals 86,400 seconds. Therefore, one *prana* is equal to 4 seconds. It is well known to everyone how easy it is to measure a second with a watch equipped with a second hand. There are even clocks that can easily measure a fifth or a tenth of a second. A tenth of a second is equivalent to one-fortieth of a *prana*. Consequently, in the modern age, even some units below a *prana* can be considered Formed <note>measurable</note>.

A *prana* is also called an *asu*. The famous astronomer Bhaskaracharya, in his work *Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas* <note>original: *Siddhānta-Śiromaṇi*</note>, provides a second definition of *prana* using the language of poetic meter <term>Chanda-shastra</term>: the time required to pronounce ten long syllables is called one *prana*. A "long" syllable <term>guru akshara</term> is defined as a syllable containing a nasal sound <term>anusvara</term>, ending in a breathy aspiration <term>visarga</term>, a long vowel, or a short vowel followed by a conjunct consonant.

A *pala* is also the name for a unit of weight, which is equal to four *tolas* <note>approximately 48 grams</note>.

---

*This word <note>referring to "year" in the text</note> indicates that the twelve months comprising a year are solar months. A year is not typically calculated using lunar, sidereal, or civil months in this context.

It establishes that a year in this system is specifically defined by the Sun's transit through the zodiac.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the systematic breakdown of time units from the previous page, transitioning from the conceptual "Formless" units (like the breath or prana) into more specific subdivisions and the definition of different types of "days" (sidereal versus civil).</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
# Chapter on Mean Motions
<note>original: "Madhyamadhikara"</note>

The time it takes for 1 *pala* (a weight equal to approximately 4 *tolas*) of water to flow into a *ghatika* instrument <note>a clepsydra or water clock</note> through a hole of a specific measurement is called a *pala*.

Bhaskaracharya II <note>a renowned 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> conceptualized the unit of *truti* <term>an infinitesimal unit of time</term> in this manner: the time it takes for an eyelid to blink is called a *nimesha*. One-thirtieth of a *nimesha* is called a *tatpara*, and one-hundredth of a *tatpara* is called a *truti*. The relationship between units larger than a *nimesha* is as follows:

-> 18 *nimeshas* = 1 *kastha* <-
-> 30 *kasthas* = 1 *kala* <-
-> 30 *kalas* = 1 *ghatika* <note>approximately 24 minutes</note> <-
-> 2 *ghatikas* = 1 *muhurta* <note>approximately 48 minutes</note> <-
-> 30 *muhurtas* = 1 sidereal day <-

In this way, 1 sidereal day = 30 × 2 × 30 × 30 × 18 *nimeshas*
= 972,000 *nimeshas*.

It was previously shown that there are 21,600 *pranas* <note>breaths</note> or 86,400 seconds in one day. Therefore, there are 45 *nimeshas* (972,000 ÷ 21,600) in 1 *prana*, and 11 1/4 *nimeshas* in 1 second.

**Sidereal Day and Night** — *Nakshatra* <term>sidereal</term> refers to a star, a constellation, or the 27th part of the great circle upon which the Sun appears to complete one revolution in a year. Due to the Earth’s daily rotation, all stars in the sky appear to rise in the east, ascend, move toward the west, set in the west, and then rise again in the east. If you record the time a star rises on a clock and then note when that same star rises again, you will find—provided the clock is accurate—that the time between these two rises is approximately 23 hours, 56 minutes, and 4 seconds. This is called a "Sidereal Day and Night" <note>original: "Nakshatra Ahoratra"</note> or simply a sidereal day. This duration is always constant; it does not increase or decrease, provided the very subtle movements of the stars themselves are not taken into account. Therefore, astronomers use this as the basis for calculating time.

**Civil Day** — The time from one sunrise to the next sunrise is called a civil day <note>original: "Savana Din"</note>. This is approximately 4 minutes longer than a sidereal day. The civil day...
