--- Page 31 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion from page 7 regarding the measurement of time. While the previous page focused on sidereal time (based on the stars), this page transitions into the variability of time measurements and the definitions of lunar and solar divisions.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta
<note>original: "Surya Siddhanta" refers to the "Sun Treatise," one of the most important historical texts on Indian astronomy.</note>

Measurement is not always uniform. Therefore, the measurement of the **mean civil day** <note>original: "madhyama savana dina"</note> is the standard of time that is understood through the use of clocks.

**Aindava Tithi or Lunar Date**—The Moon, while orbiting in the sky, reaches a point where it is very close to the Sun; this moment is called **Amavasya** <note>the New Moon</note>. The period from one New Moon to the next is called a **Lunar Month** <note>original: "chandramasa"</note>. Its mean value is 29.530587946 mean civil days. After the New Moon, the Moon moves forward toward the east, away from the Sun. When it has moved 12 degrees ahead, the first lunar date (**Pariva**) passes. When the difference is between 12 and 24 degrees, it is the second date (**Duij**). When the Moon is between 24 and 36 degrees ahead of the Sun, it is the third date (**Teej**). When the difference is between 178 and 180 degrees, it is the **Purnima** <note>the Full Moon</note>. When the Moon is between 180 and 192 degrees ahead, it is the 16th date, also called the first date of the waning half (**Pratipada**). Between 192° and 204° is the second date of that half, and so on. After the Full Moon, the Moon rises approximately 2 *ghatikas* (48 minutes) later each day after sunset. The 14 or 15 days from the Full Moon to the New Moon are called the **Krishnapaksha** <term>the dark fortnight or waning phase</term>. The New Moon is also called the 30th date; for this reason, almanacs <note>original: "panchangas"</note> use the number 30 to denote the New Moon.

**Solar Month** <note>original: "sauramasa"</note>—The path the Sun follows as it circles the sky is called the **Ecliptic** <note>original: "kranti-vritta"</note>. One-twelfth of this circle is called a **Zodiac Sign** <note>original: "rashi"</note>. At the moment the center of the Sun’s disk enters from one sign into the next, a **Sankranti** <term>solar ingress or transition</term> occurs. The time from one transition to the next is called a **Solar Month**. The twelve solar months vary in duration; the reason for this is that the Sun’s velocity is not always constant. When the Sun’s motion is fast, it completes a sign quickly, making that solar month shorter. Conversely, when the Sun’s motion is slow, the solar month is longer.

**Year**—Just as there are different types of months, there are different types of years. Twelve lunar months make one **Lunar Year**, twelve civil months make one **Civil Year** <note>original: "savana-varsha"</note>, and twelve solar months make one **Solar Year**. Our astronomers have followed this tradition. However, the direct meaning of the second verse of the 13th stanza is that a year consists of 12 months, which is called a **Divine Day** <note>original: "divyadina"</note>. Therefore, the twelve months that constitute a year are not any other kind of month, but specifically solar months. This proves that the *Surya Siddhanta* primarily discusses the solar year and considers the solar year to be the standard "year," excluding the others from this specific definition.

<summary>This page defines the lunar and solar divisions of time, explaining how lunar dates (tithis) are calculated by the angular distance between the Sun and Moon, and how solar months vary in length due to the Sun's fluctuating orbital speed.</summary>
<keywords>Lunar Date (Tithi), New Moon (Amavasya), Full Moon (Purnima), Ecliptic (Kranti-vritta), Solar Ingress (Sankranti), Solar Month, Lunar Month, Solar Year, Surya Siddhanta</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page transitions from the calculation of lunar dates (Tithis) and months discussed on the previous page to larger cosmic time scales, specifically the concept of "Divine Days" based on the Earth's poles and the calculation of the "Chaturyuga" (Great Age).</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

# Chapter on Mean Motions
<note>original: "Madhyamadhikara"</note>

**Divine Day** <note>original: "Divyadina"</note>—The North Pole of the Earth is described as the dwelling place of the gods, and the South Pole as the dwelling place of the demons. For this reason, the North Pole is called the Realm of the Gods <note>original: "Deva-loka"</note> and the South Pole is called the Realm of the Demons <note>original: "Asura-loka"</note>. At the moment the Sun reaches the celestial equator <note>original: "Vishuvavritta"</note>, day and night are equal. This event occurs only twice a year. For 6 months, the Sun remains north of the equator, and for 6 months, it remains south. In the first half of the year, the northern hemisphere experiences long days and short nights, while the southern hemisphere experiences short days and long nights. In the second half of the year, the exact opposite occurs. However, while the Sun is north of the equator, it is constantly visible at the North Pole (on Mount Sumeru) for 6 months, and it is not visible at the South Pole during this time. Therefore, this half-year is called the Day of the Gods and the Night of the Demons. When the Sun stays south of the equator for 6 months, the gods at the North Pole cannot see it, while the demons at the South Pole see it continuously for 6 months. Therefore, this half-year is called the Night of the Gods and the Day of the Demons. Thus, our 12 months are equal to one full day and night <note>original: "ahoratra"</note> of the gods or demons.

> The day and night of the gods and demons are mutually reversed. 
> Six multiplied by sixty (360) such days make a divine year, and likewise for the demons. ||14||
<note>original Latin/Sanskrit context: "Surasuranamanyonyamahoratram viparyayat..." refers to the inverse relationship of light at the poles.</note>

**Translation**—That which is the day of the gods is the night of the demons, and that which is the night of the gods is called the day of the demons. Sixty times six (360) of these divine or demonic day-night cycles are called a Divine or Demonic Year.

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vigyan Bhashya"</note>—Just as a civil year is conceptualized as having 360 civil days, similarly, a divine year is considered to consist of 360 divine days. The simple meaning of this is that one year of the gods is equal to 360 of our human years.

> Twelve thousand of those divine years are called a Great Age. 
> In the count of solar years, this is four million, three hundred and twenty thousand (4,320,000). ||15||
> This Great Age is to be understood as including the dawn and twilight periods. 
> The arrangement of the Golden Age and others is based on the state of the "feet of Dharma." ||16||
> The tenth part of the Great Age multiplied by four, three, two, and one, 
> respectively, gives the duration of the Golden Age and the others; 
> the sixth part of each belongs to their respective dawn and twilight periods. ||17||

**Translation**—A **Chaturyuga** <term>Great Age or cycle of four eons</term> consists of these twelve thousand divine years, which in solar years amounts to forty-three lakhs and twenty thousand (4,320,000). This also includes the years of the "Sandhya" (dawn) and "Sandhyansha" (twilight) periods. Within one Chaturyuga, there are the **Satyayuga** <note>the Golden Age</note>, **Treta**, **Dvapara**, and...

<summary>This page explains the astronomical basis for "Divine Days" as the six-month polar day/night cycle and defines the Chaturyuga, a cosmic cycle of 4.32 million solar years.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Day, North Pole, South Pole, Mount Sumeru, Celestial Equator, Chaturyuga, Satyayuga, Solar Year, Yuga cycle, Indian Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues from the definition of a Divine Day (one human year) to the calculation of the Great Age (Chaturyuga) and its constituent parts—the four Yugas—along with their transitional periods known as dawns and twilights.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

There are four ages <note>original: "yugas"</note>; their durations are determined by the "legs" of virtue <note>original: "dharma" — virtue is often depicted as a bull standing on four legs in the first age, losing one leg in each subsequent age</note>. The Golden Age <note>original: "Satyayuga"</note> is four times the length of the tenth part of a Great Age <note>original: "Chaturyuga"</note>; the Silver Age <note>original: "Treta"</note> is three times; the Brazen Age <note>original: "Dwapara"</note> is two times; and the Iron Age <note>original: "Kaliyug"</note> is one time. Both the morning and evening transitions <note>original: "sandhyas"</note> of each age are equal to one-sixth of that age's total duration. || 15-17 ||

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vigyan-Bhashya"</note> — In the 14th verse, it was explained that 360 days of the gods or demons constitute one "Divine Year." In the 13th verse, it was explained that one day of the gods is equal to one solar year on Earth; therefore, it is clear that one year of the gods is equal to 360 solar years. According to the 15th verse, one Great Age <note>original: "Chaturyuga"</note> consists of 12,000 Divine Years, which is equal to 4,320,000 solar years (12,000 multiplied by 360). The Great Age is also called a *Mahayuga*. Because it contains four specific ages—Satya, Treta, Dwapara, and Kali—it is called a *Chaturyuga* (the four-fold age). In the Satya age, virtue stands on four legs; in Treta, three legs; in Dwapara, two legs; and in Kali, only one leg. Similarly, in one Great Age, the Satya age consists of four parts, the Treta three parts, the Dwapara two parts, and the Kali one part. Therefore:

| Age | In Divine Years | In Solar Years |
| :--- | :---: | :---: |
| Satya Yuga (including both transitions) | 4,800 | 1,728,000 |
| Treta Yuga (including both transitions) | 3,600 | 1,296,000 |
| Dwapara Yuga (including both transitions) | 2,400 | 864,000 |
| Kali Yuga (including both transitions) | 1,200 | 432,000 |
| **The Great Age (Mahayuga)** | **12,000** | **4,320,000** |

The two transitions for each age are equal to one-sixth of its total duration; therefore, a single transition (the junction period) is equal to one-twelfth. The transition that occurs at the beginning of an age is called the "Beginning Transition" <note>original: "Adi Sandhya"</note>, and the transition at the end is called the "Twilight Portion" <note>original: "Sandhyansha"</note>. Their values are as follows:

| Transition Period | In Divine Years | In Solar Years |
| :--- | :---: | :---: |
| Beginning or End Transition of Satya | 400 | 144,000 |
| Beginning or End Transition of Treta | 300 | 108,000 |
| Beginning or End Transition of Dwapara | 200 | 72,000 |
| Beginning or End Transition of Kali | 100 | 36,000 |

Just as there are two transitions in a full day and night <note>original: "ahoratra"</note>—morning and evening—so too are there two transitions in every age of the Great Age: one at the beginning and one at the end.

<vocab>Iron Age (Kaliyuga), Golden Age (Satyayuga), Silver Age (Treta), Brazen Age (Dwapara), Great Age (Chaturyuga/Mahayuga), Divine Year, Solar Year, Transition (Sandhya), Twilight (Sandhyansha), Scientific Commentary (Vigyan-Bhashya)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the mathematical calculation of the four cosmic ages (Yugas) that form a Great Age (Mahayuga), totaling 4,320,000 solar years. It explains the proportional decline of time and virtue across these ages and defines the transitional periods (Sandhyas) that bridge them.</summary>
<keywords>Chaturyuga, Mahayuga, Yuga cycles, Sandhya, cosmic time, Surya Siddhanta, Hindu astronomy, Divine Years, Kali Yuga</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the calculation of cosmic time, moving from the definition of a single Great Age (Chaturyuga) to the larger divisions of time known as Manu Periods (Manvantaras) and the Great Cycle (Kalpa).</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
# Chapter on the Mean Motions of the Planets <note>original: "Madhyamadhikara"</note>

->Seventy-one Great Ages are here said to constitute a Manu Period.<-
->At its end, a transition period the length of a Golden Age is described as a deluge. || 18 ||<-
->Within a Great Cycle, these Manu Periods are known to be fourteen, with their transitions.<-
->Including a transition of the same length at the start of the Great Cycle, fifteen transitions are recorded. || 19 ||<-

**Translation** — Seventy-one Great Ages <note>original: "Mahayugas"</note> make one Manu Period <note>original: "Manvantara"</note>, at the end of which occurs a transition period <note>original: "Sandhi"</note> equal to the length of a Golden Age <note>original: "Satyayuga"</note>. A global deluge <note>original: "jalaplaba"</note> occurs during this transition. A Great Cycle <note>original: "Kalpa"</note> consists of fourteen such Manu Periods, each with its own transition; however, there is also a transition equal to a Golden Age at the very beginning of the Great Cycle. Therefore, one Great Cycle contains fourteen Manu Periods and fifteen transitions of the same length as a Golden Age. || 18-19 ||

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vigyan-Bhashya"</note> — In each of the four ages within a Great Age, two transitions (a dawn and a twilight) are recognized; however, for a Manu Period, only one transition is recognized at its conclusion, the value of which is equal to a Golden Age. One Manu Period consists of 71 Great Ages, which equals 71 x 4,320,000 = 306,720,000 solar years. At the end of every Manu Period, there is a transition of 1,728,000 solar years, and there is a similar transition at the beginning of the Great Cycle. Thus:

1 Great Cycle = 14 Manu Periods + 15 transitions equal to Golden Ages
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 14 x 71 Great Ages + 15 Golden Ages
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;15 x 4
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 994 Great Ages + ———— Great Ages <note>Because a Golden Age</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;10&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<note>is 4/10th of a Great Age</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 994 + 6 Great Ages
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 1,000 Great Ages
Or&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 1,000 x 12,000 = 12,000,000 Divine Years
Or&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;= 1,000 x 4,320,000 = 4,320,000,000 Solar Years

These values for the Great Age <note>original: "Mahayuga"</note> or Manu Period <note>original: "Manvantara"</note> match those found in the *Laws of Manu* <note>original: "Manusmriti"</note> and other legal-religious scriptures; however, the astronomer Aryabhata has given slightly different values for the ages in his work, the *Aryabhatiya*. According to him, there are 14 Manus in one Great Cycle, and in each Manu Period, there are 72 four-fold ages where each constituent age—Golden, Silver, Brazen, and Iron—are of equal length.$^1$

In this way, it is clear that according to Aryabhata, there are 14 x 72 = 1,008 four-fold ages <note>original: "Chaturyugas"</note> in one Great Cycle.

# Solar Treatise <note>original: "Surya Siddhanta"</note>

->Thus, a thousand Great Ages constitute the destroyer of beings:<-
->A Great Cycle is called a Day of Brahma; His Night is of the same length. || 20 ||<-

**Translation**—In this manner, one thousand Great Ages <note>original: "Mahayugas"</note> make one Great Cycle <term>Kalpa: A vast period of time in Hindu cosmology representing one day in the life of the creator god Brahma</term>, which is equal to one day of Brahma. A night of Brahma is of the same duration, during which the creation is dissolved. || 20 ||

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vigyan-Bhashya"</note>—A very beautiful illustration of Brahma’s day and night was provided by Lord Krishna in the eighth chapter of the *Bhagavad Gita* as follows:—

> “Those people who know the day and night <note>original: "ahoratra"</note> understand that a day of Brahma lasts for a thousand Great Ages, and his night also lasts for a thousand Great Ages. || 17 ||
> At the coming of day, all manifest things emerge from the Unmanifest <note>original: "Avyakta"</note>; at the arrival of night, they dissolve back into that very state known as the Unmanifest. || 18 ||
> This same multitude of beings, being born again and again, is dissolved helplessly, O Partha (Arjuna), at the arrival of night, and is reborn at the arrival of day.” || 19 ||

That is to say: “(17) Men who truly know the nature of day and night understand that (a Great Age <note>original: "Mahayuga"</note> consists of the four ages: Krita, Treta, Dvapara, and Kali, and) the duration of one thousand such Great Ages is one day of the god Brahma, and his night is of the same thousand-age duration.

(18) At the start of Brahma’s day, all manifest (material) objects are created from the Unmanifest, and when night falls, they merge back into that same previously mentioned Unmanifest.

(19) O Partha! This same community of beings (in this way) is born repeatedly and, being helpless—meaning, whether they wish it or not—merges as soon as night falls and takes birth (again) when day breaks.”*

->His total lifespan is one hundred years by that same calculation of days and nights.<-
->Half of his life has passed; of the remainder, this is the first Great Cycle. || 21 ||<-
->From this Great Cycle, six Manu Periods have passed, along with their transitions.<-
->Of the Manu Vaivasvata, twenty-seven Great Ages have passed. || 22 ||<-
->Of this twenty-eighth Great Age, one age—the Golden Age—has passed.<-
->Therefore, calculating the time, one should sum the numbers together. || 23 ||<-

**Translation**—(21) The total lifespan of Brahma is one hundred years, measured by his own scale of days and nights. At this time, half of Brahma’s life has already passed. This current Great Cycle <note>original: "Kalpa"</note> is the first one of the remaining half of his life—

# Chapter on the Mean Motions <note>original: "Madhyamadhikara"</note>

(22) In this Great Cycle <term>Kalpa: A "day" in the life of the creator deity, lasting 4.32 billion years</term>, six Manu periods <note>original: "Manus"</note> including their twilight junctions <note>original: "sandhis"</note> have passed; twenty-seven Great Ages <note>original: "Mahayugas"</note> of the seventh Manu, Vaivasvata, have also passed; (23) and of the twenty-eighth Great Age, the Golden Age <note>original: "Satya Yuga"</note> has ended. Therefore, to calculate the total elapsed time, these quantities should be gathered together. || 21-23 ||

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vigyan-Bhashya"</note>—The measure of a lifespan is traditionally considered to be one hundred years. The maximum lifespan of a human is one hundred solar years; the lifespan of a god is one hundred divine years (where one divine year equals 360 solar years). In the same way, the lifespan of Brahma should be understood as one hundred "Brahma years." One Brahma year consists of 360 Brahma days, and one Brahma day (a full day and night) consists of two Great Cycles <note>original: "Kalpas"</note> or 2,000 Great Ages <note>original: "Mahayugas"</note>. 

According to this calculation, fifty years of Brahma’s life have already passed. The first day (Kalpa) of his fifty-first year has begun. Within this current day, six Manu periods (including their junctions), twenty-seven Great Ages, and the Golden Age <note>original: "Satya Yuga"</note> of the twenty-eighth Great Age have passed. It should be remembered here that this statement is being made at the conclusion of the Golden Age, as is evident from the phrase "when the Golden Age was nearly finished" <note>original: "alpavasishtetu krite"</note> in the second verse. Based on this calculation, the time from the beginning of the current Great Cycle to the end of the Golden Age of the 28th Great Age is determined as follows:—

| :--- | :---: | :--- |
| Initial twilight of the Great Cycle <note>original: "Kalpa-adi-sandhya"</note> | = | 1,728,000 |
| 6 Manu periods = 6 x 306,720,000 | = | 1,840,320,000 |
| 6 Manu twilights <note>original: "sandhyas"</note> | | |
| &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; = 6 x 1,728,000 | = | 10,368,000 |

| Golden Age <note>original: "Satya Yuga"</note> of the 28th Great Age | = | 1,728,000 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ∴ Total time from the start of the Great Cycle | | |
| to the end of the Golden Age | = | 1,972,984,000 |

At this current time, in the year 1978 of the Vikram Era <note>approx. 1921 CE</note>, 5,023 years of the Iron Age <note>original: "Kali Yuga"</note> have passed. Therefore, if one wishes to know the total time from the beginning of the Great Cycle until now, one must add the 1,296,000 solar years of the Silver Age <note>original: "Treta"</note>, the 864,000 solar years of the Bronze Age <note>original: "Dvapara"</note>, and the 5,023 years of the Iron Age <note>original: "Kali Yuga"</note> to the total solar years listed above (which ended at the Golden Age). Thus, the total time elapsed from the beginning of the Great Cycle until now is 1,972,949,023 solar years. <note>Note: The calculation in the source text results in 1,97,29,49,023, though modern arithmetic on these figures may vary slightly.</note> In the ritual declaration <term>Sankalpa: A formal statement of intent made at the start of Hindu rituals, including the current cosmic date</term>, time is calculated in this exact manner, the chronological portion of which is as follows:—

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta <note>The Sun Treatise</note></header>

In the second half of the life of the current Brahma, in the "White Boar" Eon <term>Shveta-Varaha Kalpa: The current 4.32-billion-year cycle of the universe</term>, in the era of the Manu named Vaivasvata, in the twenty-eighth Great Age, in the first quarter of the Iron Age <note>original: "Kali Yuga"</note>... during the current incarnation of the Buddha, in this present year named [Name], in the [Northern/Southern] path of the sun, in the [Name] season, in the [Name] month, in the [Bright/Dark] fortnight, on the [Number] lunar day, on [Name] day of the week, under the [Name] lunar mansion <note>original: "Nakshatra"</note> joined with the Moon... on this lunar day... <note>This paragraph reflects a "Sankalpa," a traditional ritual declaration of the exact coordinate of time and space where a practitioner currently stands.</note>

**According to the view of Aryabhata, the time elapsed from the beginning of the Eon <note>Kalpa</note> to the beginning of the Iron Age <note>Kali Yuga</note> is:**
$= 6 \text{ Manus} + 27 \text{ Great Ages} <note>Chaturyugas</note> + \frac{3}{4} \text{ of a Great Age}$
$= (6 \times 72) + 27 + \frac{3}{4} \text{ Great Ages}$
$= 432 + 27 + \frac{3}{4} \text{ Great Ages}$
$= 459 \frac{3}{4} \times 4,320,000 \text{ solar years}$
$= (460 - \frac{1}{4}) \times 4,320,000 \text{ solar years}$
$= 1,987,200,000 - 1,080,000 \text{ solar years}$
$= 1,986,120,000 \text{ solar years.}$

If 5,023 years are added to this, the total number of solar years passed from the beginning of the Eon to the year 1976 of the Vikrama Era <note>approximately 1919 CE</note> can be found. Astronomers like Brahmagupta and Bhaskaracharya did not accept this specific view of Aryabhata. According to their calculations, the number of solar years passed since the beginning of the Eon is the same as that calculated in the *Surya Siddhanta*.

The names of the six passed Manu-intervals <term>Manvantaras: Periods of time ruled by a specific progenitor</term> are—(1) Svayambhuva, (2) Svarocisha, (3) Auttama, (4) Tamasa, (5) Raivata, and (6) Chakshusha. The name of the current Manu-interval is Vaivasvata. The current Eon <note>Kalpa</note> is called the White Boar Eon <note>Shveta-Varaha-Kalpa</note>.

> While creating the planets, stars, gods, demons, and the entire moving and unmoving world,
> Four hundred and seventy-four multiplied by one hundred divine years of Brahma passed. || 24 ||
> <note>original: "Graharksha-deva-daitya-adi-srijato-asya characharam / Krita-adri-veda-divyabda shataghna vedhaso gatah"</note>

**Translation**—In the process of creating the moving and unmoving world—including the planets, lunar mansions, gods, demons, humans, animals, birds, mountains, and trees—Brahma spent 47,400 divine years, or $47,400 \times 360 = 17,064,000$ solar years. (Therefore, the full creation was ready after this much time had elapsed from the very start of the Eon). || 24 ||

**Scientific Commentary**—The *Surya Siddhanta* holds the view that the creation was not instantaneous at the very start of the Eon. Brahma required 17,064,000 solar years to complete the creative process.

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

The second Aryabhata <note>original: "Aryabhata II," a 10th-century astronomer distinct from the earlier, more famous Aryabhata I</note> also holds this same view; however, from the mathematics of Brahmagupta <note>a 7th-century mathematician-astronomer</note>, Bhaskaracharya <note>a 12th-century mathematician-astronomer</note>, and others, it appears that this view was not acceptable to them. This is because they have calculated from the beginning of the Eon <term>Kalpa: A vast period of 4.32 billion years</term> to determine the positions of the planets; but the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>The Sun Treatise</note> has excluded the time taken for the creation of the universe from its planetary calculations.

->Moving westward with extreme speed along with the stars, the planets,<-
->while traveling in their own paths, lag behind by an equal amount. [25]<-
->Therefore, they appear to have an eastward motion; through their revolutions, their daily motion<-
->appears different due to the circumference of their orbits; because of this, they traverse the stars. [26]<-
->A fast-moving planet completes the circle in a short time, a slow-moving one in a long time;<-
->their completed revolutions at the end of the star Revati are known as "Bhaganas." [27]<-

**Translation—** (25) While always moving westward along with the fast-moving stars <note>original: "Nakshatras"</note>, the planets lag behind in their respective orbits by an equal amount of distance; (26) therefore, they appear to be moving toward the east, and their daily motion appears different according to the circumference of their orbits; thus, they complete the circle of stars in different amounts of time, meaning (27) those that move quickly complete it in a short time and those that move slowly in a long time. A completed cycle ending at the star Revati <note>the terminal star of the 27 lunar mansions, used as the zero-point for the zodiac</note> is called a revolution <term>Bhagana: One full 360-degree orbit of a planet</term>. [25-27]

**Scientific Commentary—** In these three verses, the principle of planetary motion is explained; therefore, these are very important verses. From these, we can briefly understand the views held by ancient Indian astronomers regarding the planets. In the 25th verse, it is explained that all the stars visible in the sky are moving westward along with the planets; however, because the stars move so quickly, the planets are left behind, and thus they appear to move toward the east. Their speed of moving eastward is actually uniform <note>in terms of linear distance traveled through space</note>, but because the size of their orbits differs, their [angular] speed appears different. The secret of this will be revealed by the following illustration—Suppose that in the provided diagram, the inner circle is 10 inches and the outer is 15 inches. Suppose that from positions B and C, which are aligned with center A, two ants start walking to orbit the inner and outer circles at a speed of 1 inch per second; then it is clear that the ant walking on the outer circle will complete one revolution in 15 seconds, and the ant walking on the inner circle will complete one revolution in 10 seconds. This proves that when moving at an equal linear speed, a revolution of orbits of different sizes will occur in different amounts of time. However, in the 27th verse...

<summary>This page explains the ancient Indian astronomical theory that planets only appear to move eastward because they travel westward more slowly than the stars. It introduces the concept of a 'Bhagana' (revolution) and explains how orbital circumference determines the time a planet takes to complete its cycle.</summary>
<keywords>planetary motion, Surya Siddhanta, Aryabhata, Bhagana, orbital circumference, Revati star, westward motion, Nakshatras</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the explanation of planetary motion described in verses 25-27 from the previous page, specifically addressing the apparent contradiction between equal linear speed and differing orbital periods.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta

->Figure 1<-

It has been stated that fast-moving planets complete their orbits in a short time, while slow-moving planets take a longer time. Here, there seems to be a contradiction, but it is not actually a contradiction; this is because the "equal motion" described in the previous verse refers to <term>Yojanatmaka motion: velocity measured in distance units or Yojanas</term>, whereas the motion described in this verse is <term>Angular velocity (Konatmaka gati)</term>. A full revolution consists of 360 degrees; therefore, one inch on an outer circle might create an angle of $\frac{360}{15} = 24^\circ$ at the center, while one inch on an inner circle might create an angle of $\frac{360}{10} = 36^\circ$ at the center. Thus, even if the <term>rectilinear motion (Rekhatmaka gati)</term> of two ants is equal at 1 inch per second, their angular velocities per second are different. The ant on the outer circle moves 24° per second, while the one on the inner circle moves 36° per second. Therefore, it is clear that the one moving "faster" (in terms of angle) will complete the revolution in less time, and the "slower" one in more time.

In the 27th verse, the definition of a <term>Bhagana: a complete planetary revolution</term> is also provided. Starting from the end of the constellation Revati <note>The fixed starting point for astronomical measurements in the Indian zodiac</note> and moving toward the east, when a planet completes one full circle and returns again to the end of Revati, it completes one *Bhagana* (the group of 27 constellations). For this reason, a *Bhagana* is also called a "revolution" or "cycle."

<summary>This page explains the difference between linear and angular velocity in ancient Indian astronomy, using an analogy of circles to clarify why planets with equal speed traverse their orbits in different timeframes. It also defines 'Bhagana' as a complete revolution measured from the star Revati.</summary>

<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, angular velocity, linear velocity, Bhagana, Revati constellation, planetary motion, Indian astronomy</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion from page 16 regarding the relationship between a planet's linear speed and its orbital period, transitioning into the standard units of angular measurement used in Indian astronomy.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

# Chapter on Mean Motion

According to this principle, it must be accepted that there is a specific relationship between the distance of the planets and their <term>Bhagana-kala: the orbital period or the time taken to complete one revolution</term>. The farther a planet is, the longer its orbital period. This relationship is explained here very briefly. A full explanation, comparing the theories of Indian and Western astronomers, will be provided in the twelfth chapter titled <term>Bhugoladhyaya: Chapter on the Sphere of the Earth</term>.

When the <term>Rekhatmaka gati: linear velocities</term> of all planets are assumed to be equal, it can be easily proven what the mutual relationship between the distances of the planets is; for it is not difficult to know how many days any given planet takes to complete one revolution. When it is known that Saturn completes one revolution in roughly 30 years and the Sun in 1 year, and if the linear velocities of both are equal, then it is self-evident that Saturn’s orbit is 30 times the size of the Sun’s orbit. This is because, in 30 years, the Sun travels 30 times its own orbit, while Saturn is only able to complete its orbit once. Therefore, Saturn’s orbit = 30 × Sun’s orbit. In other words, Saturn’s distance from the Earth is 30 times the Sun’s distance. The distances of other planets can be known in the same way. <note>This ancient calculation assumes planets move at the same speed in miles/yojanas, so a longer time to return to the start implies a proportionally larger circle.</note>

Through modern research, it has been discovered that the relationship between the mutual distances of the planets is not so simple, nor are their linear velocities equal. It is now proven that Saturn is approximately 10 times as far from the Earth as the Sun is.

->Sixty seconds make a minute; sixty of those make a degree.<-
->Thirty of those degrees make a sign, and twelve of those are a revolution. || 28 ||<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "vikalānīṃ kalā ṣaṣṭyā tat ṣaṣṭyā bhāga ucyate | tattiṃśatā bhavedrāśiḥ bhagaṇo dvādaśaiva te || 28 ||"</note>

**Translation**—Sixty <term>Vikala: arc-seconds</term> make one <term>Kala: arc-minute</term>, sixty minutes make one <term>Bhaga: degree</term>, thirty degrees make one <term>Rashi: zodiac sign</term>, and twelve signs make one <term>Bhagana: revolution</term>. || 28 ||

**Scientific Commentary**—These are the units for measuring angles. The entire <term>Nakshatrachakra: the star-cycle or zodiac</term> is called a <term>Bhagana</term>. If this cycle is divided into 12 equal parts, each part is called a <term>Rashi</term>. The thirtieth part of a sign is called an <term>Ansha: degree</term>, the sixtieth part of a degree is a <term>Kala</term>, and the sixtieth part of a minute is a <term>Vikala</term>. Among these, the terms "Revolution" and "Sign" are used specifically for that celestial circle (the ecliptic) in whose plane the Sun appears to orbit the Earth and the other planets orbit with slight deviations. However, degrees, minutes, and seconds are also used to measure other angles. Nowadays, the method for writing degrees in shorthand is to place a small circle slightly to the right above the number representing the magnitude of the degree—

<sig>2</sig>

<vocab>Bhagana-kala (orbital period), Rekhatmaka gati (linear motion), Vikala (arc-second), Kala (arc-minute), Ansha (degree), Rashi (zodiac sign), Bhagana (revolution), Nakshatrachakra (star-cycle/zodiac), Bhugoladhyaya (cosmology chapter)</vocab>

<meta>Continuity note: Although the previous page was numbered 17, this page is numbered 58 in the source text. However, the subject matter follows the discussion of planetary motions and their mathematical relationships. This page transitions from measuring units to the specific orbital revolution counts (Bhaganas) within a Great Age (Mahayuga).</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

To denote minutes, we draw a small slanted line leaning to the left, slightly to the right above the number. For seconds, we draw two such slanted lines. For example, if we want to write 5 degrees, 16 minutes, and 50 seconds, it is written as 5° 16' 50''.

There is a close relationship between the units used to measure angles and the units used to measure time. The time the Sun takes to complete one <term>Bhagana: a full revolution of 360 degrees</term> is one year; the time it takes to traverse one <term>Rashi: zodiac sign or 30 degrees</term> is one month; the time it takes to travel one <term>Ansha: degree</term> is one solar day; the time it takes to travel one <term>Kala: minute of arc</term> is one <term>Ghati: an ancient unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>; and the time it takes to travel one <term>Vikala: second of arc</term> is nearly equal to one <term>Pala: an ancient unit of time equal to 24 seconds</term>.

->In a Yuga, the revolutions of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus, moving eastward, are 4,320,000.<-
->The revolutions of the "conjunctions" (Shighras) of Mars, Saturn, and Jupiter are the same. (29)<-
->For the Moon, they are 57,753,336; for Mars, they are 2,296,832. (30)<-
->For the conjunction of Mercury, they are 17,937,060; for Jupiter, 364,220. (31)<-
->For the conjunction of Venus, 7,022,376; for Saturn, 146,568. (32)<-
->The Moon’s Apogee (Chandrocca) has 488,203 revolutions in a Yuga;<-
->The Lunar Node (Chandrapata), moving westward, has 232,238. (33)<-
<note>original: "युगे सूर्यज्ञशुक्राणां... वामं पातस्य..."</note>

### **Translation**
(29) In one <term>Mahayuga: a great cycle of 4,320,000 years</term>, the revolutions of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus moving toward the east are 4,320,000. The <term>Shighras: the fast-moving points or conjunctions used to calculate planetary positions</term> of Mars, Saturn, and Jupiter have the same number of revolutions. (30) The Moon has 57,753,336 revolutions, and Mars has 2,296,832 revolutions. (31) The Mercury-conjunction has 17,937,060 revolutions, and Jupiter has 364,220. (32) The Venus-conjunction has 7,022,376 revolutions, and Saturn has 146,568. (33) The Moon’s Apogee (the point furthest from Earth) has 488,203 revolutions, and the Lunar Node (the point where the Moon crosses the ecliptic), which moves westward (retrograde), has 232,238 revolutions. || 29-33 ||

### **Scientific Commentary**
At this point, it is helpful to explain the ancient Indian system for writing numbers in verse. To express digits like one, two, three, or four in poetry, specific words were traditionally assigned to them. These words, or their synonyms, are placed in order according to the rule <term>Ankanam vamato gatih: numbers move to the left</term>. This means the word representing the "ones" place is written first, followed by the "tens" place, and so on.

<note>For example, in Verse 29, the number 4,320,000 is described using "Kha-chatushka" (four voids/zeros), "Rada" (teeth, of which there are 32), and "Arnava" (oceans, of which there are 4). Reading these right-to-left gives the full figure.</note>

<summary>This page defines the notation for angular measurements (degrees, minutes, seconds) and lists the specific number of orbital revolutions each celestial body completes in a Mahayuga cycle of 4,320,000 years.</summary>
<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, Bhagana, Kala, Vikala, Ansha, Mahayuga, Ankanam vamato gatih, Sun, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Moon, Lunar Apogee, Lunar Node</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion of planetary revolutions (Bhaganas) within a Mahayuga (Great Age). While the previous page listed the specific revolution counts for the Sun, Moon, and planets according to the Surya Siddhanta, this page explains the mathematical notation used in these texts and defines the "orbital period" (Bhagana-kala).</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion, 16</header>

In the system used here, words denoting numbers are placed in sequence: first the word for the units place, then the word for the tens place, and then the word for the hundreds place. For example, if one wants to say 325, first a word indicating 5 is written—such as <term>Pancha: five</term>, <term>Ishu: arrow</term>, or <term>Margana: arrow</term> <note>These words symbolize 5 because in Indian mythology, the god of love carries five arrows.</note>—followed by a word indicating 2, such as <term>Dvi: two</term>, <term>Ashvi: the twin celestial physicians</term>, or <term>Yama: the god of death, often associated with twins</term>. Finally, a word for 3 is written, such as <term>Tri: three</term>, <term>Agni: fire</term>, or <term>Shikhi: flame</term> <note>Fire is associated with the number 3 because of the three ritual fires in Vedic tradition.</note>. In this way, we can write 325 as *Panchashvishikhi* or *Ishuyamagni*. This is the standard convention for writing numbers in the *Surya Siddhanta*, *Brahmasphuta-Siddhanta*, and *Siddhanta-Shiromani*. However, the methods for writing numbers in the *Aryabhatiya* by Aryabhata I and the *Maha-Siddhanta* by Aryabhata II are different from this.

The number of <term>Bhaganas: full revolutions</term> a planet completes in one <term>Mahayuga: a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</term> according to the *Surya Siddhanta* has been given above. There are slight differences in the values of these revolutions according to the systems of Aryabhata and Brahmagupta. Furthermore, the values for revolutions known today through the help of modern precision instruments do not match any single ancient system exactly, but rather show slight variations. On the following page, we provide a comparative view of the values from the *Surya Siddhanta*, the *Brahma-Siddhanta*, and modern calculations to demonstrate how remarkably small the difference is between the calculations of our ancient astronomers and those made with modern instruments.

The time it takes for a planet to complete one full revolution is called the <term>Bhagana-kala: orbital period</term>. According to the ancient texts, the method to calculate this is to divide the total number of solar years in a *Mahayuga* by the number of revolutions that planet completes in that same period. This will give you the length of one revolution in <term>Solar years</term>. You may then express this as a decimal fraction or in <term>Savana days: terrestrial or civil days</term>. To find the orbital period in *Savana* days, the easiest method is to divide the total number of *Savana* days in a *Mahayuga* by the total number of revolutions. The resulting quotient is the number of whole days. The remainder can be converted into <term>Ghati: 24 minutes</term>, <term>Pala: 24 seconds</term>, <term>Vipala: 0.4 seconds</term>, and so on. Just as one *Ghati* contains 60 *Palas*, one can also conceptualize one *Pala* containing 60 *Vipalas*, and one *Vipala* containing 60 *Prativipalas*.

The new terms used in these verses are <term>Graha-shighra: planetary conjunction or 'fast' motion</term>, <term>Chandrocca: Lunar Apogee</term>, and <term>Pata: Node</term>. To understand these terms, we must first understand the concepts our ancient sages held regarding the movement of planets. They considered the Earth to be stationary and believed that the Sun, Moon, planets, and stars revolved around it. However, this alone did not allow for a perfectly accurate calculation of planetary motion; therefore, they conceptualized the *Graha-shighras*. They observed that two planets, Mercury and Venus, always remained in the vicinity of the Sun; therefore...

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

To know the positions of these planets, one must first understand where the Sun is located. Once the Sun's position is determined, it becomes certain that Mercury will be approximately 28.1° ahead of or behind the Sun, and Venus will be approximately 47.2° ahead of or behind the Sun. This is why, in the 26th verse, the <term>Mahayuga Revolutions</term> <note>original: Mahayugiya Bhagana; the total number of orbits in a cycle of 4,320,000 years</note> of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus are stated to be equal. However, to determine exactly how far ahead or behind the Sun Mercury or Venus is, one cannot proceed without knowing the positions of their <term>Shighroccas</term> <note>literally "apex of quick motion"; the mathematical point used to calculate the planet's actual position relative to its orbital speed around the Sun</note>. The revolution periods of these "fast points" are equal to the time it takes for Mercury or Venus to orbit the Sun according to modern views. Therefore, the revolution period of the <term>Shighrocca</term> of Mercury or Venus should be understood as the time they take to orbit the Sun, rather than their orbit around the <term>star circle</term> <note>original: nakshatra chakra; the zodiac</note>. The case of the <term>Shighroccas</term> of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn is different from the two planets mentioned above. The revolution periods of their "fast points" are considered to be the same as that of the Sun. This means that the <term>Shighroccas</term> of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn are points that complete one full revolution of the star circle in one year. Since the Sun also completes one revolution of the star circle in one year, the <term>Shighroccas</term> of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn always remain near the Sun. These <term>Shighroccas</term> will be discussed in greater detail in the second chapter.

**Mandocca or Apogee**—It has been stated above that the Moon's <term>Mandocca</term> <note>literally "apex of slow motion"; the apogee, or the point where the planet is furthest from Earth</note> completes 488,203 revolutions in one <term>Mahayuga</term>. Therefore, according to the *Surya Siddhanta*, one revolution period is equal to 3232 <term>Savana days</term> <note>civil days from sunrise to sunrise</note>, 5 <term>ghadis</term>, 37 <term>palas</term>, and 13.6 <term>vipalas</term>. The Moon's apogee is that point in the <term>lunar orbit</term> <note>original: chandrakaksha</note> which is at the greatest distance from the Earth compared to all other points in the orbit. When the Moon is at this point, it appears very small in size because of the great distance, and its motion is also very slow. There is also a point in the lunar orbit at 180° from the <term>lunar apogee</term> <note>original: chandrochcha</note> which is very close to the Earth. When the Moon reaches this point, its speed becomes the fastest, and because it is so close, its size appears very large. Due to this uneven motion of the Moon, it is not easy to say what its position will be at any given time. It was also [mentioned] above...

---

1. The maximum distance <note>elongation</note> of Mercury from the Sun is between 16° 12' and 28° 48'.

2. The maximum distance of Venus from the Sun does not exceed 47°. (**Outlines of Astronomy by Herschel pp. 281 and 291**)

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

It has been stated that the Moon's revolution period is 27.32167 mean solar days <note>original: madhyama savana dina; a "savana" day is the time between one sunrise and the next</note>. The position of the Moon calculated from this is called the **mean position** <note>original: madhyama sthana; the position assuming a perfectly circular and uniform orbit</note>. From this mean position, the Moon is sometimes seen slightly ahead and sometimes slightly behind. The actual position where the Moon is observed is called the **true position** <note>original: spashta sthana; the real-time coordinates in the sky</note>. The maximum difference between the mean position and the true position is 5° 2' 30". The center of the lunar orbit is considered to be at a distance from the Earth equal to the **sine** of this angle, and the Moon is seen to revolve around the Earth while moving around this center. In Figure 2, **P** is the center of the Earth, **C** is the center of the Moon’s orbit, and **PC**

->Figure 2 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Figure 3<-

->Figure 4<-

is the sine of 5° 2' 30". The Moon revolves around the Earth while moving on the **U-Cha-Ni** <note>original: u-ca-ni; an abbreviation for Ucca (high/apogee), Chaya (shadow/node), and Nicha (low/perigee)</note> circle. It is clear that when the Moon is at **U**, it is at its greatest distance from **P**, and when it is at **Ni**, it is at its closest distance. **U** is called the <term>Apogee</term> (**apogee**) <note>original: chandrocca; the point in the orbit farthest from Earth</note> and **Ni** is called the <term>Perigee</term> (**perigee**) <note>original: nicha; the point in the orbit closest to Earth</note>. This point **U**

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>Continuity note: Page 22 introduced the concept of the Moon’s mean and true positions, explaining how the Moon's orbit was traditionally viewed as a circle with the Earth slightly off-center. This page (Page 23) transitions from that ancient model to the modern understanding of elliptical orbits and begins discussing the orbital nodes.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

The Moon's <term>apogee</term> <note>original: ucca; the point in the orbit furthest from Earth</note> does not remain fixed in one place in the sky but instead moves slowly toward the east. The Moon's apogee completes one full revolution in approximately 3,232 <term>solar days</term> <note>original: savana dina; the time from one sunrise to the next</note>. The apogees of other planets move even more slowly toward the east. Nowadays, the ancient model of circular epicycles is no longer used. Science has proven that the Moon revolves around the Earth, and the Earth and other planets revolve around the Sun; furthermore, the path of this revolution is not a perfect circle, but an <term>ellipse</term> <note>original: dirgha-vritta; literally "long-circle"</note>. Before discussing this further, it is necessary to explain some properties of an ellipse. **U-Ca-Ni** is a diagram of an ellipse (Figure 3). **U-Ni** is called the **major axis** and **Ca-Ci** is called the **minor axis**, and the point **K**, where these two axes meet, is called the center of the ellipse. At the center, the minor axis and major axis are divided into two equal parts. On the major axis, at an equal distance from the center, there are two points, **N** and **Na**, such that if they are connected to any point **P, Pa,** or **Pi** on the ellipse, then **P N + P Na = Pa N + Pa Na = Pi N + Pi Na**. The points **N** and **Na** are called the **foci** <note>original: nabhi; literally "navel" or "hub"</note> of the ellipse. If **U-Ca-Ni** is taken to be the Moon’s orbit, then the Earth's position would be at focus **N**. The distance from **N** to the Moon's orbit is greatest at point **U** and least at point **Ni**; therefore, point **U** is called the Moon's <term>Apogee</term> and point **Ni** is called the Moon's <term>Perigee</term> <note>original: nicha</note>. If the position of the apogee is known, the position of the perigee can be easily found, as it is always exactly 180° away from the apogee.

Similarly, the Earth, Mars, Mercury, Venus, etc., also revolve around the Sun in ellipses, and the Sun stays at one of the foci of these orbital circles. The reason why the speed is very slow at the "high" point (apogee/aphelion) and very fast at the "low" point (perigee/perihelion) is the increasing and decreasing of <term>gravitational force</term> <note>original: akarshan shakti</note>. When a planet is at its furthest point, the gravitational force is at its weakest because the distance is so great, causing the planet’s speed to slow down. When it is at its closest point, the gravitational force is at its strongest because the distance is so small, which makes the planet’s speed very fast. Several rules have been discovered regarding this, which are famous as **Kepler’s Laws**; these will be discussed at the appropriate place in the second chapter, titled the "Chapter on True Motion" <note>original: Spashtadhikara</note>.

**Nodes** <note>original: Pata</note>—The path on which the Sun appears to move around the sky over the course of one year is called the **Ecliptic** <note>original: kranti-vritta</note>. Similarly, the path on which the Moon moves as it revolves around the Earth is called the **Lunar Orbit** <note>original: chandra-kaksha</note>. The Ecliptic and the Lunar Orbit are not on the same plane, nor are they parallel; therefore, these two orbits appear to meet each other at two points—just as the strings of two flying kites might appear to meet at one point even though they are actually far apart...

It also introduces the concept of the Ecliptic and the Lunar Nodes where the paths of the Sun and Moon intersect.</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>Continuity note: The previous page concluded with a discussion of the Earth's position within an elliptical orbit. This page (Page 25) continues the explanation of orbital dynamics, specifically introducing the concept of orbital nodes and the astronomical reality behind Rahu and Ketu.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...appear to be; and due to the difference in the motions of those celestial bodies, this point is not seen in the same direction. These points are called the Moon's nodes <note>original: pāta</note>. Moving in its own orbit, the Moon remains north of the **ecliptic** <note>the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> for half of its orbital period and stays south of the ecliptic for the other half. When it reaches its node, it moves either toward the north or toward the south of the ecliptic. The node at which it arrives and begins moving toward the north is called the North Node (**Ascending node**), and the node at which it arrives and begins moving toward the south is called the South Node (**Descending node**).

The North Node is also known as **Rahu**, while the South Node is known as **Ketu**. When the Moon is near these nodes at the time of a full moon or a new moon, a lunar or solar eclipse occurs; for this reason, the mythological idea arose that Rahu and Ketu are demons that cause eclipses. Some people believe that the tip of the Earth’s shadow is Rahu and the tip of the Moon’s shadow is Ketu; however, this is a misconception.

The positions of these nodes are not fixed; rather, they appear to shift slowly westward. The time it takes for them to complete one full revolution while shifting westward is called their <term>revolution period</term> <note>original: bhagaṇa-kāla</note>. The nodes of other planets should be understood in the same way. The reason why they shift westward is explained in **Physical Astronomy** through very precise mathematics, which will also be explained at the appropriate place in this scientific commentary. In <term>Figure 4</term>, if **Sa-Ra-Sa-Ka** is taken as the Sun’s path—that is, the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>original: krāntivritta</note>—and **Cha-Ra-Cha-Ka** as the Moon’s orbit, then points **Ra** and **Ka** are called the Moon's nodes. While revolving in the direction of the arrow, when the Moon moves past node **Ra**, it goes north of the ecliptic and remains north through the **Ra-Cha-Ka** segment; therefore, node **Ra** is called the North Node. Upon reaching point **Ka**, the Moon moves south of the ecliptic, so **Ka** is called the South Node. 

Indian astronomers call the Moon’s North Node Rahu and the South Node Ketu. The angle **Cha-Ra-Sa** is the angle between the planes of the ecliptic and the Moon’s orbit, the value of which is approximately 5°. This angle is called the Moon’s <term>inclination</term> <note>original: vikśepa; also refers to celestial latitude</note>, which is discussed in the 68th verse of this chapter.

Here, a question may be raised: how were the revolution periods mentioned above determined, and why are there differences between various schools of thought? According to the great mathematician **Bhaskaracharya**, the answer is as follows:

> This can only be understood through the study of the celestial sphere by one who is skilled in the use of various instruments and knowledgeable in the arrangement of the celestial fields, and not by anyone else. The slow and fast apogees and nodes of the planets, moving in their respective paths, [complete] this many revolutions in a <term>Kalpa</term>... <note>original Sanskrit: "Sātu tattadvāpākuśalena tattad kśetrasaṃsthānajñena śruta golenaiva śrotuṃ śakyate, nāntyena. Graha manda śīghrocca pātāḥ sva svamārgeṣu gacchantah etāvataḥ paryyayān kalpe"; A Kalpa is an immense period of time in Hindu cosmology, totaling 4.32 billion years.</note>

<summary>This page defines the orbital nodes (Rahu and Ketu) where the Moon's path crosses the ecliptic, explaining their role in eclipses and their westward regression. It concludes by citing Bhaskaracharya on the necessity of observational instruments to calculate these movements over vast cosmological cycles.</summary>
<keywords>ascending node, descending node, Rahu, Ketu, ecliptic, lunar orbit, Bhaskaracharya, inclination, Kalpa, Indian astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>Continuity note: The previous page discussed the nodes of the Moon (Rahu and Ketu) and the shifting nature of planetary orbits. This page shifts focus to the philosophical and practical foundations of astronomical knowledge, debating whether tradition or direct observation should be the ultimate authority for determining the length of planetary cycles.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

The proof for the statement "they perform [these revolutions]" is tradition <note>original: āgama; refers to the established astronomical texts and parameters passed down through generations</note> alone. Over a vast span of time, that tradition has become varied and conflicting due to the errors of scribes, teachers, and students. In such a case, which one should be considered authoritative? 

Now, if it is argued that in the field of mathematical astronomy, only a tradition supported by rational proof <note>original: upapatti; the logical or observational demonstration of a mathematical truth</note> is authoritative, and only those planetary revolutions <note>original: bhagaṇa; the number of full orbits a planet completes in a specific cosmic cycle</note> that are proven through observation should be accepted—that too is not correct. 

Why? Because even a very wise man can only understand the *method* of the proof; he cannot, through his own observation, determine the exact quantity of those revolutions because human life is too short. To establish a proof through observation, a planet must be tracked daily with an instrument until its entire cycle is complete. In this way, a single revolution of **Saturn** <note>original: śanaiścara</note> takes thirty years to complete. The revolutions of the **apsides** <note>original: mandocca; the point in an orbit where the planet is farthest from the center of motion</note>, however, require many hundreds of years. 

Therefore, this task is not achievable by a single person. For this reason, highly intelligent astronomers accept some established tradition that aligns with current observations and has been endorsed by a master mathematician. They then compose new treatises to demonstrate their own exceptional skill in the calculations of the celestial sphere, and to refute the incorrect meanings or errors introduced by others through confusion. Their intention is this: "We must demonstrate our skill in the necessary procedures of planetary mathematics, regardless of which tradition we follow."

**Meaning**—However, this method can only be understood by one who has mastered the technical language of <term>Jyotiḥśāstra</term> <note>the traditional Indian science of astronomy and mathematics</note>, knows the positions of the stars, and has studied the geography of the Earth and the mechanics of the celestial sphere. 

Regarding the fact that the planets, the **apsides** <note>original: mandocca</note>, the **conjunction-points** <note>original: śīghrocca</note>, and the **nodes** <note>original: pāta</note> perform a specific number of revolutions in a <term>Kalpa</term> <note>an immense eon in Hindu cosmology, often cited as 4.32 billion years</note> while traveling in their respective paths—the proof for this is <term>Agama</term> <note>traditional knowledge or scripture</note>. 

But as much time has passed, these traditions have become numerous because of the mistakes made by those who write, teach, and study them! Therefore, the question arises: which tradition should be taken as the standard? If one says that we should only accept the tradition that proves accurate according to mathematics, and only those revolution-counts that emerge from it—that is also not right. 

Even a person of great wisdom can only master the *ritī* <note>the procedural method or logic</note>; he cannot derive the total number of planetary revolutions through that method alone. This is because a human life is very short, and to obtain a "proof," one must observe the planet with an instrument every single day until the revolution is finished. In this manner, one revolution of **Saturn** is completed in 30 years. The revolutions of the **apsides** take many centuries. Thus, this work is not "man-achievable" <note>original: puruṣasādhya</note>. 

Consequently, wise astronomers adopt a tradition that is currently deemed accurate and has been accepted by a prestigious mathematician. They write further texts to display their skill in the mathematics of the planets and the celestial sphere, and to correct the harmful errors that have appeared due to delusion. Their motive is that they should show proficiency in the correct calculation of the planets, no matter which tradition is used as the base.

---

*From the **Siddhānta Śiromaṇi, Gaṇitādhyāya**, pages 16-17 (Calcutta edition, Second Edition).

<summary>This page discusses the tension between traditional astronomical parameters and direct observation, concluding that since planetary cycles (like those of Saturn and the apsides) exceed a human lifespan, astronomers must rely on refined tradition as a starting point for their calculations.</summary>
<keywords>Agama, Upapatti, planetary revolutions, Saturn, Apsides, Bhaskara II, Siddhanta Shiromani, Indian astronomy, mathematical proof, celestial sphere</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>Continuity note: Following the discussion on the limitations of human lifespan in observing long-term planetary cycles, this page presents Bhaskaracharya’s specific observational methods for calculating the shorter cycles of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus, bridging the gap between theoretical tradition and practical proof.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

Regarding the <term>bhagaṇa</term> <note>the number of full revolutions or orbits completed in a cosmic cycle</note> of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus, <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> <note>a preeminent 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> states that there are as many <term>bhagaṇa</term> of the Sun as there are years in a <term>Kalpa</term> <note>an immense eon in Hindu cosmology, often cited as 4.32 billion years</note>. Therefore, the time taken for one <term>bhagaṇa</term> of the Sun is exactly one year. Mercury and Venus always remain near the Sun, sometimes slightly ahead and sometimes slightly behind, like attendants. For this reason, their <term>bhagaṇa</term> are considered equal to the <term>bhagaṇa</term> of the Sun.

The following method is provided to determine the <term>bhagaṇa</term> period of the Sun:—

Draw a circle on level ground and mark the directions upon it. When the Sun begins its <term>Uttarayana</term> <note>the northward path of the sun, starting from the winter solstice</note>, on the day it rises slightly south of the East, perform a <term>bedha</term> <note>an observational sighting or "piercing" of the target</note> of the rising Sun using a nail fixed in the center of the circle. After this, one must count the sunrises for one full year. In one year, there will be 365 sunrises. The final sunrise will occur slightly south of the first day’s rising position. Note the difference between these two points. On the next day, perform a <term>bedha</term> of the rising Sun again. On this day, it will rise slightly north of the East. Observe how much further north it rises compared to the previous day’s position. Then, using a ratio, determine this: if the Sun moves this far north in 60 <term>ghadi</term> <note>an ancient unit of time; 1 ghadi equals 24 minutes</note>, how much time would it have taken to cover the first recorded difference? In this way, it is found that the Sun returns to the exact same position it occupied at the start of the year in 365 <term>savana</term> days <note>civil days measured from sunrise to sunrise</note>, 15 <term>ghadi</term>, 30 <term>pal</term>, 22 <term>vipal</term>, and 30 <term>prati-vipal</term> <note>subdivisions of time: 60 pal = 1 ghadi; 60 vipal = 1 pal; 60 prati-vipal = 1 vipal</note>. Therefore, this duration is the <term>bhagaṇa</term> period of the Sun. Then, by proportion, one can calculate that if there are this many <term>savana days</term> in one year, how many <term>savana days</term> there would be in the years of one <term>Kalpa</term>, and so on.

The method used today to determine the <term>vasanta-sampata</term> <note>the vernal equinox, when the sun crosses the celestial equator</note> is very similar to the method described by <term>Bhaskaracharya</term>. The difference is that <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> advised performing the <term>bedha</term> on the <term>kshitijavritta</term> <note>the horizon circle</note>, whereas today the <term>bedha</term> is performed on the <term>yamottara-vritta</term> <note>the meridian circle</note>, which prevents errors caused by <term>lamban</term> <note>parallax; the displacement in the apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight</note> and <term>prakash vakribhavan</term> <note>refraction of light through the atmosphere</note>. A second difference is that modern instruments are very precise, while <term>Bhaskaracharya’s</term> method relied on the naked eye.

The proof of the Moon's <term>bhagaṇa</term> should also be understood through <term>bedha</term> using the <term>gola yantra</term> <note>an armillary sphere or celestial globe</note> upon which the <term>nakshatra-chakra</term> <note>the circle of the constellations/zodiac</note>, <term>kranti-vritta</term> <note>the ecliptic</note>, <term>vishuvad-vritta</term> <note>the celestial equator</note>, <term>chandra-kaksha</term> <note>the lunar orbit</note>, and <term>graha-kaksha</term> <note>planetary orbits</note> are marked. It does not seem necessary to describe this in great detail. This has been written only to show that ancient astronomers also tested the statements given in texts through <term>bedha</term> (observation), and accepted only that which proved to be correct.

<vocab>Sun, Mercury, Venus, revolution (bhagaṇa), Bhaskaracharya, aeon (Kalpa), northward path (Uttarayana), observation (bedha), 24-minute unit (ghadi), 24-second unit (pal), 0.4-second unit (vipal), 0.006-second unit (prati-vipal), civil day (savana day), vernal equinox (vasanta-sampata), horizon circle (kshitijavritta), meridian circle (yamottara-vritta), parallax (lamban), refraction (prakash vakribhavan), armillary sphere (gola yantra), zodiac circle (nakshatra-chakra), ecliptic (kranti-vritta), celestial equator (vishuvad-vritta), lunar orbit (chandra-kaksha), planetary orbit (graha-kaksha)</vocab>

It also compares these medieval techniques with modern astronomical practices regarding the meridian and atmospheric refraction.</summary>
<keywords>Bhaskaracharya, solar year calculation, observational astronomy, bedha, gola yantra, meridian vs horizon, refraction and parallax, Sanskrit astronomy</keywords>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

## Method to Determine the Revolution Period of the Moon’s Apogee

Every day, one should perform a <term>bedha</term> <note>an observational sighting or "piercing" of the target</note> of the Moon using a <term>gola yantra</term> <note>an armillary sphere or celestial globe instrument</note> to determine its <term>spashta gati</term> <note>true or apparent motion</note>. On the day when the motion is at its minimum, there is no difference between the positions of the <term>madhyama</term> <note>mean</note> and <term>spashta</term> <note>true</note> Moon. This is the position of the Moon's <term>ucca</term> <note>apogee; the point in the orbit farthest from Earth where the planet appears to move slowest</note>. By continuing these daily observations, when the Moon's motion again reaches its absolute minimum, that position should be understood as the apogee. This position will be slightly ahead of the previous one. By determining how much further it has moved and using a proportion, one can calculate the daily motion of the apogee and the number of days required to complete one full <term>bhagaṇa</term> <note>revolution or orbit</note>.

**Method to Determine the Revolution Period of the Moon’s Node—** While performing daily observations of the Moon, one should observe on which day the Moon's <term>dakshina vikshepa</term> <note>southern celestial latitude</note> decreases until it becomes zero. At the moment the <term>vikshepa</term> is zero, the Moon is at the position of its <term>pata</term> <note>node; the point where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>. Similarly, during the next cycle, when the Moon's southern latitude again reaches zero, it should be understood that it has reached its node. This second time, the node’s position will be slightly to the west of its previous position; therefore, it is said that the motion of the node is <term>viloma</term> <note>retrograde; literally "against the hair" or backward</note>, meaning it moves toward the west. Then, through calculation by proportion, one should determine: if the node moves this much in these many days, how much will it move in a single day? This should be understood as the daily motion of the node. In this manner, one should also determine how many <term>bhagaṇa</term> <note>revolutions</note> occur in a <term>Kalpa</term> <note>an immense eon of time</note>.

**Regarding the Swift Apogees of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—** When the Sun is ahead of Saturn, Jupiter, or Mars, those planets are slightly ahead of their <term>madhyama</term> <note>mean</note> position, and when the Sun is behind them, the planets are behind their mean position. Therefore, scholars have theorized that the <term>sheeghrocca</term> <note>the "swift apogee" or the center of the epicycle; in Indian astronomy, the position of the Sun acts as the sheeghrocca for superior planets</note> of these three planets remains with the Sun and attracts the planets toward itself—that is, toward the Sun. Consequently, the <term>bhagaṇa</term> <note>revolutions</note> of their <term>sheeghroccas</term> are equal to those of the Sun.

> <note>original: "भानामष्टाक्षिवस्वद्रित्रिद्विद्वष्ट्शरेन्दवः । भोदया भगणैः स्वैः स्वैरूनाः स्वस्वोदया युगे ॥३४॥"</note>
> The revolutions of the stars in a Great Age are eight, two, eight, seven, three, three, two, two, eight, five, and one. The terrestrial risings of a planet in an eon are the stellar revolutions minus the planet's own revolutions. ॥34॥

**Translation—** In one <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note>, there are 1,58,22,37,828 revolutions of the <term>nakshatras</term> <note>stars/constellations</note>. By subtracting a planet's total revolutions in a <term>Mahayuga</term> from the total revolutions of the stars in a <term>Mahayuga</term>, the remainder is the number of times that planet rises on the eastern horizon during one <term>Mahayuga</term>. ॥34॥

**Scientific Commentary—** In the scientific commentary of the 12th verse, the definition of a <term>nakshatra ahoratra</term> <note>a sidereal day and night</note> was given. In one sidereal day, the stars move toward the west and complete one...

<summary>This page describes observational methods for calculating the motion of the Moon's apogee and nodes, explains the solar influence on the "swift apogees" of superior planets, and provides the calculation for planetary risings in a Mahayuga.</summary>
<keywords>Moon's apogee, Moon's node, retrograde motion, sheeghrocca, Mahayuga, sidereal revolutions, planetary risings, Surya Siddhanta</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>Continuity note: While the previous page (27) focused on the observational techniques for finding the Moon’s apogee (ucca) and nodes (pata) using the armillary sphere, this page (26) steps back to explain the mathematical relationship between sidereal rotations and civil days. It establishes the fundamental constants used to calculate the number of sunrises in a Great Age.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...they complete their revolution. This revolution is called a <term>nakshatra bhagana</term> <note>sidereal revolution; the completion of an orbit relative to the fixed stars</note>. Therefore, the number of sidereal revolutions that occur in one <term>mahayuga</term> <note>a "Great Age" consisting of 4,320,000 solar years</note> is exactly equal to the number of sidereal day-and-night cycles.

The latter part of the verse above explains the method for determining how many times a planet rises on the eastern horizon during one Mahayuga. Subtract the number of revolutions a planet makes in a Mahayuga from the total number of sidereal revolutions in that Mahayuga; the remaining number will be the total number of times that planet rises on the eastern horizon during that Great Age. For example, suppose we want to know how many times the Sun rises on the eastern horizon in one Mahayuga. In Verse 28, it is stated that the Sun completes 4,320,000 revolutions in a Mahayuga. If this is subtracted from the total sidereal revolutions of a Mahayuga (1,58,22,37,828), the remainder is 1,57,79,17,828. This is the number of times the Sun rises on the eastern horizon in one Mahayuga. However, as explained in the scientific commentary on Verse 12, the duration from one sunrise to the next is called a <term>savana dina</term> <note>civil day; a terrestrial day measured from sunrise to sunrise, rather than by the stars</note>. Therefore, according to Verse 34, there are 1,57,79,17,828 civil days in one Mahayuga.

The number of rises for other planets can be determined in the same way. The logic behind this is as follows: if on a certain day the Sun rises alongside a specific star, then on the next day, that star will rise approximately 3 minutes and 56 seconds earlier than the Sun. This occurs because, during that interval, the Sun moves approximately one degree eastward <note>against the background of the stars</note>. On the third day, the star will rise twice as early (7 minutes 52 seconds); on the fourth day, three times as early; and by the 16th day, fifteen times as early—approximately 59 minutes or nearly an hour before the Sun. Continuing this lag, by the 361st day (which is 360 <term>nakshatra dina</term> <note>sidereal days; rotations relative to the stars</note>), the star will rise 24 minutes less than 24 hours early. After 366 sidereal days, it will rise a full 24 hours (one full day) earlier, at which point the Sun and that star will once again rise together. Therefore, in the time it takes the stars to complete 366 revolutions, the Sun rises one fewer time and completes one full revolution of its own. Thus, in the period of one revolution (one <term>saur varsh</term> <note>solar year</note>), the Sun rises 366 minus 1 times. The rises of other planets should be calculated using this same principle.

> The lunar months are the difference between the revolutions of the Sun and the Moon.
> When the solar months are subtracted from these, the remainders are the intercalary months. || 35 ||
<note>original: "भवन्ति शशिनो मासा: सूर्य्यन्दुभगणान्तरम् । रविमासोनितास्ते तु शेषा: स्युरधिमासका: ॥३५॥"</note>

**Translation—** The difference between the total revolutions of the Sun and the Moon in a Mahayuga...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical derivation of "civil days" versus "sidereal days" within a Mahayuga, demonstrating that the number of sunrises is the total sidereal rotations minus the planet's own orbital revolutions. It provides a practical example for the Sun and introduces the calculation for lunar and intercalary months.</summary>
