--- Page 52 ---
<meta>Continuity note: The previous page concluded the calculation of civil days (savana dina) in a Great Age by subtracting solar revolutions from sidereal revolutions. This page continues the mathematical derivation of time units, specifically defining lunar months and the necessity of intercalary months (adhimasa) to synchronize lunar and solar calendars.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions 26</header>

That same number represents the lunar months in a <term>mahayuga</term> <note>a "Great Age" of 4,320,000 solar years</note>. When the number of solar months in a Great Age is subtracted from the number of lunar months in that Great Age, the remainder is the number of <term>adhimasas</term> <note>intercalary or "extra" months added to a lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar seasons</note>. ||35||

**Scientific Commentary —** The moment when the Sun and the Moon are in conjunction is called <term>amavasya</term> <note>the New Moon phase</note>. At this time, the Moon and the Sun are very close to each other. The duration from one New Moon to the next is called a <term>chandra-masa</term> <note>lunar month</note>. Therefore, if one wishes to know how many lunar months occur in one Great Age, one must first determine how many times the Sun and Moon come into conjunction during that Great Age. 

To find this, it is sufficient to calculate the difference between the total <term>bhaganas</term> <note>orbital revolutions</note> of the Moon and the Sun within the Great Age. This concept can be easily understood: if two boys start running around a circular field, and one boy completes 5 laps in an hour while the other completes 3, and both start from the same place at the same time, they will meet each other $5 - 3 = 2$ times during that hour. 

The movement of the hour and minute hands of a clock provides a very appropriate example. At twelve o'clock, both hands are together—that is, they are in conjunction. After this, they both begin to rotate. They meet for the first time at $1$ hour and $5 \frac{5}{11}$ minutes. They meet a second time at $2$ hours and $10 \frac{10}{11}$ minutes; a third time at $3$ hours and $16 \frac{4}{11}$ minutes; a fourth time at $4$ hours and $21 \frac{9}{11}$ minutes; a fifth time at $5$ hours and $27 \frac{3}{11}$ minutes; a sixth time at $6$ hours and $32 \frac{8}{11}$ minutes; a seventh time at $7$ hours and $38 \frac{2}{11}$ minutes; an eighth time at $8$ hours and $43 \frac{7}{11}$ minutes; a ninth time at $9$ hours and $49 \frac{1}{11}$ minutes; a tenth time at $10$ hours and $54 \frac{6}{11}$ minutes; and the eleventh time exactly at twelve o'clock. For these eleven conjunctions to occur, the minute hand had to complete 12 revolutions and the hour hand had to complete 1 revolution. Therefore, the number of conjunctions is the difference between their revolutions ($12 - 1 = 11$).

In the same way, the number of lunar months in a Great Age is:

$= \text{Lunar Revolutions in a Great Age} — \text{Solar Revolutions in a Great Age}$
$= 57,753,336 — 4,320,000$
$= 53,433,336$

**Intercalary Months —** The counting of months is based on the lunar month, while the counting of years is based on the solar year. In one solar year, there are 12 solar months and approximately 365.25875 mean <term>savana</term> days <note>civil days measured from sunrise to sunrise</note>. However, 12 lunar months consist of only 354.36705 mean civil days. Therefore—

<note>original Sanskrit terms: Madhyamadhikara (Chapter on Mean Motions), Mahayuga (Great Age), Chandra Masa (Lunar Month), Saura Masa (Solar Month), Adhimasa (Intercalary Month), Vigyan Bhashya (Scientific Commentary), Amavasya (New Moon), Yuti (Conjunction), Bhagana (Revolution), Savana Dina (Civil Day), Chandra-Bhagana (Lunar Revolutions), Surya-Bhagana (Solar Revolutions)</note>

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the number of lunar months and intercalary months (adhimasa) in a Great Age by finding the difference between solar and lunar revolutions, using a clock-hand analogy for clarity.</summary>
<keywords>Mahayuga, Lunar Month, Solar Month, Adhimasa, Intercalary Month, Conjunction, Amavasya, Indian Astronomy, Time Calculation</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>Continuity note: The previous section discussed the calculation of lunar months within a Great Age (Mahayuga) based on the relative motions of the Sun and Moon. This page transitions into the calculation of intercalary months (adhimasas) and omitted lunar days (tithi-kshayas) necessary to synchronize the lunar and solar calendars.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

A year of 12 lunar months is shorter than a solar year by 10.88970 mean civil days <term>savana dina</term> <note>a day measured from one sunrise to the next</note>; therefore, in approximately thirty-three months, this difference becomes equal to one lunar month. In the solar year where this difference equals one lunar month, that solar year contains 13 lunar months. Then, one lunar month is set aside under the name <term>adhimasa</term> <note>an intercalary or "extra" month</note> or <term>malamasa</term> <note>literally "impure month," during which certain religious ceremonies are traditionally avoided</note>. If this were not done, the festivals and observances celebrated according to the lunar month would begin to fall in different seasons, much like Islamic festivals <note>the Islamic calendar is purely lunar and does not use leap months, so its months rotate through the solar seasons</note>. In the verse above, it is explained that the number of lunar months in a <term>mahayuga</term> <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note> that exceeds the number of solar months are the lunar months to be discarded as *adhimasas*. Therefore, the number of *adhimasas* in a Great Age is:

->= Lunar months in a Mahayuga — Solar months in a Mahayuga<-
->= 5,34,33,336 — (4,320,000 X 12)<-
->= 1,593,336<-

> By subtracting the number of civil days from the number of lunar days, the "omitted lunar days" are found. The time from one sunrise of the Sun to its next sunrise is called a terrestrial civil day. || 36 ||
> <note>original Sanskrit: सावनाहानि चान्द्रेभ्यो द्युभ्यः प्रोझ्य तिथिक्षयाः । उदयादुदयं भानोर्भूमिसावनवासराः ॥ ३६ ॥</note>

**Translation**—Subtracting the number of civil days <term>savana dina</term> in a Great Age from the number of lunar days <term>tithis</term> in that same period yields the count of those days which are "lost" <term>kshaya</term>, meaning they are not included in the calendar count. The time between one sunrise of the Sun and the next is called a terrestrial civil day <term>bhumi-savana dina</term>. ||36||

**Scientific Commentary**—There are 30 <term>tithis</term> <note>lunar days, defined as the time it takes for the longitudinal angle between the moon and sun to increase by 12 degrees</note> in one lunar month. Therefore, if the number of lunar months in a Great Age is multiplied by 30, one can find the total number of *tithis* in a Great Age. It has already been explained how many civil days occur in a Great Age, and since only one *tithi* is generally counted per civil day, the amount by which the number of *tithis* exceeds the number of civil days represents the *tithis* that are not counted in the daily calendar; hence, these are called <term>kshaya</term> <note>lost</note> or <term>avama</term> <note>omitted</note> *tithis*.

The explanation for the latter half of this verse has been given several times already. Here, it is only further specified that a civil day is also called a "terrestrial civil day" <term>bhumi-savana dina</term>.

> In a Great Age, the lunar days are 1,603,000,080. The intercalary months are 1,593,336. The omitted lunar days are 25,082,252. || 37-38 ||
> <note>original Sanskrit: वसुदच्वष्टारिद्रुरूपाङ् क सप्ताद्रितिथयो युगे । चान्द्राः खाष्टखव्योमखाग्निछर्तुनिंशाकराः ॥ ३७ ॥ षड्वह्नि त्रिहुताशाङ्कतिथयश्चाधिमासकाः । तिथिक्षया यमार्थिाश्विद्वयष्टव्योमशराश्विनः ॥ ३८ ॥</note>
> <note>The Sanskrit text uses the "Bhuta-sankhya" system where words represent numbers: e.g., 'Vasu' = 8, 'Datsu' = 32, 'Adri' = 7, etc. The values are read from right to left.</note>

<vocab>Surya Siddhanta (Ancient astronomical treatise), Chandra-masa (Lunar month), Saura varsha (Solar year), Adhimasa (Intercalary month), Malamasa (Impure/Extra month), Mahayuga (Great Age), Savana dina (Civil day), Savana-hani (Loss of civil days), Tithi-kshaya (Omitted lunar day), Bhumi-savana dina (Terrestrial civil day), Avama tithi (Omitted lunar day)</vocab>

->There are 51,840,000 solar months in a Great Age.<-
->The terrestrial civil days are calculated by subtracting the number of solar revolutions from the total number of sidereal risings. || 39 ||<-
<note>original Sanskrit verse 39: "khacatushka-samudrāṣṭa-kupañca ravimāsakāḥ | bhavanti bhodayā bhānubhaganairuṇitāḥ kahāḥ ||"</note>

**Translation—(37)** In one Great Age <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>a cycle of 4,320,000 years</note>, there are 1,577,917,828 terrestrial civil days <term>savana dina</term>; 1,603,000,080 lunar days <term>tithis</term>; **(38)** 1,593,336 intercalary months <term>adhimasa</term>; 25,082,252 omitted lunar days <term>kshaya tithi</term>; and **(39)** 51,840,000 solar months. By subtracting the number of solar revolutions <term>bhagana</term> from the total number of sidereal risings <term>nakshatra udaya</term>, the number of terrestrial civil days is determined. || 37–39 ||

**Scientific Commentary—** The numbers provided in these verses serve as examples for the calculations described in the preceding three verses.

The figures given above for a Great Age are not identical across all astronomical systems <term>Siddhantas</term>. Minor variations exist. The number of civil days in one Great Age, as found in different systems, can be easily understood from the following table:

| Astronomical System | Number of Civil Days |
|----------|----------|
| According to the Standard *Surya Siddhanta* | 1,577,917,828 |
| According to the *Surya Siddhanta* in the *Panchasiddhantika* | 1,577,917,800 |
| According to the *Aryabhatiya* | 1,577,917,500 |
| According to the *Brahmasphuta Siddhanta* and *Siddhanta Shiromani* | 1,577,916,450 |
| According to the *Mahasiddhanta* | 1,577,917,542 |

---

->Intercalary months, omitted lunar days, sidereal, lunar, and civil days;<-
->These, along with the planetary revolutions, when multiplied by one thousand, are the totals for a Kalpa. || 40 ||<-

**Translation—** The numbers previously stated for intercalary months, omitted lunar days <term>avama tithi</term>, sidereal days <term>nakshatra dina</term>, lunar days, and civil days, as well as the revolutions of the planets, should be multiplied by one thousand to obtain the totals for a Kalpa <note>a "Day of Brahma," consisting of 1,000 Great Ages</note>. || 40 ||

**Scientific Commentary—** One Kalpa consists of 1,000 Great Ages <term>Mahayugas</term>. Therefore, the totals for revolutions and day-counts in a Kalpa are found by multiplying the Great Age figures by 1,000.

---

->For the direct motion of the Sun's apogee in a Kalpa, the count is 387;<-
->For Mars, it is 204; for Mercury, it is 368;<-
->For Jupiter, it is 900; for Venus, it is 535;<-
->For Saturn's apogee, it is 39. Now, for the nodes in reverse motion... || 41–42 ||<-
<note>These verses use a word-numeral system: "saptashtavahnyah" (7, 8, 3) for 387; "vedalvayama" (4, 0, 2) for 204, etc. In ancient Sanskrit astronomy, apogee refers to the point where a planet is furthest from Earth, moving at its slowest.</note>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

->The apogees of Mars are 204; of Mercury, 368;<-
->of Jupiter, 900; and of Venus, 535. || 43 ||<-
<note>original Sanskrit verse 43: "manudastrāstu kaujasya bodhasyāṣṭāṣṭasāgarāḥ | kṛtādricaṃdrā jaivasya trikhāṅkāśca bhṛgostathā ||"</note>
->The revolutions of Saturn’s nodes in a Kalpa are 662;<-
->the revolutions of the Moon’s apogee and nodes were mentioned previously. || 44 ||<-
<note>original Sanskrit verse 44: "śanipātasya bhagaṇāḥ kalpe yamarasaṛttavaḥ | bhagaṇāḥ pūrvemevātra proktāḥ candroccapātayoḥ ||"</note>

### Translation—
(41) Moving eastward in one <term>Kalpa</term> <note>a cosmic cycle of 4,320,000,000 years</note>, the Sun's apogee <term>mandocca</term> <note>the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> completes 387 revolutions <term>bhagana</term>, Mars's apogee completes 204 revolutions, and Mercury's apogee completes 368 revolutions. (42) Jupiter's apogee completes 900 revolutions, Venus's apogee 535 revolutions, and Saturn's apogee 39 revolutions. The motion of the nodes <term>pata</term> <note>the points where a planet's orbit intersects the ecliptic</note> is toward the west. In one Kalpa, Mars's node completes 214 revolutions, Mercury's node 488 revolutions, Jupiter's node 174 revolutions, Venus's node 903 revolutions, and (44) Saturn's node 662 revolutions. The number of revolutions for the Moon's apogee and nodes was already stated previously (in verse 33).

### Scientific Commentary—
The movement of planetary apogees and nodes is extremely subtle. None of them complete even one full revolution in a single Great Age <term>Mahayuga</term>; therefore, their counts are provided according to the duration of a Kalpa.

The definitions of "apogee" and "node" were discussed alongside the explanation of the Moon's movements. There is no explicit historical record of how these specific numbers were determined; however, there is no doubt that this data was gathered through observations spanning very long periods of time. Some Western scholars suggest that Indian astronomers may have acquired this knowledge from the Greeks, but this is a misconception, as will be evident from the tables provided later. It is necessary to mention here that these revolution counts differ significantly from the figures determined by modern precision instruments.

In modern astronomical texts, the annual motion of these apogees and nodes is usually provided. Therefore, one can compare the values by calculating the annual motion from the Kalpa-based revolutions of the *Surya Siddhanta*. The annual motion is calculated as follows: the total number of revolutions in a Kalpa is divided by the total number of solar years in a Kalpa, resulting in a fraction of a revolution. Multiplying this by 360 gives the measurement in degrees; multiplying the degrees by 60 gives the minutes <term>kala</term>; and multiplying the minutes by 60 gives the seconds <term>vikala</term>. For example, if the Sun's apogee completes 387 revolutions in one Kalpa, its motion in one year would be:

->387 X 360 X 60 X 60 / 4,320,000,000 seconds, which is 1161 / 10,000 seconds, or .1161<-
seconds.

It also provides a mathematical method for converting these vast cosmic numbers into annual rates of motion for comparison with modern astronomy.</summary>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>
<sig>3</sig>

In this way, the annual motion of the apogees <term>mandocca</term> <note>the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the center of motion</note> and nodes <term>pata</term> <note>the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> of other planets can be determined in seconds of arc <term>vikala</term>.

In this table, the figures preceded by a plus sign (+) indicate that the motion is toward the east, while those preceded by a minus sign (-) indicate that the motion is toward the west.

The figures provided in the second column are calculated based on "Tropical Aries" <term>Sayana-Mesha</term>; that is, they show the annual change in the distance of the planetary apogees and nodes from the Vernal Equinox <term>Vasanta-Sampata</term>.

However, Tropical Aries is mobile. It shifts westward by 50.26 seconds of arc every year. Therefore, if one wishes to find the annual distance from "Sidereal Aries" <term>Nirayana-Mesha</term> (which is fixed relative to the stars), 50.26 seconds must be subtracted from the figures in the second column. The resulting differences represent the annual motion of the apogees and nodes relative to Sidereal Aries. By performing this calculation, it is observed that the motion of Venus's apogee is toward the west; in other words, rather than moving eastward among the fixed stars, the apogee of Venus is shifting westward. In our traditional astronomical texts, the precession of the equinoxes <term>ayana-chalana</term> is assumed to be 60 seconds. This is because our traditional year-length is 8.5 seconds longer than the actual sidereal year <term>nakshatra-varsha</term>; consequently, if the annual motion of the Vernal Equinox is taken as 60 seconds and subtracted from the figures in the second column, the resulting values are those recorded in the fourth column. The figures in this column should be compared with the values provided in the *Surya Siddhanta*.

->Annual Motion of Apogees and Nodes<-

->According to modern precise* observations<-

| Planet | From Tropical Aries or Vernal Equinox | From actual Sidereal Aries | From Sidereal Aries (Our Siddhantas) | According to the Surya Siddhanta |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
| **Apogee** | **Seconds** | **Seconds** | **Seconds** | **Seconds** |
| Sun | $+61.5$ | $+11.24$ | $+1.5$ | $+0.1161$ |
| Mars | $+65.7$ | $+15.46$ | $+5.7$ | $+0.0612$ |

<note>*Taken from Loomis's Practical Astronomy</note>

Indian Astronomy, page 207

If the values from these Siddhantas <term>Siddhanta</term> <note>treatises on Indian astronomy</note> are compared with the values used by the Greeks, it will become evident how precisely our Siddhanta-authors performed their examinations (see the table on page 35).

By comparing the figures in the 4th and 7th columns <note>the 7th column likely appears on the following page mentioned</note>, one can see how close our Siddhanta-authors were to the actual positions and how far off Ptolemy <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note> was. The calculations performed by Keropant <note>Keru Lakshman Chhatre (1824–1884), a prominent Indian mathematician and astronomer who reconciled traditional and modern systems</note> are according to modern values; therefore, his calculations reveal the actual positions of the apogees <term>mandocca</term> and nodes <term>pata</term>. From this comparison, it is also clear that our Siddhanta-authors discovered all these revolution-values through independent experience and did not take them from the Greeks or other nations, as is the opinion of some Western scholars.

From that sum, subtract the time spent in the creation of the universe as mentioned previously (in verse 24). (47) What remains...

<meta>This page continues the chronological calculations of the Surya Siddhanta, moving from the broad tally of cosmic ages discussed on previous pages to the specific mathematical formulas used to determine the exact number of days elapsed since the creation of the world.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

This is the total number of (**current**) solar years until the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term> <note>the "Golden Age," the first of the four cyclic ages of the world</note>, which is 1,95,37,20,000. || 45-47 ||

**Scientific Commentary**—What was stated in the previous 22nd and 23rd verses is repeated here. In the scientific commentary of those verses, the method for determining the number of solar years from the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term> <note>a vast cosmic period or "Day of Brahma"</note> to the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term> of the current <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>a "Great Age" comprising the four smaller ages: Satya, Treta, Dvapara, and Kali</note> was explained, totaling 1,97,07,84,000. If the 1,70,64,000 solar years spent by the Creator in the process of creation are subtracted from this, the remainder is 1,95,37,20,000. These are the solar years that have passed from the dawn of creation to the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term>.

->From that point forward, add the elapsed years of the current time.<-
->Convert them into months and add the months passed since the bright half of <term>Chaitra</term>. || 48 ||<- <note>original: "Ata urdhvamabhi . yukta gatakalabdasankhya / Masikrta yuta mairmadhu sukladibhirgataih"</note>
->Place that sum in two places; multiply one by the <term>Adhimasas</term> of a <term>Mahayuga</term> and divide by the solar months.<-
->Add the resulting <term>Adhimasas</term> to the months, convert to days, and add the elapsed lunar days. || 49 ||<- <note>original: "Prthaksthaste’dhimasaghna suryamasavibhajitah / Labdhadhimasakaiyukta dinikrtya dinanvitah"</note>
->Place that in two places; multiply by the <term>Kshaya-tithis</term> and divide by the total lunar days.<-
->Subtract the resulting omitted days to find the civil days at midnight in <term>Lanka</term>. || 50 ||<- <note>original: "Dvisthastithiksaya-bhyastashchandravasarabhajitah / Labdhonaratri rahita lankayamardharatrikah"</note>

**Translation**—(**48**) To the solar years mentioned above (from the beginning of creation to the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term>), add the number of solar years that have passed since the end of that <term>Satyayuga</term>. The sum will be the total solar years up to the <term>Ishtakala</term> <note>the "desired time" or the specific moment for which one is calculating</note>. Convert this into months—that is, multiply by 12. To this number of months, add the number of lunar months that have passed from the first day of the bright half of the month of <term>Chaitra</term> <note>the start of the lunar year</note> up to the <term>Ishtakala</term>.

(**49**) Place this number in two separate positions. Multiply one by the number of <term>Adhimasas</term> <note>intercalary or "extra" months used to align the lunar and solar calendars</note> in a <term>Mahayuga</term>, and divide the product by the number of solar months in a <term>Mahayuga</term>. The result is the number of intercalary months that have passed from the beginning of creation to the <term>Ishtakala</term>. Add this result to the number of months stored in the second position. The sum is the total number of <term>lunar months</term> from creation to the <term>Ishtakala</term>. Multiply this by 30 to convert it into lunar days or <term>Tithis</term>, and then add the number of lunar days that have passed in the current month up to the <term>Ishtakala</term>. This gives the total <term>Tithis</term> elapsed since creation.

(**50**) Place this total number of <term>Tithis</term> in two positions. Multiply one by the number of <term>Kshaya-tithis</term> <note>omitted or "lost" lunar days, which occur because a lunar day is slightly shorter than a solar day</note> in a <term>Mahayuga</term>, and divide the product by the total number of <term>Tithis</term> in a <term>Mahayuga</term>. The result is the number of omitted days from creation to the <term>Ishtakala</term>. Subtract this result from the total <term>Tithis</term> stored in the second position. The remainder (minus one) is the number of <term>Savana-days</term> <note>civil days, measured from sunrise to sunrise</note> elapsed up to <term>midnight</term> at <term>Lanka</term> <note>the hypothetical city on the equator used as the prime meridian in ancient Indian astronomy</note>. || 48-50 ||

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedure for calculating the "Ahargana" or the total number of civil days elapsed from the beginning of creation to a specific target date. It explains how to reconcile solar, lunar, and civil time using intercalary months and omitted lunar days.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the Surya Siddhanta, transitioning from the general lengths of cosmic ages to a specific mathematical demonstration. It provides the "scientific commentary" and a practical example for calculating the total number of civil days elapsed since the beginning of creation (Ahargana).</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

**Scientific Commentary**—When it is necessary to determine the positions of the planets at a specific desired time, one must first determine how many <term>Savana</term> <note>civil or terrestrial days, measured from sunrise to sunrise</note> days have passed from the beginning of creation until that desired time. Once the number of civil days is known, it becomes easy to determine the positions of the planets using the <term>Trairashika</term> <note>the "Rule of Three" or mathematical proportion</note>. This is because at the beginning of creation, all planets were in the same position, and the number of <term>Bhaganas</term> <note>full revolutions through the zodiac</note> they complete in a <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note>, as well as the number of civil days in that period, has already been stated. Therefore, by knowing that a certain number of revolutions occur in the total civil days of a <term>Mahayuga</term>, one can calculate how many revolutions will have occurred in the civil days elapsed until the desired time; this calculation provides the planet's position. The method for calculating the number of civil days until a desired time, which is called the <term>Ahargana</term> <note>literally "heap of days"; the total count of days from an epoch</note>, has been explained in the three preceding verses.

**Example**—How many days have passed from the beginning of creation until <term>Vasant Panchami</term> <note>the spring festival occurring on the 5th day of the bright half of the month of Magha</note> in the year 1676 of the <term>Vikram</term> era?

From the beginning of creation to the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term> = 1,95,37,20,000 solar years
To the end of the <term>Treta</term> age = 12,96,000 solar years
To the end of the <term>Dvapara</term> age = 8,64,000 solar years
Until the start of the 1st day of the bright half of <term>Chaitra</term> in 1676 <term>Vikram Samvat</term> = 5,023$^*$ solar years
Therefore, from the beginning of creation until the start of
the 1st day of the bright half of <term>Chaitra</term> in 1676 <term>Vikram Samvat</term> = 1,95,58,85,023 years
= 23,47,06,20,276 solar months$^*$
From the 1st of the bright half of <term>Chaitra</term> to the start of the 1st of the bright half of <term>Magha</term> = 10 lunar months
Therefore, from the beginning of creation
until the 1st of the bright half of <term>Magha</term> 1676 = 23,47,06,20,286 mean months
Since there are 5,18,40,000 solar months in a <term>Mahayuga</term>, there are 15,93,336 <term>Adhimasas</term> <note>intercalary or "extra" months added to align the lunar and solar calendars</note>; therefore, the number of intercalary months in 23,47,06,20,286 mean months is:

$\frac{23,47,06,20,286 \times 15,93,336}{5,18,40,000} = 72,13,84,726$ <term>Adhimasas</term>

Therefore, from the beginning of creation until the
1st of the bright half of <term>Magha</term> 1676 <term>Vikram Samvat</term>, there have been 24,19,20,05,012 lunar months
= 7,25,76,01,50,360 <term>Tithis</term> <note>lunar days</note>
Until the 5th day of the bright half of <term>Magha</term>, there were 7,25,76,01,50,365 <term>Tithis</term>

---

$^*$ These will be completed at the time the sun enters Aries (<term>Mesha-Sankranti</term>) in the year 1676 <term>Vikram Samvat</term>. Therefore, these should be called mean solar years or months.

<summary>This page explains the "Rule of Three" method for finding planetary positions and provides a step-by-step mathematical example calculating the total lunar days (Tithis) elapsed since creation up to the year 1676 of the Vikram era.</summary>
<keywords>Ahargana, Savana days, Trairashika, planetary revolutions, solar years, lunar months, Adhimasa, Tithis, Vikram Samvat, Surya Siddhanta</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical demonstration of the Ahargana calculation started on page 37. It moves from the theoretical framework to a concrete numerical example involving a massive scale of time since the beginning of creation, before suggesting more practical modern methods for calculation.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

However, in one <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note> there are 53,433,336 <term>Chandramasa</term> <note>lunar months</note> and 2,50,82,252 <term>Kshaya Tithis</term> <note>omitted lunar days; these occur because a lunar day is slightly shorter than a solar day</note>. Therefore, in 24,16,20,05,012 lunar months, the 

Number of <term>Kshaya Tithis</term> = 
(24,16,20,05,012 × 2,50,82,252) / 53,433,336 = 11,35,60,18,761

∴ Up to the 5th day of the bright half of the month of Magha, from the beginning of creation:
->Number of <term>Savana</term> days = 7,14,40,41,31,604<-

The <term>Ahargana</term> <note>the total count of days</note> up to the midnight 
->before Magha Sudi 5 = 7,14,40,41,31,603<-

This rule for determining the number of <term>Savana</term> days <note>civil days from sunrise to sunrise</note> up to a certain point in time is very painstaking, and even a slight error can make hours of labor go to waste. Therefore, in practical application, such massive spans of time are not calculated this way; instead, <term>Karana Granthas</term> <note>concise astronomical manuals used for daily calculations</note> and tables have been created through which these calculations can be done easily. Further along in this book, instructions are also given to calculate the <term>Ahargana</term> from the end of the <term>Satyayuga</term> <note>the first and longest of the four ages, simplifying the math by using a more recent epoch</note>. If one wishes to perform an independent calculation in a simpler manner, the method described below can be utilized:

It has already been stated that in one <term>Mahayuga</term>, there are 43,20,000 <term>Saura Varsha</term> <note>solar years</note> and 1,57,79,17,828 <term>Savana</term> days.

Therefore, in one solar year, there are:
(1,57,79,17,828 / 43,20,000) <term>Savana</term> days

This means there are 365.2587564815 <term>Savana</term> days in a year. To find the <term>Ahargana</term> for a desired time, first calculate the <term>Ahargana</term> for the day of the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> <note>the moment the Sun enters the sign of Aries, marking the solar new year</note> of that particular year. To do this, multiply the number of <term>Savana</term> days in one year by the total number of solar years passed from the beginning of creation until the desired year. In the aforementioned example, 1,95,58,85,023 solar years had passed from the beginning of creation until the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> of the year 1876 of the <term>Vikram Samvat</term> <note>an era in the Indian calendar started in 57 BCE</note>; therefore, by the time of the mean <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> of this year, 365.2587564815 × 1,95,58,85,023 <term>Savana</term> days—that is, 7,14,40,41,31,321.7700260745 <term>Savana</term> days—had passed. However, the true <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> occurs 2.1706644 <term>Savana</term> days before the mean <term>Sankranti</term>. Therefore, if 2.1706644 <term>Savana</term> days are subtracted from the <term>Ahargana</term> of the mean <term>Mesha Sankranti</term>, then by the time of the true <term>Mesha Sankranti</term>, 7,14,40,41,31,319.5963...

***

<note>original: "पुस्तक में चन्द्र मासों की जगह तिथियां कही गयी हैं..." In the original text, <term>tithis</term> (lunar days) are mentioned instead of lunar months, which would make the calculation more precise; however, since that requires much more multiplication and division, lunar months have been used here instead. Because of this, it is possible for a one-day error to occur, which can be corrected by determining the <term>vara</term> (the day of the week).</note>

...316,755 <term>Savana</term> <note>civil days from sunrise to sunrise</note> days passed. This simply means that after this many mean <term>Savana</term> days had passed from the beginning of creation, the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> <note>the Sun’s entry into the sign of Aries, marking the solar new year</note> of the year 1976 <term>Vikrami Samvat</term> <note>a traditional Hindu calendar era starting in 57 BCE</note> occurred at Lanka <note>the theoretical point on the equator used as the prime meridian in ancient Indian astronomy</note>.

Therefore, the <term>Ahargana</term> <note>the total number of days elapsed from a fixed epoch</note> until the midnight of the day when the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> occurred was 7,14,40,41,31,320. Now we should determine how many days after this <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> the festival of <term>Basant Panchami</term> <note>the fifth day of the bright half of the lunar month of Magha</note> fell.

To do this, we must first know which <term>Tithi</term> <note>lunar day</note> fell on the day of the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term>.

One lunar month consists of 29.5305884607 <term>Savana</term> days. Therefore, if the <term>Ahargana</term> of the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> is divided by this number of <term>Savana</term> days, the resulting quotient will be the number of lunar months passed from the beginning of creation until the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term>, and the remainder will be the <term>Savana</term> days of the current lunar month. If this remainder is multiplied by 30 and the product is again divided by the number of <term>Savana</term> days in a lunar month, the resulting quotient will be the number of <term>Tithis</term>.

By doing this, the <term>Tithi</term> count at the time of the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> comes to 16.52576. According to the <term>Purnimanta</term> <note>month-ending with the full moon</note> calculation, this is the 2nd day of the dark fortnight of Vaisakha, and by the <term>Amanta</term> <note>month-ending with the new moon</note> calculation, it is the 2nd day of the dark fortnight of Chaitra.

Now we must determine how many <term>Savana</term> days passed from the 2nd day of the dark fortnight of Vaisakha to the 5th day of the bright fortnight of Magha. To find this, we first see how many <term>Tithis</term> passed in this interval. From the 3rd of the dark fortnight of Vaisakha to the 2nd of the dark fortnight of Magha, there are 6 lunar months (as there was no <term>Malamasa</term> <note>intercalary or leap month</note> in this year), and from the 3rd of the dark fortnight of Magha to the beginning of the 5th of the bright fortnight of Magha—that is, until the end of the 4th—there are 17 <term>Tithis</term>. Therefore, from the <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> to the end of the 4th of the bright fortnight of Magha, 6 $\times$ 30 $+$ 17 <term>Tithis</term>, which is 297 <term>Tithis</term>, passed.

<note>Note: The calculation 6 × 30 + 17 equals 197, but the text consistently uses 297 in subsequent lines and calculations.</note>

However, 30 <term>Tithis</term> $=$ 29.5305884607 mean <term>Savana</term> days; therefore 297 <term>Tithis</term>
$= 297 \times 29.5305884607 \times \frac{1}{30}$
$= 292.506261350736$
$= 292.51$ <term>Savana</term> days, approximately.

But the <term>Ahargana</term> for the midnight of <term>Mesha Sankranti</term> $= 7,14,40,41,31,320$
Therefore, the <term>Ahargana</term> from creation to the 5th of the bright fortnight of Magha $=$
->7,14,40,41,31,602.51<-

This means that by the first midnight of the 5th of the bright fortnight of Magha, 7,14,40,41,31,603 <term>Savana</term> days had passed, which matches the <term>Ahargana</term> calculated by the first method. <meta>This page continues the mathematical verification of the Ahargana (total day count). While the previous page calculated the days elapsed until a specific historical date (the Mesha Sankranti of 1976 Vikrami), this page explains how to verify the accuracy of such large numbers by determining the day of the week and the planetary rulers of months and years.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

The number of solar years [since the start of creation] is in the billions. If one needs to calculate the <term>Ahargana</term> <note>the total number of civil days elapsed since a fixed epoch</note> from the beginning of the <term>Treta</term> or <term>Kali Yuga</term> <note>two of the four great ages in Hindu cosmology</note>—instructions for which are provided later—it will be sufficient to calculate the number of <term>Savana</term> <note>civil days, measured from sunrise to sunrise</note> days in a lunar month and a solar year to seven decimal places.

It now remains to be tested whether this number is accurate or not. To do this, it is sufficient to check which weekday begins after this many days have passed since the beginning of creation. If the calculated weekday matches the actual day, or if there is a difference of only one day, the <term>Ahargana</term> should be considered correct; otherwise, it is incorrect. The method for this is given in the following verses:

> The <term>Ahargana</term> of <term>Savana</term> days is counted from Sunday; from that, the Lords of the Day, Month, and Year are determined. The remainder after dividing by seven is the Lord of the Day, counting from the Sun (Sunday). **(51)**
>
> If the number of civil days is divided by the number of days in a month and a year respectively, and the quotients are multiplied by two and three, then increased by one and divided by seven—the remainders indicate the Lord of the Month and the Lord of the Year, counting from the Sun. **(52)**

**Translation—(51)** From the total count of <term>Savana</term> days, the Lord of the Day, the Lord of the Month, and the Lord of the Year should be known by counting from the Sun. Divide this total count by 7; the remainder indicates the Lord of the Day, beginning the sequence of weekdays with the Sun (Sunday). **(52)** If this total count of <term>Savana</term> days is divided by the number of days in a month (30) and a year (360) respectively, and the resulting quotients are multiplied by two and three respectively, and 1 is added to each product, and these sums are then divided by 7—the remainders identify the Lord of the Month and the Lord of the Year, counting from the Sun in the sequence of weekdays. **(51-52)**

**Scientific Commentary—The Weekday (Day)** is named after the planet that is considered the lord of the first hour (**Hora**) at the start of that day. The planet that rules the first hour is also considered the ruler of that entire day. Similarly, the weekday on which a <term>Savana</term> month begins determines the "Lord of the Month," and the weekday on which a <term>Savana</term> year begins determines the "Lord of the Year." For example, the ruler of the first hour of Sunday is the Sun (*Ravi*); therefore, the ruler of that day is the Sun. A <term>Savana</term> month starting on a Sunday has the Sun as its ruler, and a <term>Savana</term> year starting on a Sunday also has the Sun as its ruler.

---

<note>One should not confuse *Savana* with the lunar month of *Shravana*. A month consisting of 30 *Savana* (civil) days is called a *Savana* month, and a year consisting of 12 such *Savana* months (360 civil days) is called a *Savana* year.</note>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Lords of the Day, transitioning from the mathematical calculation of the day-count (Ahargana) to the cosmological and astronomical explanation of why the days of the week follow their specific order based on the "Hora" (hour) system.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

To understand which planet is the lord of which hour (<term>Horā</term>), one must understand the sequence in which the lords of the hours change. The planet Saturn is the most distant of all planets from the Earth; closer than that is Jupiter; closer than Jupiter is Mars; closer than Mars is the Sun; closer than the Sun is Venus; closer than Venus is Mercury <insert>1</insert>; and closer than Mercury is the Moon. The lords of the <term>Horā</term> <note>an hour; a 24th part of a day</note> change in this specific order. If the lord of the first hour is Saturn, then the lord of the second hour is Jupiter, the third is Mars, the fourth is the Sun, the fifth is Venus, the sixth is Mercury, the seventh is the Moon, the eighth is Saturn again, and so on, following the same sequence. However, the day on which the lord of the first hour is Saturn must be named Saturday. Therefore, the lord of the second hour of Saturday is Jupiter, the third is Mars, and so on. In this way, after every seven hours, the same sequence of lords begins again. Consequently, the lord of the 22nd hour of Saturday is Saturn, the 23rd is Jupiter, the 24th is Mars, and the lord of the hour following the 24th must be the Sun. But this 25th hour is actually the first hour of the next day, and since its lord is the Sun, Saturday is followed by Sunday. Moving through the sequence for Sunday, the lord of the second hour is Venus, the third is Mercury, the fourth is the Moon, and continuing in this order, the lord of the 11th, 18th, and 25th hours is also the Moon. Since the 25th hour is the first hour of the following day, that day was named Monday <note>literally "Moon-day" or Chandravār</note> after the lord of that hour. The naming of the other days of the week <insert>2</insert> occurred in this same manner.

It has now become clear why Sunday follows Saturday, Monday follows Sunday, and Tuesday follows Monday. In the sequence of planets, the Sun is the fourth planet from Saturn, the Moon is the fourth planet from the Sun, and Mars is the fourth planet from the Moon. Therefore, the rule has been established that by counting from Saturn and taking every fourth planet in the sequence, one finds the lord of the subsequent day.

---

<margin>1. Mercury is actually further from the Earth than Venus, but in our astronomical texts, Venus is considered further. The reason for this is that they believed the further a planet is, the longer it takes to complete a <term>Bhagana</term> <note>a full revolution through the zodiac</note>. Our astronomers held this belief, but they calculated this distance from the Earth, whereas modern astronomers calculate it from the Sun.</margin>

<margin>2. This sequence of the days of the week is found in almost all countries. However, whether the derivation <note>original: upapatti</note> for their naming is the same elsewhere as it is explained here is a matter worthy of research.</margin>

It clarifies that the traditional planetary order (by distance from Earth) dictates that every 25th hour—and thus every subsequent day—is ruled by the fourth planet in the descending sequence.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the explanation of how planets rule over specific hours (Horā) and the naming of the seven-day week, this page expands the concept of planetary lordship to larger divisions of time—the civil month and the civil year.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

**Lord of the Month**—If the first day of a <term>Sāvana</term> month <note>The civil or "terrestrial" month in Vedic astronomy, consisting of exactly 30 solar days</note> is Sunday, then the first day of the next civil month will be the 31st day starting from that Sunday, because a civil month consists of 30 days. However, the 31st day from Sunday is Tuesday (the third day of the fifth week). Therefore, the lord of the second civil month is the planet Mars. The first day of the third civil month is the 31st day from Tuesday—meaning, starting from Tuesday and counting to the third day of the fifth week, we arrive at Thursday. Therefore, the lord of the third civil month is Jupiter. In the same way, the lord of the fourth civil month is Saturn, the ruler of Saturday (the third day from Thursday); the lord of the fifth civil month is the Moon, the ruler of Monday (the third day from Saturday); the lord of the sixth civil month is Mercury; and the lord of the seventh civil month is Venus. From the eighth civil month onward, this cycle repeats. Therefore, following the order of the days of the week, every third day designates the first day and the ruler of the subsequent civil month. Now, if observed carefully, it will be seen that the sequence of the Lords of the Month follows the planets in this order: Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Moon, Mercury, and Venus; then Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, and so on. If this cycle were to begin with the Moon, the order would match the sequence in which these planets are understood to be increasingly distant from the Earth.

**Lord of the Year**—The day on which a <term>Sāvana</term> year <note>A civil year consisting of 360 solar days (12 months of 30 days each)</note> begins is considered the lord of that year. If the first civil year begins on a Sunday, the second civil year will begin on the 361st day from Sunday. This is the fourth day of the week following 51 completed weeks—which is Wednesday. Therefore, the lord of the second civil year will be Mercury. The third civil year will begin on the 361st day after the start of the second civil year; therefore, it will be the fourth day from Wednesday, which is Saturday, ruled by Saturn. Thus, the lord of the third civil year is Saturn. In the same manner, the lord of the fourth civil year is Mars, the ruler of Tuesday (the fourth day from Saturday). The lord of the fifth civil year is Venus, the ruler of Friday (the fourth day from Tuesday). The lord of the sixth civil year is the Moon, the ruler of Monday (the fourth day from Friday); the lord of the seventh civil year is Jupiter, the ruler of Thursday (the fourth day from Monday); and the lord of the eighth civil year will again be the Sun, the ruler of Sunday (the fourth day from Thursday). Thus, the same cycle begins again from the eighth civil year. The sequence of these lords is as follows: Sun, Mercury, Saturn, Mars, Venus, Moon, Jupiter; then Sun, Mercury, Saturn, and so on. Therefore, if the sequence is aligned according to the days of the week, the upcoming civil year's...

<meta>Continuing from the explanation of how planetary lords are assigned to months based on the 30-day civil cycle, this page concludes that discussion and introduces the calculation for the Lord of the Year and the Lord of the Hour (Horā), citing the foundational verses from the Surya Siddhanta.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Mean Motions of Planets</header>

The first day [of a new year] will be the fourth day counting from the first day of the previous <term>Sāvana</term> year <note>A civil year in ancient Indian astronomy consisting of exactly 360 days</note>. And if the sequence of the planets is arranged, then starting from Saturn, every third planet in the series becomes the Lord of the Year. These principles are recorded in verse form in the 78th and 79th stanzas of the <term>Bhūgolādhyāya</term> <note>The "Chapter on Geography and Cosmography" within the Surya Siddhanta</note> as follows:

> In descending order from Saturn, every fourth planet is the Lord of the Day. Similarly, every third planet is proclaimed the Lord of the Year. <note>original Sanskrit: "Mandādadhaḥkrameṇa syuś-caturthā divasādhipāḥ | varṣādhipatayas-tadvat-tṛtīyāś-ca prakīrtitāḥ || 78 ||"</note>
> 
> In ascending order from the Moon, they are known as the Lords of the Months. The Lords of the Hours are designated in successive downward order starting from Saturn <note>referred to here as "the son of the Sun"</note>. <note>original Sanskrit: "Ūrdhvakrameṇa śaśino māsānāmadhipāḥ smृताः (smṛtāḥ) | horeśāḥ sūryatanayādadho'dhaḥ kramaśastathā || 79 ||"</note>

->Surya Siddhanta, Chapter on Geography<-

->Sequence according to the Days of the Week<-

This visualizes how the remainder of days in a month (30 days) or year (360 days) shifts the starting day of the next period.</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of concepts introduced on page 43, specifically applying the rules for identifying planetary rulers to the calculation of time since the beginning of creation.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

Having understood the rules regarding the names of the weekdays and their connection with the Lords of the Year and Month, the logic behind verses 51 and 52 can now be easily grasped.

To find the weekday at a desired time, take the <term>Ahargaṇa</term> <note>The total number of "civil" or solar days elapsed since a fixed starting point—in this case, the beginning of creation</note> and divide it by seven. The remainder represents the number of days that have passed in the week. Because creation began on a Sunday, Sunday is considered the first day of the week and Saturday the last (the seventh day). Therefore, if the remainder is 5, it should be understood as Thursday <note>the day of Jupiter or Vṛhaspati</note>, the day on which that Ahargaṇa count concludes at midnight. For example, in the previous calculation, the Ahargaṇa was 714,404,131,603; dividing this by seven leaves a remainder of 1. Thus, the day the Ahargaṇa was calculated for is Sunday, the first day of the week. However, since this count only reaches the midnight *before* <term>Basant-Panchami</term> <note>A traditional Hindu festival marking the onset of spring</note>, the day of Basant-Panchami itself would be Monday.

To identify the <term>Māsapati</term> <note>Lord of the Month</note>, one must divide the Ahargaṇa by 30. The resulting quotient represents the number of <term>Sāvana</term> <note>civil</note> months that have passed since the beginning of creation. Multiply this number of months by two, add 1, and then divide by seven. This formula is used because the "Lord of the Month" changes to the third day in the weekday sequence every month, and the cycle repeats every seven months. The remainder indicates the ruler of the weekday who serves as the lord of the current month. For example, dividing the Ahargaṇa mentioned above by 30 yields 23,813,471,053 civil months and 13 civil days. Multiplying the number of months by 2 and adding 1 results in 47,626,942,107. Dividing this by seven leaves a remainder of 3. Therefore, the first day of the current civil month was Tuesday, making Mars <note>Maṅgala</note> the Lord of this Month.

To identify the <term>Varṣapati</term> <note>Lord of the Year</note>, divide the Ahargaṇa by 360, or divide the previously calculated civil months by 12. The quotient represents the number of elapsed civil years. Multiply this number by three, add 1, and divide by seven. This is because the Lord of the Year changes to the fourth day in the weekday sequence every year, and the cycle resets after seven years. The remainder indicates the weekday ruler who is the lord of the current civil year, starting from Sunday.

In the example above, dividing the Ahargaṇa by 360 (or the months by 12) shows that the number of elapsed civil years is 1,684,455,621.

->**Vocabulary**<-
**Surya Siddhanta:** An ancient Indian treatise on astronomy.
**Ahargaṇa:** The total count of days elapsed from a specific epoch.
**Sāvana Day:** A civil day measured from sunrise to sunrise.
**Creation:** The beginning of the current cosmic cycle.
**Weekday:** The seven-day cycle of planetary rulers.
**Lord of the Year:** The planetary ruler of the first day of the civil year.
**Lord of the Month:** The planetary ruler of the first day of the civil month.
**Sunday, Saturday, Monday, Tuesday:** Days of the week associated with the Sun, Saturn, Moon, and Mars respectively.
**Jupiter:** The ruler of Thursday.
**Basant-Panchami:** A specific lunar calendar festival.
**Mars:** The ruler of Tuesday and the month in the example.
**Sāvana Month:** A civil month of exactly 30 days.
**Sāvana Year:** A civil year of exactly 360 days.

<header>The Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

Multiplying this by three and adding 1 results in 5,95,33,67,734. When this is divided by 7, the remainder is 1. Therefore, the current <term>Sāvana</term> <note>civil</note> year began on a Sunday, and the Lord of the Year for this period is the Sun.

->**Figure 6—The order of planets according to their distance from the Earth**<-

This concludes the method for determining the <term>Varṣapati</term> <note>Lord of the Year</note> according to the <term>Surya Siddhanta</term> <note>One of the foundational Sanskrit texts of Indian astronomy</note>. In almost all modern <term>Panchāngs</term> <note>traditional Hindu almanacs</note>, the Lord of the Year (<term>Varṣeśa</term>) is considered to be the ruler of the day on which <term>Chaitra Shukla Pratipadā</term> <note>the first day of the waxing moon in the month of Chaitra, marking the lunar New Year</note> falls. The "Minister" of the year is the ruler of the day on which <term>Mesha Sankrānti</term> <note>the Sun’s entry into the sign of Aries</note> occurs. The <term>Meghēśa</term> <note>Lord of the Clouds</note> is the ruler of the day when the Sun enters the <term>Ārdrā Nakshatra</term> <note>the lunar mansion associated with the star Betelgeuse</note>. Predictions for the entire year are derived based on these considerations. In the <term>Makaranda Sāriṇī</term> <note>A famous 15th-century astronomical table/manual</note>, the rules differ from the Surya Siddhanta as follows:

> Whatever weekday falls on <term>Chaitra Shukla Pratipadā</term>, that ruler is the King. Whatever weekday falls on <term>Mesha Sankrānti</term>, that ruler is the Minister. Whatever weekday falls on <term>Karka Sankrānti</term> <note>the Sun's entry into Cancer/Summer Solstice</note>, that ruler is the Lord of Truth. Whatever weekday falls on <term>Tulā Sankrānti</term> <note>the Sun's entry into Libra/Autumnal Equinox</note>, that ruler is the Lord of Flavors. Whatever weekday falls on <term>Mṛga Sankrānti</term> <note>the Sun's entry into Capricorn/Winter Solstice</note>, that ruler is the Lord of the Essence-less. The Sun's entry into <term>Ārdrā</term>... <note>original Sanskrit: "Chaitra śukla pratipaddivase yo vāraḥ sa rājā... Ārdrāpraveśa"</note>

<summary>This page explains the calculation to identify the planetary ruler of the year and compares the Surya Siddhanta’s mathematical method with the observational and transition-based rules used in modern almanacs and the Makaranda Sarini.</summary>
<keywords>Lord of the Year, Varṣapati, Surya Siddhanta, Makaranda Sarini, Mesha Sankranti, Chaitra Shukla Pratipada, planetary order, Hindu astronomy</keywords>

# Surya Siddhanta

The day [of the week] on which that occurs is the Lord of Aries. The day on which the Sun enters Sagittarius is the Lord of Western Grains.*

The practice of the <term>Sāvana</term> <note>civil or terrestrial</note> year and <term>Sāvana</term> month is no longer in use anywhere today. Therefore, I do not know what purpose is served by the rule given in the <term>Surya Siddhanta</term> for determining the <term>Varṣādhipa</term> <note>Lord of the Year</note> and <term>Māsādhipa</term> <note>Lord of the Month</note> according to these cycles. If any learned person knows, please inform me. From the thirteenth verse, as I have noted in my commentary on it, the implication is that the "true" year is actually the solar year. In that case, what is the necessity of calculating the Lord of the Year and Lord of the Month according to the civil year and civil month?

> Just as the number of days elapsed, multiplied by the revolutions of a specific planet and divided by the number of civil days in a Great Age, results in the mean position of that planet in revolutions and so on. ||53||
> 
> In the same way, the positions of the "Fast Anomalies" and "Slow Apogees," which have been described as moving eastward, are determined. The "Nodes," however, move in a reverse direction; therefore, their calculated signs, degrees, and minutes must be subtracted from a full circle of twelve signs to find their true positions. ||54||

**Translation—**(53) Take the total <term>Ahargaṇa</term> <note>the number of days elapsed from the beginning of creation to the desired date</note> and multiply it by the number of <term>Bhagaṇas</term> <note>revolutions</note> the planet completes in a <term>Mahāyuga</term> <note>a Great Age of 4,320,000 years</note>. Divide this product by the total number of <term>Sāvana</term> <note>civil</note> days in a Great Age. The resulting quotient indicates how many full revolutions that planet has completed (since the beginning of creation) according to its mean motion. Multiply the remainder by 12 and divide again (by the civil days of a Great Age) to find the number of <term>Rāśis</term> <note>zodiac signs</note> the planet has traversed in its current revolution. Multiply the new remainder by 30 and divide by the same divisor to find the number of degrees the planet has covered in its current sign, and so on. (54) In this same manner, the positions of the previously mentioned <term>Śīghrocca</term> <note>the "fast" anomaly or apex of motion</note> and <term>Mandocca</term> <note>the "slow" anomaly or apogee</note>, which move eastward, can be known. The motion of the <term>Pātas</term> <note>Nodes</note> is retrograde (toward the west); therefore, once you have calculated their signs, degrees, and minutes, you must subtract that value from a full circle—that is, from 12 signs. The remainder is the actual position of the Nodes. || 53-54 ||

**Scientific Commentary—**In these verses, the method for determining the mean positions of the planets at any desired time is explained. In short, it means: if a planet completes its stated revolutions in one <term>Mahāyuga</term> (the total civil days in a Great Age), then how many revolutions will it complete in the civil days elapsed up to the desired time? Therefore, using the <term>Trairāśika</term> <note>the Rule of Three or simple proportion</note> method, this rule can be expressed as follows:

->Civil days in a Great Age : Current Total Days (Ahargaṇa) :: Total Revolutions in a Great Age : Desired Revolutions<-

---

* <margin>Makaranda Sāriṇī, printed by Venkateshwar Press in 1960 Vikrami, page 476</margin>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical formulas for calculating a planet's mean position using the "Rule of Three" based on the total days elapsed since creation (Ahargaṇa). The author also questions the modern relevance of civil (Sāvana) time-reckoning as prescribed in the Surya Siddhanta.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical explanation of planetary positions from the previous page, specifically detailing how to derive signs, degrees, and minutes from the basic revolution formula and introducing the calculation for the Jovian year (Samvatsara) cycle.</meta>

# Chapter on Mean Motions
<page-num>47</page-num>

If 'S' is the number of civil days in a Great Age, 'A' is the number of elapsed days <note>original: "Ahargaṇa"</note>, 'B' is the number of revolutions in a Great Age, and 'b' is the desired planetary position in revolutions, it can be written concisely as:

->A × B<-
->b = —————<-
->S<-

This result is a fraction. When simplified, the whole number obtained represents the number of full <term>revolutions</term> <note>original: "Bhagaṇa"</note> the planet has completed up to that time. By multiplying the remaining fraction by 12 and simplifying, the resulting whole number gives the completed **Signs**. Then, by multiplying the remaining fraction by 30 and simplifying, one finds the **Degrees** of the current sign. If one wishes to know the **Minutes** and **Seconds** as well, one must continue the process by multiplying the remainders by 60.

This rule applies to all planets that move forward, as well as to the **Fast Anomalies** <note>original: "Śīghroccas"</note> and **Slow Apogees** <note>original: "Mandoccas"</note>. If one wishes to find the position of a planet's **Nodes** <note>original: "Pātas"</note>, the signs, degrees, minutes, and seconds calculated by the above method must be subtracted from 12 signs. This is because the motion of a node is retrograde <note>original: "ulṭī"</note>; therefore, it moves through the zodiac in reverse order. For example, if the calculation shows that a certain node has completed its revolutions and sits at 2 signs, 3 degrees, and 5 minutes, this must be counted backward from the starting point of Aries—meaning from the end points of Pisces, Aquarius, and Capricorn. Specifically, it would be 3 degrees and 5 minutes from the end of Capricorn, which is 26 degrees and 55 minutes of Capricorn. Therefore, if 2 signs, 3 degrees, and 5 minutes are subtracted from 12 signs, the result is 9 signs, 26 degrees, and 55 minutes. This means the node has passed through 9 signs of the zodiac and is at 26 degrees and 55 minutes of the tenth sign.

->Multiply the completed revolutions of Jupiter by twelve, add the<-
->number of the sign currently occupied, and divide the sum by sixty.<-
->The remainder indicates the year of the cycle, starting with Vijaya. ||55||<-

**Translation**—Multiply the completed revolutions of Jupiter by 12 and add the sequence number of the zodiac sign in which Jupiter is currently located. Divide this sum by 60; the remainder indicates the current <term>Samvatsara</term> <note>a year in the 60-year Jovian cycle</note> in the sequence starting from **Vijaya** || 55 ||.

**Scientific Commentary**—The time it takes for Jupiter, moving at its mean speed, to pass through one zodiac sign is called a **Samvatsara**. Therefore, in one full revolution of Jupiter (which takes 4332.3206 civil days), there are twelve Samvatsaras. One Samvatsara consists of 361.02672 civil days. From this, it is clear that a Samvatsara is 4.23203 civil days shorter than a **Solar Year** and 1.02672 civil days longer than a **Civil Year** <note>original: "Sāvana Year"</note>.

# Surya Siddhanta

In one cycle, there are 60 <term>Samvatsaras</term> <note>original: "Samvatsara" — a Jovian year, or one year in a 60-year lunar-solar cycle linked to the motion of Jupiter.</note> whose names in order are as follows:

| | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Vijaya | 21 | Pramadi | 41 | Shrimukha |
| 2 | Jaya | 22 | Ananda | 42 | Bhava |
| 3 | Manmatha | 23 | Rakshasa | 43 | Yuva |
| 4 | Durmukha | 24 | Anala (Nala) | 44 | Dhata |
| 5 | Hemalamba | 25 | Pingala | 45 | Ishvara |
| 6 | Vilamba | 26 | Kalayukta | 46 | Bahudhanya |
| 7 | Vikari | 27 | Siddhartha | 47 | Pramathi |
| 8 | Sharvari | 28 | Raudra | 48 | Vikrama |
| 9 | Plava | 29 | Durmati | 49 | Vrisha |
| 10 | Shubhakrita | 30 | Dundubhi | 50 | Chitrabhanu |
| 11 | Shobhana | 31 | Rudhirodgari | 51 | Subhanu |
| 12 | Krodhi | 32 | Raktaksha | 52 | Tarana |
| 13 | Vishvavasu | 33 | Krodhana | 53 | Parthiva |
| 14 | Parabhava | 34 | Kshaya | 54 | Vyaya |
| 15 | Plavanga | 35 | **Prabhava** | 55 | Sarvajit |
| 16 | Kilaka | 36 | Vibhava | 56 | Sarvadhari |
| 17 | Saumya | 37 | Shukla | 57 | Virodhi |
| 18 | Sadharana | 38 | Pramoda | 58 | Vikriti |
| 19 | Virodhakrit | 39 | Prajapati | 59 | Khara |
| 20 | Paridhavi | 40 | Angira | 60 | Nandana |

<term>Varahamihira</term> <note>A famous 6th-century Indian astronomer and polymath.</note> in the <term>Brihat Samhita</term> <note>original: "Vṛhatsaṃhitā" — Varahamihira's encyclopedic work on astrology and planetary movements.</note> <note>1</note> did not consider the <term>Samvatsara cycle</term> to begin with *Vijaya*, but rather with the 35th year, *Prabhava*. This practice remains prevalent today. It is necessary to investigate when this practice first began. Within the 60-year cycle, there are several smaller divisions that also begin with *Prabhava*. These will be discussed in the final chapter titled <term>Manadhyaya</term> <note>The "Chapter on Measures [of Time]".</note>.

> When the Preceptor of the Gods <note>Jupiter</note> reaches the first part of the asterism Dhanishtha and rises in the month of Magha, the first of the year-groups, named **Prabhava**, begins; that year is for the welfare of all beings. <note>original Sanskrit: "ādyaiṃ dhaniṣṭhāṃśamabhiprapanno māghe yadāyātyudayaṃ surejyaḥ | ṣaṣṭhaścabda pūrvaḥ prabhavaḥ sanāmnā pravarttate bhūtahitāstadabdaḥ ||"</note>

<header>Chapter on Mean Motions 49</header>
<sig>4</sig>

## Chapter on Mean Motions

When one wishes to know which <term>Jovian year</term> <note>original: "Samvatsara" — a year in the 60-year cycle linked to Jupiter's passage through the zodiac signs.</note> is active at a given time, they must first determine the mean position of <term>Jupiter</term> <note>original: "Brihaspati"</note> according to the 53rd verse. Calculating the mean position of a planet by finding the <term>total day-count</term> <note>original: "Ahargana" — the number of days elapsed since the beginning of the creation.</note> from the start of creation is very difficult. Therefore, it is more convenient to calculate the <term>total day-count</term> from the beginning of the <term>Kali Yuga</term> <note>The current "Dark Age" in Hindu cosmology, which began in 3102 BCE.</note>, as the <term>Vijaya</term> year of the cycle also began at that time. For those familiar with decimal fractions, it is even more convenient to use solar years instead of the total day-count. In this way, the mean position of Jupiter at the time of the <term>Aries Ingress</term> <note>original: "Mesha-Sankranti" — the moment the Sun enters the sign of Aries, marking the solar new year.</note> can be found. For example, suppose we want to know which Jovian year will be active at the time of the Aries Ingress in the year 1881 of the <term>Vikrama Era</term> <note>original: "Vikramiya" — a historical calendar era beginning in 57 BCE.</note>, and how long it will last?

From the start of the Kali Yuga to the Aries Ingress of 1881 Vikrama Samvat, 5,025 solar years will have passed. By determining how many <term>revolutions</term> <note>original: "Bhagana" — a full 360-degree orbit through the zodiac.</note> Jupiter has completed and which sign it resides in during that time, the Jovian year can be identified. In one <term>Great Age</term> <note>original: "Mahayuga" — a cycle of 4,320,000 years.</note>, Jupiter completes 364,220 revolutions; therefore, in 5,025 solar years:

->364,220 × 5,025<-
->= -------------------------- revolutions<-
->4,320,000<-

->364,220 × 5,025<-
->= -------------------------- × 12 Jovian years<-
->4,320,000<-

->= 5,084.80416666 Jovian years.<-

This means that at the time of the Aries Ingress in 1881 Vikrama Samvat, the 5,085th <term>Jovian year</term> is currently running. Of this year, 0.80416666 parts have already passed, and 0.19583334 parts remain.

Since one cycle consists of 60 <term>Jovian years</term>, dividing 5,084 by 60 gives a quotient of 84 and a remainder of 44. This indicates that 84 full cycles of 60 years have passed since the start of the Kali Yuga. In the 85th cycle, the 44th year, known as <term>Dhata</term>, is currently active. At the time of the Aries Ingress, 0.195833... of that year remains to be completed.

If one wishes to know how many days the <term>Dhata</term> year will last during the year 1881 Vikrama Samvat, this remainder should be multiplied by the total <term>civil days</term> <note>original: "Savana din" — a natural day from sunrise to sunrise.</note> in one Jovian year, which is 361.02672. By doing this, the product is 34.5884 civil days. Therefore, from the time of the mean Aries Ingress in 1881, 34.5884 civil days will pass...

<vocab>Jovian year, Jupiter, Day-count, Aries Ingress, Revolution, Great Age, Dhata (44th year), Civil day, Vikrama Era</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical process for calculating the current year in the 60-year Jovian cycle using Jupiter's mean motion, using 1881 Vikrama Samvat as a specific example. It demonstrates how to convert total solar years into Jovian years and identify the specific name of the year (Dhata) from the remainder of a 60-year division.</summary>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

Then the end of the year <term>Dhātā</term> <note>The 10th year in the 60-year Jovian cycle.</note> and the beginning of the year named <term>Īśvara</term> <note>The 11th year in the 60-year Jovian cycle.</note> will occur. It was stated previously that the true <term>Aries Ingress</term> <note>original: "Mesha-Sankranti" — the moment the Sun enters Aries.</note> occurs 2.1777 <term>civil days</term> <note>original: "Saavana dina" — a day measured from sunrise to sunrise.</note> before the mean Aries Ingress; therefore, 36.7661 civil days after the true Aries Ingress—or at 36 days, 46 <term>ghadi</term>, 8 <term>pala</term>, and 40 <term>vipala</term> <note>Units of time in ancient Indian astronomy: 1 day = 60 ghadi; 1 ghadi = 60 pala; 1 pala = 60 vipala.</note>—the entry into the year *Īśvara* will take place.

> This has been described in detail; for practical use, however, a summary [method]
> should be used to calculate the mean motion of the planets from a desired Age. ||56||
> At the end of this <term>Satya Yuga</term>, all planets in their mean motion—
> except for their nodes and apogees—were in alignment at the beginning of Aries. ||57||
> The Moon's apogee was at the start of Capricorn, and its node was at the start of Libra.
> Others [nodes and apogees] were at fractional parts because of their slow motion. ||58||

**Translation**—(56) The method for determining the mean positions of the planets has been described in detail thus far; however, for practical daily use, this calculation should be performed more concisely starting from a chosen <term>Yuga</term> <note>An "Age" or cosmic era.</note>. (57) At the end of this *Satya Yuga* <note>The first and most perfect of the four cosmic ages.</note>, with the exception of the nodes and the apogees, the mean positions of all planets were identical at the beginning of Aries; that is, all seven planets had reached the starting point of the zodiac simultaneously. (58) The Moon's <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "Mandoccha" — the point in an orbit farthest from the center of motion.</note> was at the beginning of the sign of Capricorn, and its <term>node</term> <note>original: "Paata" or "Rahu" — the intersection point of the orbit with the ecliptic.</note> was at the beginning of Libra. The nodes and apogees of the other planets, due to their extremely slow motion, were not at whole degree marks; therefore, no specific position is mentioned for them. (56–58).

**Scientific Commentary**—The method for calculating the mean positions of planets described in verses 45–50 of this chapter is not practical for daily use due to the extensive calculations required, as is clear from the examples provided. Therefore, there is no need to calculate the <term>total day-count</term> <note>original: "Ahargana".</note> from the very beginning of creation to the end of the *Satya Yuga*. Instead, one should calculate the day-count from the beginning of the current era to the desired time, according to the latter half of verse 48 and verses 49–50, to find the mean positions of the planets.

The moment the "Being of the Sun" <note>original: "Suryamsha Purusha" — the divine manifestation of the Sun who revealed this knowledge.</note> taught the *Surya Siddhanta* to <term>Maya</term> <note>The mythical astronomer-asura to whom the text was revealed.</note> was, according to the second verse of this chapter, at the end of the *Satya Yuga*. Therefore, verse 57 provides the cue to begin the day-count from the start of the <term>Treta Yuga</term> <note>The second cosmic age.</note> and shows that at this time, the mean positions of all planets were aligned at the beginning of the sign of Aries. Following this logic, for the <term>Kali Yuga</term>, the method of calculating the day-count from the beginning of the *Kali Yuga* itself is most convenient, and this is the practice currently in use. Just as the mean positions of all planets were aligned at the beginning of Aries at the start of the *Treta Yuga*, so too at the beginning of the *Kali Yuga*, at the start of the sign of Aries...

# Chapter on Mean Motions

...were equal; this is because from the beginning of the <term>Treta Yuga</term> to the beginning of the <term>Kali Yuga</term> is exactly half a <term>Mahayuga</term> <note>A Great Cycle of 4,320,000 years</note>, which is a duration in which all planets complete full revolutions. However, because the Moon’s <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "uchcha" — the point in an orbit farthest from Earth</note> completes an odd number of revolutions in a Mahayuga, it was at the beginning of Cancer rather than the beginning of Capricorn, though the <term>node</term> <note>original: "paat" — the point where an orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> remained at the beginning of Libra. This is the opinion of the *Surya Siddhanta*. According to <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> <gloss>1</gloss>, the mean positions of the planets (excluding the Sun and Moon) at the start of the Kali Yuga were as follows:

| | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | Moon’s Apogee | Rahu* |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Sign** | 11 | 11 | 11 | 11 | 11 | 4 | 5 |
| **Degree** | 26 | 27 | 26 | 28 | 28 | 5 | 3 |
| **Minute** | 3 | 24 | 27 | 42 | 46 | 26 | 12 |
| **Second** | 50 | 28 | 36 | 14 | 34 | 46 | 58 |

Up to this point, the method for determining the mean positions of planets at midnight for <term>Lanka</term> or <term>Ujjain</term> <note>Lanka (on the equator) and Ujjain were the primary reference points for the ancient Indian prime meridian</note> has been explained. In the following verses, the method for determining the mean positions of planets at midnight for countries to the east or west of Lanka will be described.

->The diameter of the Earth is twice eight hundred;<-
->The square root of ten times its square is the Earth's circumference. || 56 ||<-

**Translation**—The diameter of the Earth is twice 800, which is 1600 <term>yojanas</term> <note>An ancient unit of distance</note>; by multiplying the square of this by 10 and finding the square root of the product, the result is the Earth’s circumference. || 56 ||

**Scientific Commentary**—If the diameter of the Earth is taken as 'd', then its circumference = $\sqrt{d^2 \times 10}$ = d $\times \sqrt{10}$ = d $\times$ 3.1623. This proves that [the text considers] the circumference to be 3.1623 times the diameter. Nowadays, this relationship (the value of $\pi$) is considered accurate to four decimal places as 3.1416, which differs significantly from 3.1623. However, one should not conclude from this that the author of the *Surya Siddhanta* lacked an accurate understanding of the diameter and circumference—

---

1. *Siddhanta Shiromani*, Ganitadhyaya, pages 32-33; Calcutta edition, 1815 AD.
* This position of <term>Rahu</term> <note>The North Node of the Moon</note> is recorded in the Calcutta edition of the *Siddhanta Shiromani* and the edition by Mahamahopadhyaya Pandit Bapu Deva Shastri; however, according to my own calculations, it should be at 6 signs, 26 degrees, 47 minutes, and 24 seconds at the start of the Kali Yuga, which is obtained by subtracting the above position from 12 signs.

<meta>This page transitions from calculating the specific positions of the planets at the start of the Kali Yuga to the geometric principles used in those calculations, specifically focusing on the ratio between a circle's diameter and its circumference.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>

# Surya Siddhanta

The exact relationship was not known; because in the second chapter, the ratio of the radius to the circumference is assumed to be 3438 : 21600, which results in the circumference being 3.14136 times the diameter. Therefore, in this verse, the circumference is taken as $\sqrt{10}$ <note>approximately 3.162</note> times the diameter for the sake of convenience and to simplify mathematical operations. This is similar to how today, when working in a "gross" <note>approximate or rough</note> manner, some take the value as $\frac{22}{7}$ and others as 3.14, but where very "fine" <note>precise</note> calculations are required, it must be taken accurately to five or seven decimal places.

The value taken for this relationship in various <term>Siddhantas</term> <note>traditional Indian astronomical treatises</note> can be understood from the following excerpt:

| Source | Ratio | Details | Simplified Form |
|:---|:---|:---:|:---|

| From modern precise mathematics | | | 1 : 3.1415927 |

Bhaskaracharya and Aryabhata II have described the relationship between diameter and circumference in two ways: one "fine" <note>sukshma</note> and the other "gross" <note>sthula</term> and useful for practical application. Hereafter, the relationship between diameter and circumference will be indicated by the symbol $\pi$, as is the current custom; that is, if the diameter is 1, then the circumference is $\pi$, whereas the value of $\pi$ for practical [usage]...

### Chapter on Mean Motion

According to the requirement, values like $\frac{22}{7}$, 3.14, 3.142, 3.1416, and so on can be taken.

In this verse, the second word, ‘<term>yojana</term>’ <note>a traditional Indian measure of distance</note>, is of great importance. Nowadays, people generally understand a <term>yojana</term> to be four <term>kos</term> <note>a traditional unit of distance; one kos is roughly 2 miles, but varies by region</note>, but the value of the <term>kos</term> itself is not fixed. In some provinces, the <term>kos</term> is very small, and in others, it is very large. Similarly, the measurement of a <term>yojana</term> is also not fixed. This is the reason why in different <term>Siddhantas</term> <note>traditional astronomical treatises</note>, the values for the <term>Earth’s circumference</term> <note>original: "bhuparidhi"</note> or the <term>Earth’s diameter</term> <note>original: "bhuvyasa"</note> are given in different figures. The excerpts written below reveal what values are given for the <term>Earth’s diameter</term> in the various treatises:

| Text / Opinion | Value | Unit |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Earth's diameter according to the <title>Pancha-siddhantika</title> <gloss>1</gloss> | 1018$\frac{9}{10}$ | yojana |
| Aryabhata <gloss>2</gloss> and Lalla | 1050 | ,, |
| Current <title>Surya-siddhanta</title> | 1600 | ,, |
| <title>Siddhanta Shiromani</title> <gloss>3</gloss> | 1581$\frac{1}{24}$ | ,, |
| Second <title>Arya-siddhanta</title> (<title>Mahasiddhanta</title>) <gloss>4</gloss> | 2106 | ,, |
| **According to the modern European view** <gloss>5</gloss> | | |
| Equatorial | 7927 | miles |
| Polar | 7900 | ,, |

From the figures above, it is evident that the <term>yojanas</term> used by Varahamihira, Aryabhata, and Lalla are nearly the same, and the <term>yojanas</term> of the *Surya Siddhanta* and *Siddhanta Shiromani* are also nearly equal; however, the <term>yojana</term> used by the first three teachers is almost one and a half times the <term>yojana</term> used by the latter two. Therefore, these two types of <term>yojanas</term> will be compared with the modern <term>mile</term>. In our <term>Siddhantas</term>, the Earth is considered to be perfectly spherical, which is why this distinction <note>between equatorial and polar diameters</note> was not kept...

1. 2. <title>Aryabhatiya</title>, page 10, first section, 5th verse.
