<meta>This page continues the comparison of the Earth's dimensions as recorded in ancient Indian astronomical texts versus modern measurements, specifically focusing on defining the length of a 'yojana' and the methodology for measuring the Earth's circumference.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

It has been noted that the <term>equatorial circumference</term> <note>the distance around the Earth's middle</note> is different from the <term>polar circumference</term> <note>the distance around the Earth passing through the North and South Poles</note>. Therefore, for the purpose of comparison, it is appropriate to use the polar circumference, as the ancient teachers established the magnitude of the Earth's circumference using this measurement. Therefore:

| According to Aryabhata | According to the *Siddhanta Shiromani* |
| :--- | :--- |
| 1050 <term>yojanas</term> = 7900 miles | 1581 <term>yojanas</term> = 7900 miles |
| $\therefore$ 1 <term>yojana</term> = $\frac{7900}{1050}$ miles | $\therefore$ 1 <term>yojana</term> = $\frac{7900}{1581}$ miles |
| = 7.52 miles | = 5 miles |
| If there are four <term>kos</term> in 1 <term>yojana</term>, then: | $\therefore$ 1 <term>kos</term> = $\frac{5}{4}$ miles = 1$\frac{1}{4}$ miles |
| 1 <term>kos</term> = $\frac{7.52}{4}$ miles = 1.88 miles | |

Nowadays, 1 <term>kos</term> is considered equal to 2 miles; therefore, the modern <term>yojana</term> closely matches <term>Aryabhata's</term> <note>a famous 5th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> <term>yojana</term>. The <term>kos</term> mentioned in the *Siddhanta Shiromani* is perhaps equal to the "Gau-kos" (Cow-kos) <note>a traditional unit of distance, literally the distance at which the lowing of a cow can be heard</note>, which is still prevalent in certain provinces today.

Now the question remains: how was the Earth's circumference measured? Nothing has been written on this subject in the *Surya Siddhanta*. <term>Bhaskaracharya</term>¹ <note>a 12th-century mathematician who wrote the *Siddhanta Shiromani*</note> says to measure the distance in <term>yojanas</term> between two places situated on the same North-South line (meridian). Then, find the difference between the <term>latitudes</term> <note>original: "akshansha"</note> of those two places. Then, through the <term>Rule of Three</term> <note>original: "trairashika"; a mathematical method to find an unknown fourth value from three known values based on proportions</note>, one should calculate: if this much difference in latitude results in this many <term>yojanas</term> of distance, then what will the distance be for 360°? The <term>proof</term> <note>original: "upapatti"</note> of this is as follows:

In Figure 7, one should measure the distance in <term>yojanas</term> (S to SA) between two places situated on the same North-South line. Then, one should determine the difference in latitude—the angle (S BH SA).

---

<margin>1. *Goladhyaya, Bhuvanakosha*, page 13, verse 14—</margin>
> <note>original Sanskrit: "purāntaraṃ cedidamuttaraṃ syāt tadakṣa viśleṣa lavaistadākim | cakrāṃśakaiैरityanupātayuktyā yuktaṃ niruktaṃ paridhe: pramāṇam ||"</note>
> If the distance between two towns is North-South, then by using the difference in their latitudes and applying the proportion of the total degrees in a circle (360°), the measurement of the circumference is logically determined.

Or, from the *Ganitadhyaya*, page 56, verse 1—
> <note>original Sanskrit: "yāmyodaka purayo: palāntara hataṃ bhūveṣṭanaṃ bhāṃśa hṛta | tadbhūtastha purāntarādhvana iha jñeyaṃ samaṃ yojanam ||"</note>
> The distance between two towns (North and South) multiplied by the degrees in a circle and divided by the difference in their latitudes gives the circumference of the Earth in <term>yojanas</term>.

भ (Bh) = Center of the Earth.
वभ (V-Bh) = <term>Equatorial radius</term> <note>the distance from the center of the Earth to the equator</note>.
उ (U) = North Pole or <term>Sumeru</term> <note>In ancient Indian cosmology, Mt. Sumeru is situated at the North Pole</note>.
स (Sa), सा (Sā) = Two locations on the same north-south line (meridian).

Latitude of स (Sa) = ∠ व भ स (V-Bh-Sa).
Latitude of सा (Sā) = ∠ व भ सा (V-Bh-Sā).
The difference between the two latitudes = ∠ स भ सा (Sa-Bh-Sā).

Next, the following proportion should be calculated:

∠ Sa-Bh-Sā : 360° :: Sa-Sā : Circumference of the Earth

               360° × Sa-Sā
∴ Earth's Circumference = ———————
                ∠ Sa-Bh-Sā

The method for determining <term>latitude</term> <note>the angular distance of a place north or south of the equator</note> will be explained in several ways in the third chapter, titled the <term>Triprashnadhyaya</term> <note>The "Chapter on the Three Problems" (Direction, Place, and Time)</note>.

The Earth's circumference is still measured using this same method today; however, due to modern precision instruments, this work is now performed with much greater accuracy.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical explanation of Earth's dimensions, focusing on how the Earth's circumference varies by latitude and how this affects the calculation of planetary positions relative to a prime meridian (the line passing through Ujjain and Lanka).</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

-> **The Earth's circumference multiplied by the cosine of latitude and divided by the radius gives the corrected circumference of one's own location.** <-
<note>original Sanskrit: "lambajyāghnastrijovāptas-sphuṭo bhūparidhiḥ svakaḥ"</note>
-> **By that, the daily motion of the planet multiplied by the longitude distance should be divided.** [60] <-
-> **The result, in minutes of arc and so on, should be subtracted from the planets if one is to the east.** <-
-> **For those situated to the west of the prime meridian, the local result should be added.** [61] <-

**Translation—** (60) By multiplying the Earth's circumference by the <term>cosine of latitude</term> <note>Sanskrit: "lambajya"</note> of a location and dividing by the <term>radius</term> <note>Sanskrit: "trijya"</note>, the <term>corrected circumference</term> <note>Sanskrit: "sphuta paridhi"; the circumference of the circle of latitude at that specific location</note> of that place is found. One should multiply the distance in longitude of their location by the daily motion of the planet and divide the product by this corrected circumference. (61) If the daily motion is taken in <term>minutes of arc</term> <note>Sanskrit: "kala"</note>, the result will also be in minutes. If one's location is to the east of <term>Lanka</term> <note>In ancient Indian astronomy, Lanka represents a point on the equator on the prime meridian</note>, this result should be subtracted from the <term>mean planet</term> <note>the average position of a celestial body</note> calculated for midnight at Lanka. If the location is to the west of Lanka, it should be added. By doing this, the mean positions of the planets for midnight at one's own location are obtained. [60-61]

**Scientific Commentary—** According to algebra, these verses can be expressed as follows:

                                 Earth's Circumference × Cosine of Latitude
           Corrected Circumference = ———————————————————————————————————————— (1)
                                                  Radius

           Longitude |   Longitude distance (in yojanas) × Planet's daily motion (in minutes)
           Correction| = —————————————————————————————————————————————————————————————————————— (2)
                     |                       Corrected Circumference

           Mean Planet at local midnight
           = Mean Planet at Lanka midnight ± Longitude Correction (3)

If the location is east of Lanka, use the negative sign; if west, use the positive sign.

To understand the logic behind this, one must first know what the cosine of latitude, corrected circumference, longitude, and so on, actually are.

**Sine (Jya)—** If the length of one side of a right-angled triangle is divided by the length of its hypotenuse, the result is called the <term>Sine</term> <note>Sanskrit: "jya"</note> of the angle opposite that side. In Figure 8, *Sa-Bha-Bha* is a right-angled triangle; therefore, for this *Sa-Bha-Bha* triangle:

                          Side Sa-Bha                      Side Bha-Bha
     Sine of the angle = ————————————— and Sine of the angle = ————————————— . In a right-angled
                          Hypotenuse Sa-Bha                 Hypotenuse Sa-Bha

# Chapter on Mean Motion

<meta>The text explicitly refers to "Figure 6" in the final paragraph, even though the caption provided in the diagram area is "Figure 8." The diagram shows a right-angled triangle with vertices labeled with the Devanagari letters 'Sa' (स), 'Bha' (भ), and 'Bha' (भा).</meta>

->Figure 8<-

The length of the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>Sanskrit: "karna"</note> is greater than the length of any <term>side</term> <note>Sanskrit: "bhuja"</note>; therefore, the <term>sine</term> <note>Sanskrit: "jya"</note> of the angle opposite a side will be less than one. Because of this, sines are written as <term>decimal fractions</term> <note>Hindi: "dashamlav bhinn"</note> today. This is the modern convention. In ancient times, when decimal fractions were not in common use, the sine of an angle was written in <term>integers</term> <note>Hindi: "purnank"</note>.

To determine the sine of any angle, our mathematical systems utilize a circle with a <term>radius</term> <note>Sanskrit: "trijya" or "arddhavyas"</note> of 3,438 units and a <term>circumference</term> <note>Sanskrit: "paridhi"</note> of 21,600 units. In this model, each single unit of length is equal to one <term>minute of arc</term> <note>Sanskrit: "kala"</note>. This is because the circumference represents a full circle consisting of 360 <term>degrees</term> <note>Sanskrit: "amsha"</note>, or 360 x 60 = 21,600 minutes of arc. To find the sine, two radii are drawn from the <term>center</term> <note>Sanskrit: "kendra"</note> to the circumference, forming an angle between them equal to the angle whose sine is sought. Then, from the point where the first radius meets the circumference, a <term>perpendicular</term> <note>Sanskrit: "lamba"</note> is dropped onto the second radius. The length of this perpendicular in units (minutes of arc) is called the "sine" of that angle. In Figure 6, 'A' <note>original: 'अ'</note> is the center; 'A-Aa' <note>original: 'अ आ'</note>, 'A-U' <note>original: 'अ उ'</note>, and 'A-Uu' <note>original: 'अ ऊ'</note> are three radii drawn from center 'A' to the circumference. From 'U' <note>original: 'उ'</note> or 'Uu' <note>original: 'ऊ'</note>, the perpendiculars 'U-I' <note>original: 'उ इ'</note> or 'Uu-Ii' <note>original: 'ऊ ई'</note> are dropped onto 'A-Aa'. Assuming the radius measures 3,438 units, the measure of 'U-I' or 'Uu-Ii' in those same units will be the sine of the angle 'U-A-I' or...

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

->Figure 6<-

The length within the angle Uu-Aa-Ee will be called the <term>sine</term> <note>original: "jya"; in ancient Indian trigonometry, the sine was a length in a circle of a specific radius, rather than a dimensionless ratio</note>. Those who are only familiar with modern mathematical practices may find this confusing; therefore, they should understand this distinction clearly. The value of the <term>radius</term> <note>original: "trijya"</note> was taken as 3,438 because when the <term>circumference</term> <note>original: "paridhi"</note> is divided into <term>minutes of arc</term> <note>original: "kala"</note>, the value of the radius according to modern precise calculation stands at 3437$\frac{3}{7}$ minutes, the nearest whole number of which is 3,438. The measure of the radius is considered to be equal to the number of full minutes contained in one modern **radian***.

**Corrected Circumference** <note>original: "sphuta paridhi"</note>—The circle on the Earth's surface that is equidistant from the North and South Poles and divides the Earth's surface into two equal halves is called the **Equator** <note>original: "vishuvat rekha"</note>. The half of the globe to the north of the Equator is called the **Northern Hemisphere** <note>original: "uttar gol"</note> and the southern half is called the **Southern Hemisphere** <note>original: "dakshin gol"</note>. From this line, the <term>celestial poles</term> <note>the points in the sky directly above the Earth's North or South Poles; the Northern Pole Star is currently about 1° away from the true North Pole</note> are visible on the horizon. At this location, the <term>latitude</term> <note>original: "akshansh"</note> is zero and the <term>co-latitude</term> <note>original: "lambansh"; the complement of the latitude (90° - latitude)</note> is 90°. Therefore, the Equator is also called the **Circle of No Latitude** <note>original: "nirakshavritta"</note>. In Figure 10, 'Va-Vaa-Vi' is the Equator. If a circle is drawn on the Earth's surface through any location 'Sa' parallel to the Equator (**Parallel of latitude**), its measurement is called the **corrected circumference** of that location 'Sa'. As you travel North or South, the <term>reduced circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta paridhi"; the circumference of the circle of latitude at a specific point</note> gradually decreases, until it becomes zero at the poles. In the same manner, the latitude increases while the co-latitude decreases; at the poles, the latitude reaches 90° and the co-latitude becomes zero. <meta>This page continues the geometric proof for calculating the "reduced circumference" (the circumference of a circle of latitude) based on the Earth's total equatorial circumference. It transitions from calculating latitude-based distances to defining longitude (Deshantara) using a new diagram.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

The radius is 'S Bha,' which is also called the <term>sine of the co-latitude</term> <note>original: "lambajya"</note> of point 'S,' because the <term>co-latitude</term> <note>original: "lambansha"</note> of S is ∠ S Bh U, the opposite side of which is the arm S Bha.

It is proven by <term>geometry</term> <note>original: "rekhaganit"</note> that the ratio between the circumferences of two circles is the same as the ratio between their radii or diameters. Therefore,

->Radius V Bh : Radius S Bha :: Equatorial Circumference V Va Vi : Reduced Circumference S Sa Si<-

->∴ Reduced Circumference S Sa Si = (Equatorial Circumference V Va Vi × Sine of Co-latitude S Bha) / Radius V Bh = (Earth's Circumference × Sine of Co-latitude) / Radius<-

This formula applies when the radius is 3438 <note>In traditional Indian trigonometry, the total sine table is calculated using a radius of 3438 units (arc-minutes) to simplify calculations</note> and the value of the sine of the co-latitude is expressed in arc-minutes according to the <term>Siddhantic</term> system, the table for which is provided in the second chapter.

If one wishes to find the <term>reduced circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta paridhi"; the circumference of the circle of latitude at a specific location</note> according to modern methods, then: 
Reduced Circumference S Sa Si = Earth's Circumference × **Sine of Colatitude**, where the sine of the co-latitude is given in decimals (because in this method, the Sine of Co-latitude = (S Bha) / (S Bh) = (S Bha) / (V Bh)).

**Longitude** <note>original: "deshantara"</note>—Figure 11 is a diagram of the surface of a hemisphere of the Earth in which the <term>latitudes</term> <note>original: "akshansha"</note> of the locations Si, A, S, and Sa in the Northern Hemisphere are the same; therefore, for these four locations:

<note>This diagram represents a grid on the Earth's surface. 'U' and 'D' mark the North and South Poles. The line 'V-Va' represents the Equator. The points Si, A, S, and Sa represent various cities or observation points located along the same parallel of latitude.</note>

<summary>This page provides the geometric formula to calculate the reduced circumference of the Earth at any given latitude and introduces the concept of longitude (Deshantara) through a hemispheric diagram.</summary>
<keywords>circumference, sine of co-latitude, geometry, longitude, latitude, Surya Siddhanta, 3438 radius</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page explains the calculation of longitude (Deshantara) both in terms of distance (yojanas) and time (nadi/ghatika), using the meridian passing through Ujjain and Lanka as the prime meridian for ancient Indian astronomy.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

The <term>reduced circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta paridhi"; the circumference of the circle of latitude at a specific location</note> is also unique to each latitude. The north-south lines (<term>meridians</term>) of these locations are, in sequence, U C P D, U A L D, U S P D, and U Sa Pa D. If the points A (<term>Avanti</term> <note>Modern-day Ujjain, considered the prime meridian in ancient Indian astronomy</note>) and L (<term>Lanka</term> <note>A hypothetical point on the equator directly south of Ujjain, not modern Sri Lanka</note>) lie on the line U A L D, then this will be called the <term>standard meridian</term> <note>original: "madhya rekha"</note> of India; just as today the north-south line passing through Greenwich is called the <term>prime meridian</term> <note>original: "bhumadhya rekha"</note> by people in Europe and America. That segment of the reduced circumference of a place which falls between that location's north-south line and the standard meridian is called the <term>longitude in yojanas</term> <note>original: "deshantara"; a yojana is a traditional measure of distance, roughly 8–9 miles</note>. For example, the longitude of S is S-A, the longitude of Sa is Sa-A, and the longitude of Si is Si-A. Similarly, the longitude of P is P-L, the longitude of Pa is Pa-L, and the longitude of Pi is Pi-L. It is also clear from the diagram that although P and S are on the same north-south line, their longitudes (in yojanas) are not equal because the reduced circumference at S is smaller than the reduced circumference at P (the earth's equatorial circumference). If there were a location 'K' further on this same line, its longitude 'K-Ka' (in yojanas) would be even smaller. In verse 60, the word "longitude" is used according to this definition. However, this definition is not simple or practical for daily use. Later, in verse 64, there is a discussion of the <term>longitude in time</term> <note>original: "deshantara nadi"</note>. This is also a definition of longitude that is simpler; therefore, it is appropriate to explain it here as well.

In all locations situated on the same north-south line, such as K, S, P, and Kh, midday or midnight occurs at the exact same time. However, in locations to the east of this line, midday or midnight occurs earlier, and in locations to the west, it occurs later. The amount of time by which midday occurs at S before it occurs at A (the standard meridian) is what we call the <term>Eastern Longitude-Time</term> <note>original: "purva deshantara kala"</note> of S. We can express this in units of time; if written in <term>ghatikas</term> or <term>pals</term> <note>Traditional Indian units where 1 day = 60 ghatikas/nadis; 1 ghatika = 24 minutes</note>, we call it <term>longitude-ghatika</term> or <term>longitude-nadi</term>. If written in hours and minutes, we call it longitude hours or minutes. This definition provides the convenience of expressing the longitude of K, S, P, and Kh with a single value, whereas their longitudes in yojanas would have to be written differently for each.

Similarly, the amount of time by which midday occurs at Si after the standard meridian is called the <term>Western Longitude-Time</term> of Si.

Midday or midnight occurs earlier or later because the Earth rotates once on its axis from west to east like a spinning top in 24 hours (or 60 <term>ghadis</term>), which makes celestial bodies like the sun, moon, and stars appear to revolve from east to west <unclear>...</unclear>

</vocab>

It explains that while linear longitude varies by latitude, time-based longitude remains constant for all points on the same meridian.</summary>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...they seem to be moving. In our scientific treatises <note>original: "siddhantas"</note>, this apparent motion of the celestial bodies is called the **motion caused by the Pravaha wind** <note>original: "pravaha-vayu-janita gati"; ancient Indian cosmology attributed the daily rotation of the stars and planets to a powerful, invisible cosmic wind called Pravaha</note>. For the sake of convenience, the Sun itself will occasionally be described as circling the Earth in these writings, as making such an assumption does not cause any errors in the mathematical calculations.

->Figure 12<-

In Figure 12, **Bha** represents the center of the Earth, while **Pa** and **La** are two locations situated on the <term>equator</term> <note>original: "vishuvat rekha"</note>. The line **Pa-Bha** represents the Earth's <term>radius</term> <note>original: "arddhavyas"</note>, which has been drawn significantly out of proportion to make the diagram clear; in reality, the distance to the Sun is approximately twenty-three thousand times the Earth's radius. The Sun completes one full revolution around the Earth in 60 <term>ghadis</term> <note>A traditional unit of time where 1 ghadi equals 24 minutes; 60 ghadis comprise a full 24-hour day</note> along the path marked Ra, Ra', Ri, Ree... The line **Ra-Pa-Ru**, which touches the equator, is a tangent line representing the <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "kshitij"</note> for an observer at point **Pa**. The Sun is visible from location **Pa** only when it is above this line. When the Sun reaches point **Ra** just above the horizon, it is <term>sunrise</term> <note>original: "suryodaya"</note> for the inhabitants of **Pa**. At the moment the Sun reaches point **Ri**, it is <term>noon</term> <note>original: "madhyahna"</note> at **Pa**. When it reaches **Ru**, it will appear to be sinking or <term>setting</term> <note>original: "suryasta"</note> to the people at **Pa**, and when it reaches **Re**, it is midnight at **Pa**. Similarly, the sunrise will be visible from location **La** when the Sun is at **Ra'**, noon will occur when it is at **Ree**, and sunset...

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motion</header>

...time will be when it is at 'Ha', and **midnight** <note>original: "arddharatri"</note> will be at that time when it is at 'Ra'.

It is clear from the diagram that sunrise at 'La' will occur later than sunrise at 'Pa' by the same amount of time it takes the Sun to travel from 'Ra' to 'Ra''. However, to travel from Ra to Ra', it must move through the angle **Ra-Bha-Ra'** or angle **Pa-Bha-La**, because the angle between two tangent lines of a circumference is equal to the angle between the <term>radii</term> drawn from the points of contact. This matter will be understood even more easily from the positions of the Sun at **midday** <note>original: "madhyahna"</note> or midnight; because it has already been explained that midday occurs at 'Pa' when the Sun reaches 'Ri' and at 'La' when it reaches 'Ree'. Therefore, midday at 'La' will occur as much later than at 'Pa' as the time it takes the Sun to travel from 'Ri' to 'Ree'. This duration of time is called the **longitudinal time** <note>original: "deshantara kala"</note> between 'Pa' and 'La'. The longitude of Pa and La can also be expressed by the angle Pa-Bha-La, and the longitude can be written in **degrees, minutes, and seconds** <note>original: "ansha, kala, vikala"</note>. Whether the unit for expressing longitude is in <term>ghadis</term> and <term>pals</term> <note>traditional Indian units of time; 1 ghadi = 24 minutes, 1 pal = 24 seconds</note>, or in degrees and minutes, both methods are convenient, and whichever is required is used. It is evident that in 60 ghadis or 24 hours, the Sun completes one rotation, which is 360°; therefore, in one ghadi it will travel 6°, and in 1 hour it will travel 15°. Consequently, if the longitude of two places differs by one degree, there will be a difference of 10 pals or 4 minutes in their times of midday or midnight. In short, it is written that the longitude of the two is 1°, 10 pals, or 4 minutes. Generally, it is the convention to measure longitude from the **prime meridian** <note>original: "madhya rekha"</note>. For places that are East of the prime meridian, 'East' must be written before their longitude, and for those in the West, 'West' must be written before their longitude; otherwise, there is a risk of confusion.

It can also be easily known from the diagram that if the **mean position** <note>original: "madhyama sthana"; the calculated average position of a planet ignoring specific orbital anomalies</note> of a planet is calculated for the time of midnight at **Lanka** <note>In ancient Indian astronomy, Lanka refers to a fictional point on the equator sharing the same meridian as Ujjain, serving as the 0° longitude reference</note> (La), it will not be the mean position for the time of midnight at place 'Pa'. Because 'Pa' is East of Lanka, midnight will occur there earlier, and since the planet is always in motion, it will be at a position slightly before its position at Lanka. How much earlier it will be should be determined by the **rule of three** <note>original: "trairashika"; a mathematical method of proportion</note>: if the planet travels this much in 60 ghadis, how much will it travel during the longitude-ghadis of 'Pa'? Whatever result is obtained should be subtracted from the mean position at midnight in Lanka. If the place is West of the prime meridian, then midnight there will be later than midnight in Lanka by the amount of that place’s longitude-ghadis, and the planet [will have moved further]...

If <term>longitude</term> <note>original: "deshantara"</note> is expressed in <term>ghadis</term> <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> or degrees rather than in <term>yojanas</term> <note>a traditional unit of distance</note>, the rule from verse 60 takes this simplified form:

The following verse explains which cities fall upon the <term>Prime Meridian</term> <note>original: "madhya-rekha"</note>.

->The line passing through the center of the abode of the Rakshasas and the mountain of the gods,<-
->Also passes through Rohitaka, Avanti, and the nearby lake. ||62||<-
<note>original: "Rakshasalaya-devaukash-shailayor-madhya-sutraga / Rohitakam-avanti cha yatha sannihitam sarah ||62||"</note>

**Translation**—(62) The countries situated on the line passing between the abode of the <term>Rakshasas</term> <note>beings from Hindu mythology; here referring to Lanka</note>, i.e., Lanka, and the abode of the gods, i.e., Mount <term>Sumeru</term> (the North Pole), such as Rohitaka, Avanti, Kurukshetra, etc., are on the Prime Meridian. ||62||

**Scientific Commentary**—In the scientific commentary of the previous verse, the Prime Meridian was discussed thoroughly in relation to longitude. Here, it is necessary to add that the point where the North-South line passing through Ujjain intersects the Equator is called Lanka. It cannot be said with certainty whether this astronomical "Lanka" is the same Lanka where Ravana lived or a different imaginary point assumed for the convenience of calculation. Some people¹ consider the modern island of <term>Simhala</term> (Ceylon) to be Ravana's Lanka and Polonnaruwa to be Ravana's capital, speculating that it is a corruption of the name <term>Paulastya-nagar</term> <note>The city of the descendants of Pulastya, i.e., Ravana</note>.

Whether Rohitaka is the modern Rohtak or some other place of the same name is worth considering; because the longitude of modern Rohtak is given as 162 seconds "East" in *Indian Chronology*, p. 160, which suggests that Rohtak is from the Prime Meridian—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 64 regarding the methods for calculating longitude, specifically transitioning from mathematical ratios to the practical observation of lunar eclipses to determine a location's distance from the Prime Meridian.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

...is 8 <term>palas</term> <note>a unit of time; 1 pala equals 24 seconds</note> east. There are currently no means available here to determine what the <term>longitude</term> <note>original: "deshantara"</note> of Kurukshetra is considered to be nowadays; therefore, it cannot be proven whether Kurukshetra lies exactly on the Prime Meridian or is shifted slightly to the east or west.

The following three verses explain how longitudinal time is determined via a lunar eclipse and how the distance in <term>yojanas</term> <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-13 kilometers</note> is derived from that longitude:

->If the moon's emergence from eclipse occurs after the time calculated for the meridian,<-
->then one's own location is to the east of the center. (63)<-
->If it occurs before, or similarly during the start of total darkness,<-
->multiply the true circumference of the Earth by the difference in those ghadis. (64)<-
->Dividing by sixty, the resulting yojanas indicate the distance to the east or west.<-
->One must know the circumference at their own latitude; through this, one should calculate the longitudinal correction. (65)<-

**Translation** — (63) One should first calculate the time (how many <term>ghadis</term> <note>1 ghadi = 24 minutes</note> after midnight) at which the totally eclipsed Moon will begin to emerge from the shadow on the Prime Meridian (the north-south line passing through Ujjain or Lanka). Then, through observation, one must determine the exact time at which the eclipsed Moon begins to emerge at their own location (how many ghadis after their own local midnight). If the observed time is greater than the mathematically calculated time, it should be understood that one's location is east of the Prime Meridian. (64) If the observed time is less than the calculated time, one's location is west of the Prime Meridian. Similarly, this can also be determined by observing the moment the Moon's full disk enters the shadow (total obscuration). The difference between the mathematically calculated time and the observed time is called the <term>longitudinal time</term> <note>original: "deshantara kala"</note> or longitudinal ghadis of one's location (since time is typically recorded in ghadis). By multiplying these longitudinal ghadis by the <term>corrected circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta paridhi"; the circumference of the Earth at a specific latitude</note> (65) and dividing the product by sixty, the result obtained is the longitudinal yojanas to the east (if the place is in the east) or the longitudinal yojanas to the west (if the place is in the west). Using these longitudinal yojanas, one should perform the <term>longitudinal correction</term> <note>original: "deshantara sanskara"</note> for the planets (according to the method described in verses 60-61). (63–65)

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

...is the name given to the time when a <term>total eclipse</term> <note>original: "khagrasa"; literally "swallowing the whole"</note> begins. This is also called the **Time of Immersion** <note>original: "sammilana-kala"</note>.

**Emergence** <note>original: "unmilana"</note> == (1) The opening of the eyes, (2) becoming manifest, (3) the exit of the totally eclipsed Moon from darkness. Therefore, the **Time of Emergence** refers to the moment when the disc of the totally eclipsed Moon begins to come out of the darkness.

<term>Time of Contact</term> <note>original: "sparsha-kala"</note> refers to the moment when the Moon's disc begins to enter the darkness—that is, the moment the actual eclipse begins.

<term>Time of Release</term> <note>original: "moksha-kala"</note> refers to the moment when the entire disc of the Moon has come out of the darkness.

In Figure 13, **R** is the center of the Sun, **P** is the center of the Earth, and **Chha** is the tip of the Earth’s shadow. The Moon revolves around the Earth in the <term>Lunar Orbit</term> <note>original: "chandra-kaksha"</note>. When the center of the Moon reaches the point in its orbit marked **1**, the Moon's disc touches the Earth's shadow; therefore, this position of the Moon is the state of the <term>Time of Contact</term> during the eclipse. At this moment, the Moon's disc is seen entering the darkness. When the Moon reaches the position indicated by the number **2**, its entire disc is within the darkness. This is the state of the **Time of Full Immersion** or the **Time of Totality**. When the Moon reaches the position shown by the number **3**, the Moon is about to emerge from the darkness. This is the state of the **Time of Emergence**. And when the entire disc of the Moon has come out of the darkness, as shown by the number **4**, that is the state of the <term>Time of Release</term>.

When any of these four events occurs in the sky, it is seen on the Earth's surface at that same moment<sup>1</sup>. However, because sunrise or noon—by which <term>clocks</term><sup>2</sup> <note>original: "ghadiya"</note> can be easily and accurately set—does not occur at the same time in all places on Earth (as proven in the scientific commentary of verses 60-61 while explaining the definition of <term>Longitude</term> <note>original: "deshantara"</note>), the time of observing an event will differ on the clocks of different locations. The clocks of places east of the prime meridian...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on determining geographic longitude (Deshantara) through the observation of lunar eclipses. It explains why certain phases of an eclipse, specifically 'Emergence' (Unmilana), provide more accurate time measurements than others due to the visual clarity of the event.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>The Sun’s Treatise</header>

...clocks remain ahead of the time at the <term>Prime Meridian</term> <note>original: "madhya-rekha"; the zero-longitude line passing through Ujjain in ancient Indian geography</note> because sunrise occurs earlier here. Therefore, the moment an eclipse is observed at this location will be later than the time recorded on the Prime Meridian, while in places to the west, it will be earlier. The time an eclipse is seen on the Prime Meridian is the same as the time derived through calculation. Therefore, by using mathematics to determine when an event will occur on the Prime Meridian and comparing it to when it is observed according to one's local clock, one can easily understand the difference between the local times of these two places. This difference is known as the <term>Time of Longitude</term> <note>original: "deshantara-kala"</note> for that specific location.

To determine this time of longitude, the method of observing the <term>Time of Emergence</term> <note>original: "unmilana-kala"; the moment the Moon begins to exit total darkness</note> was discussed previously. The reason for this is that at that moment, the Moon is about to emerge from the darkness, and the Earth's surface is also covered in shadow; thus, as soon as the lunar disc begins to enter the light, it becomes visible with great clarity, and there is very little error in determining the exact time. At the <term>Time of Full Immersion</term> <note>original: "sammilana-kala"; when the Moon is fully covered</note>, it is somewhat difficult to know exactly when the Moon has completely entered the darkness, so there may be some error in calculating longitude from it. Regarding the <term>Time of Contact</term> <note>original: "sparsha-kala"; the start of the eclipse</note> and the <term>Time of Release</term> <note>original: "moksha-kala"; the end of the partial phase</note>, one cannot accurately determine for several minutes <note>original: "pal"; a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</note> when the actual event took place; therefore, these are not used for calculating longitude.

How the <term>Distance of Longitude</term> <note>original: "deshantara-yojana"</note> is known from the time of longitude should be understood from the scientific commentary on verses 60-61. It is clear that the Sun completes a revolution of the Earth in 60 <term>ghadis</term> <note>one ghadi equals 24 minutes; 60 ghadis equal 24 hours</note>, traveling around the <term>Local Circumference</term> <note>original: "sphuta-paridhi"; the circumference of the Earth at a specific latitude</note> of any location in that time. Therefore, the segment of the Local Circumference that represents the distance between that place and the Prime Meridian will be proportional to the time of longitude. This is expressed through the <term>Rule of Three</term> <note>original: "trairashika"; a mathematical proportion</note> as follows:

->60 ghadis : Longitude in ghadis :: Local Circumference : Longitude in distance.<-

It is necessary to briefly discuss some other methods of determining longitude here.

**Methods of Determining Longitude:** One method has already been described above. This is a very ancient method, and before modern precision instruments were created, there could be no better method than this. Most of the methods prevalent today are variations of this one; the only difference is that today, the exact time of celestial events like eclipses can be known with extreme precision, allowing the time of longitude to be determined as accurately as possible. In the method described above, some inaccuracies remain...

It introduces the mathematical proportion (Rule of Three) used to convert time differences into physical distance (yojanas) based on the Earth's local circumference.</summary>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>Section on Mean Motions</header>

...is the case. The reason for this is that it cannot be precisely observed with the naked eye or even with a telescope <note>original: "durvikshana yantra"</note> at exactly which moment the emergence <note>original: "unmilana"; the moment the moon begins to leave the shadow</note> or the full immersion <note>original: "sammilana"; the start of totality</note> of a lunar eclipse begins. If two observers sitting close to one another were to take their own clocks to record when the immersion begins, and were to quietly write down the time each one first perceived it, it would be found that there is a difference of two to three <margin>1. Godfray's Treatise on Astronomy. Sixth edition, page 261.</margin> minutes between their observations. Perhaps this is the reason why there is a difference of two or three minutes in the longitudes <note>original: "deshantara"</note> of Ujjain, Kurukshetra, and Rohtak, even though these are stated in the 62nd verse to be on the same North-South line—that is, on the <term>Prime Meridian</term> <note>the zero-longitude line in ancient Indian geography, passing through Ujjain</note>. Below, the longitudes of these places are compared with Greenwich:

| City | Longitude from Greenwich | Longitude from Ujjain (Angular) | Longitude from Ujjain (Temporal) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| | | | **Ghadi | Pala | Vipala | Minute | Second** |
| Ujjain² | 75° 46' 6" East | | |
| Kurukshetra³ | 77° 20' " | 0° 33' 54" East | 0 | 5 | 36 | (2 | 15.6) |
| Rohtak | 76° 35' " | 0° 48' 54" " | 0 | 8 | 6 | (3 | 15.6) |
| Kashi⁴ | 83° 3' 4" " | 7° 16' 58" " | 1 | 12 | 50 | (29 | 8) |

<note>Traditional Indian time units: 1 Ghadi = 24 minutes; 1 Pala = 24 seconds; 1 Vipala = 0.4 seconds.</note>

It is evident from these figures that the method of determining longitude by a lunar eclipse can result in a difference of two or three minutes.

**The Second Method** — Just as the Moon revolves around the Earth and undergoes eclipses, similarly, four or five bodies are seen revolving around Jupiter through a telescope. These are called the Moons of Jupiter. When these enter the shadow of Jupiter, they also undergo eclipses. Jupiter's moons are very small and their orbital periods are also short; therefore, eclipses occur in them quite frequently. The times of their disappearance and reappearance due to eclipses are provided in **Nautical Almanacs** <note>original: "navik panchanga"</note> according to Greenwich time. Therefore, if at any place, according to its local time, the disappearance or reappearance of Jupiter's moons...

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...if the time is observed, the difference from the time given in the **Nautical Almanac** <note>original: "Navik Panchang"</note> is the **longitude** <note>original: "deshantara"</note> of that place from **Greenwich**. However, this method is also coarse, like the one mentioned above, because the exact moment of the disappearance or reappearance of Jupiter's moon cannot be definitively determined even with a **telescope** <note>original: "durvikshana"</note>; nevertheless, it does not involve as much error as the first method.

**The Third Method** — By comparing the moments of the appearance and disappearance of falling stars <note>meteors</note> with the local times of different places, longitude can be determined with precision, provided that the timing of the stars' fall can be established beforehand and there is no confusion in identifying them.

**The Fourth Method: Determining longitude by sending news via electricity** — If two places are so connected that a message can be sent from one to the other via electricity <note>referring to the telegraph</note>, then the longitude of these two places can be known quite easily. This is because the time taken for an electric message to arrive is so minuscule that any error it might cause is almost non-existent.

Suppose the longitude of Lucknow from Kashi <note>Varanasi</note> is to be determined. The observers in both cities should maintain the same type of clock; for instance, if one clock shows **civil time** <note>original: "Savan Kaal"; in Indian astronomy, time measured from sunrise to sunrise</note>, then the other clock must also show civil time. Both clocks should be synchronized with their respective local times so that each clock accurately reflects the local time of its own station. Kashi is to the east of Lucknow; therefore, Kashi’s local time will be ahead of Lucknow’s local time, and the difference between these two is the longitude between Kashi and Lucknow. At the moment when Kashi’s clock shows the time ‘$S_1$’, an electric signal should be sent from Kashi. At the moment this signal reaches Lucknow, the time should be noted on Lucknow’s clock. If this clock shows the time ‘$S_2$’ and it is assumed that the signal reached Lucknow at the exact same moment it was sent from Kashi, then the longitude ‘$D_1$’ from Kashi to Lucknow will be established by the following equation:

-> $D_1 = S_1 - S_2$ <-

However, the value of longitude derived from this equation will be slightly less than the true longitude, because some amount of time must surely be required for the electric signal to travel from Kashi to Lucknow. If the value of this time is ‘$y$’ and the true longitude from Kashi to Lucknow is ‘$D$’, then the form of the aforementioned equation will be:

-> $D = (S_1 + y) - S_2 = D_1 + y$ <-

<sig>(1)</sig>

<vocab>longitude, Nautical Almanac, Greenwich, Jupiter, telescope, civil time, electric signal, equation</vocab>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

# Chapter on Mean Motions <note>original: "Madhyamadhikara"</note>

...because at the moment the news reaches Lucknow, the time in Kashi will be ‘S₁ + y’ <note>where 'S' likely refers to 'Samaya' or time, and 'y' is the signal delay</note>. To find the value of ‘y’, a signal should be sent from Lucknow to Kashi and the local times of both should be noted again. Suppose at the moment the signal was sent from Lucknow, the time on the Lucknow clock was ‘S₂’, and at the moment the signal reached Kashi, the time on the Kashi clock was ‘S₁’. If we assume that the signal takes no time to arrive, then the difference between these two, D₂, would be the **longitude** <note>original: "deshantara"</note> of Lucknow, which takes this form:—

-> D₂ = S₁ - S₂ <-

However, the value of D₂ will be slightly greater than the actual value because it necessarily takes some amount of time for the signal to arrive, which will again be equal to ‘y’. Therefore, the true longitude is:

-> D = S₁ - (S₂ + y) = (S₁ - S₂ - y) = D₂ - y (2) <-
-> By adding the corresponding sides of equations (1) and (2) <-
-> 2 D = D₁ + D₂ <-
-> or D = (D₁ + D₂) / 2 (3) <-

This means that the longitude time obtained by sending a signal from Kashi to Lucknow should be added to the longitude time found by sending a reverse signal from Lucknow to Kashi. Then, by adding both and dividing by two, the true longitude time will be known. There are several other methods for determining longitude which are useful for sailors and which require assistance from a **Nautical Almanac** <term>original: "Navik Panchang"; a publication used in navigation that provides the positions of celestial bodies</term> or a clock synchronized with **Greenwich** <term>the Prime Meridian in London used as the international reference for longitude</term>; therefore, they are not described in this place.

> The commencement of the day in an eastern country occurs after midnight;
> in the west, it should be indicated as occurring before midnight, by the number of nadis <note>original: "nadi"; an Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of its own longitude. || 66 ||

**Translation** — (66) In places located to the east of the **Prime Meridian** <note>original: "Madhya Rekha"; historically, the line passing through Ujjain in Indian astronomy</note>, the **commencement of the day** <note>original: "vāra-pravṛtti"; the start of the astrological weekday</note> occurs as much time after that location's midnight as the value of that location's longitude time. In places to the west of the Prime Meridian, the commencement of the day occurs as much time before that location's midnight as the value of that location's longitude time.

**Scientific Commentary** <note>original: "Vijnana Bhashya"</note> — According to this rule, in Kashi (Varanasi), which is 73 *palas* <note>original: "pal"; a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</note> east of Ujjain or the Prime Meridian of India, the commencement of the day occurs when—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on timekeeping and longitude, transitioning from the technical methods of calculating longitude (like telegraphic signals) to the traditional astronomical theories regarding when a new day actually begins.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

In Kashi, according to local time, midnight is recorded as 12 o'clock and 73 *palas* <note>a traditional unit where 60 palas equal 24 minutes</note>, which is 12:26:12 AM. In Bombay, which is about 26 *palas* west of Ujjain, the **commencement of the day** <term>original: "vara-pravritti"; the point at which the weekday changes</term> occurs about 26 *palas* or 11 minutes and 36 seconds before 12:00 midnight. This means that at the moment it is midnight on India's central meridian, the day's transition should be understood to occur at other locations in India as well. Therefore, the planetary positions calculated through mathematics for midnight at Lanka or Ujjain apply to other locations at the moment their own day begins. For this reason, if one wishes to calculate the position of a planet for midnight at a specific location, the **longitude correction** <term>original: "deshantara-phala"; the adjustment made to account for the distance from the prime meridian</term> must be added or subtracted.

It is the opinion of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>an ancient and foundational Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy</note> that the commencement of the day occurs at all places simultaneously with midnight in Ujjain. However, Great teachers such as Brahmagupta¹ and Bhaskaracharya² have considered the day to begin at the moment of **sunrise** <note>original: "suryodaya"</note> in Lanka. This is because, in their view, the creation of the universe began at the moment the sun was first seen in Lanka, and the first day also began at that time. Today, this rule is generally the most prevalent. That said, followers of the Vaishnava sect³ consider the day to begin at midnight; for religious rituals, they observe the lunar date (*tithi*) that was present at the previous midnight. Consequently, their *Ekadashi* <note>the eleventh lunar day, significant for fasting</note> often falls on the day of *Dvadashi* <note>the twelfth lunar day</note>. In most almanacs (*panchangs*), the true positions of the planets are also given for the time of midnight.

Between these two views, considering the day to start at midnight is simpler and more universal. All places located on the same north-south line (longitude) always experience midnight or midday simultaneously. However, except for two days in the year <note>the equinoxes</note>, sunrise never occurs at the same time for all such places. Sunrise depends on the sun's **declination** <term>original: "kranti"; the angular distance of the sun north or south of the celestial equator</term> and the **latitude** <term>original: "akshansha"</term> of the locations...

---

१. <note>original Sanskrit: "jagatti tamobhūte’smin sṛṣṭyādau bhāskarādibhiḥ sṛṣṭaiḥ | yasmāddinapravṛttirdinavāro’rkodayāt tasmāt || 33 ||"</note>
"In this world enveloped in darkness, at the beginning of creation, the Sun and other bodies were created. Therefore, the commencement of the day and the weekday is from sunrise."
— *Brahmasphuta Siddhanta*, Chapter on Mean Motion.

२. <note>original Sanskrit: "laṅkānagaryāmudayācca bhānostasyaiva vāre prathamaṃ babhūva | madhoḥsitāderdina māsa varṣa yugādikānāṃ yugapat pravṛttiḥ ||15||"</note>
"From the rising of the Sun in the city of Lanka, his day first began. At that same moment, the first day of the bright half of the month of Chaitra, the month, the year, and the start of the Yugas all commenced simultaneously."
— *Siddhanta Shiromani*, Chapter on Mathematics, page 7.

<page-num>73</page-num>
# Chapter on Mean Motion

...accordingly, it varies slightly ahead or behind, which will be explained in the third chapter. Furthermore, in eastern and western countries, due to the **longitude correction** <term>deshantara-samskara: the mathematical adjustment for the distance from the prime meridian</term>, there is a significant difference in the time of sunrise. Due to all these factors, the **commencement of the weekday** <term>vara-pravritti</term> sometimes occurs an hour before or after sunrise, which is quite complicated. However, if the day is considered to begin at midnight, no discrepancy arises from the sun's declination or the **latitude** <term>akshansha</term> of the locations. Yes, the longitude correction will still be necessary, but even then, the start of the day will remain at night, preventing any confusion. In common practice, morning rituals, evening prayers, or travel are often undertaken before sunrise, which suggests that generally, the start of the day is assumed to be two or three *ghadis* <note>a traditional unit of time; 1 ghati equals 24 minutes</note> before sunrise. This topic has been extensively discussed in texts like the **Dharma Sindhu** <note>original: "Dharma Sindhu"; a 17th-century Sanskrit compendium on religious law and ritual timing</note>, **Nirnaya Sindhu**, and others.

Nowadays, in European countries, the date changes and the day is considered to begin at midnight; therefore, English dates also change at midnight. Because of this, many people believe that starting the weekday at midnight is an English concept, but this is a mistake. In our tradition too, there is a rule for considering the **commencement of the weekday** <term>vara-pravritti</term> from midnight.

Up to this point, it has been explained what the **mean position** <term>madhyama-sthana</term> of a planet is at **midnight** <term>ardha-ratri</term> for any given location and how it is determined. In the next verse, it is explained what to do if one needs to calculate the **mean planet** <term>madhyama-graha</term> for any time other than midnight.

->Multiply the daily motion by the desired time (in ghadis), then divide by sixty to get the results in minutes of arc.<-
->Subtract this from the planet's position if the time is past, and add it if the time is yet to come; thus the planet's position for that specific moment is found. || 67 ||<-
<note>original: "ishta-nadi-guna bhuktish-shashtya bhakta kaladikah / gate shodhyo yute gamye grahas-tat-kaliko bhavet"</note>

**Translation**—(If you wish to know the **mean planet** <term>madhyama-graha</term> at a time other than midnight) multiply the **desired ghati** <term>ishta-ghadi</term>—meaning the number of *ghadis* before or after midnight—by the planet's daily mean motion (which is convenient to write in minutes of arc), and divide the product by 60. Subtract the resulting quotient from the mean planet at **midnight** <term>ardha-ratri</term> if the **desired time** <term>ishta-kala</term> has already passed...

<note>1. Morning prayer (sandhya) occurs three ghadis before sunrise; evening prayer occurs two ghadis after sunset. *Dharma Sindhu*, first section, page 2, Nirnayasagar Press edition (Shaka 1826).</note>

<page-num>74</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta

...and add it if the **desired time** <term>ishta kala: the specific moment for which a calculation is being made</term> occurs after midnight. By doing this, the **instantaneous position** <term>tatkalika sthana: the actual position of a planet at a specific moment</term> of the planet will be determined. ||67||

**Scientific Commentary**—It is clear that the **mean position** <term>madhyama sthana: the average or theoretical position of a planet ignoring irregularities</term> of a planet calculated at midnight does not remain the same at any other time because planets are in constant motion. Therefore, to know the mean position of a planet at any desired time before or after midnight, it is necessary to determine how far the planet moves during that specific interval. This can be easily calculated using the **Rule of Three** <term>trairashika: a mathematical proportion used to find an unknown value based on three known values</term>—

->60 ghadis : Desired ghadi :: Daily motion : Motion in the desired ghadi<-
<note>A "ghadi" is a traditional Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes; there are 60 ghadis in a 24-hour day.</note>

->Therefore, Motion in desired ghadi = (Desired ghadi × Daily motion) / 60 ghadis<-

Therefore, the position of the planet at the desired time:

->= Position at midnight ± (Desired ghadi × Daily motion) / 60 ghadis<-

If the desired time is before midnight, the minus sign should be used (subtraction), and if it is after midnight, the plus sign should be used (addition).

This is so clear that there seems to be no need to increase the size of the book by providing examples.

> By one-eightieth of the minutes of the zodiac, the Moon is deflected to its maximum north or south distance from the ecliptic by its own node. ||68||
> Jupiter is deflected by two-ninths of that amount, Mars by three-ninths, and Mercury, Venus, and Saturn by four-ninths through their respective nodes. ||69||
> Thus, multiplied by ten, these values—twenty-seven, six, twelve, six, twelve, and twelve—are the mean latitudes in minutes for the Moon and the other planets in order. ||70||
<note>original: "भचक्रलिप्ताशीत्यंशै:..." etc. These verses define the maximum celestial latitude (vikshepa) or the "sway" of planets away from the Sun's path.</note>

**Translation**—(68) Due to its **node** <term>pata: the point where a planet's orbit intersects the ecliptic</term>, the Moon moves a maximum of 270 **minutes** <term>kala: 1/60th of a degree</term> north or south from the nearest point on the **ecliptic** <term>kranti vritta: the apparent path of the Sun</term>. (69) Through their own nodes, Jupiter moves by 2/9 of this amount, Mars by 3/9 (or 1/3) of this amount, and Mercury, Venus, and Saturn move by 4/9 of this amount. (70) Thus, the mean latitudes of the six planets—Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn—are 270, 60, 120, 60, 120, and 120 minutes respectively. ||68-70||
<note>There is a slight discrepancy between traditional Sanskrit lists and this specific commentary's values for Mars and Mercury; the commentator here lists them as 60 and 120.</note>

**Scientific Commentary**—The scientific commentary of the previous 33rd verse contains a description of the Moon's node. In Figure 4, the lunar orbit and the ecliptic are shown intersecting each other...

<page-num>75</page-num>
# Chapter on Mean Motion

...have been shown. When the Moon resides at its **node** <term>pata: the point where a planet's orbit intersects the ecliptic</term>, it is seen exactly on the **ecliptic** <term>kranti vritta: the apparent path of the Sun through the stars</term>; at other times, it is seen shifted slightly to the north or south of the ecliptic. Exactly how far it has shifted at any given time can be easily determined through calculation. When the Moon is 90° ahead of or behind its node, it is at its maximum distance from the ecliptic. In Figure 4, this maximum distance is indicated by the labels *Chasa* or *Chas*. This is called the Moon's **maximum celestial latitude** <term>parama vikshepa: the greatest angular distance a planet reaches north or south of the ecliptic</term>. Similarly, other planets also shift north or south of the ecliptic, and their mean latitudes are provided in verses 66–70. There is a very close relationship between the latitudes of the planets and their nodes; for this reason, our ancient teachers believed that the node itself "pushes" the planets to the north or south.

The maximum latitudes of the planets are not the same according to the opinions of all teachers. The information obtained today through precision instruments does not match the values found in any of our ancient texts. For the sake of comparison, a table of maximum latitudes is provided below:

<page-num>76</page-num>

## Surya Siddhanta

From the table above, it will be seen that there is a significant difference between the modern values for the maximum **latitudes** <term>vikshepa: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> of Mercury and Venus and the values provided in the ancient Siddhantas. The reason for this is that modern latitude values are **heliocentric** (sun-centered), meaning they are calculated as they would appear from the center of the Sun. In contrast, the values in our Siddhantas are **geocentric** (earth-centered), meaning they are calculated as they appear when observed from the center of the Earth. Due to the difference in the observer's position, there is not much difference in the observed latitudes of those planets that are far from the Sun. However, for Mercury and Venus, which are close to the Sun, the difference in latitude becomes quite large, which will become clear from the example below:

->Figure 14<-

In the provided Figure 14, **R** (र) is the center of the Sun, **P** (प) is the center of the Earth, **P-R-Pa** (प र पा) represents the **ecliptic** <term>kranti-vritta: the apparent path of the Sun</term>, and **B-R-Ba** (ब र बा) represents the orbit of Mercury. When viewed from the Sun (**R**), the orbit of Mercury forms an angle **B-R-P** or **Ba-R-Pa** with the ecliptic, which according to modern views is 7°10'. However, when viewed from the center of the Earth (**P**), the orbit of Mercury appears to form the angle **B-P-R**, the value of which is much less than the angle **B-R-P**. This is because if **P-B** (the mean distance from Earth to Mercury) is 1, then **B-R** (the mean distance from the Sun to Mercury) is only 0.3871. Using **trigonometry** <term>trikonamiti: the study of triangles</term>, the value of angle **B-P-R** can be easily calculated, because in any triangle, the ratio of a side to the **sine** <term>jya: a chord-based trigonometric function used in ancient India</term> of its opposite angle is constant. Therefore:

| Sine ∠ BPR | | Sine ∠ BRP |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| BR | = | PB |

| Or, Sine ∠ BPR | = | (BR / PB) × Sine ∠ BRP |
| :--- | :---: | :--- |
| | = | 0.3871 / 1 × Sine 7°10' |
| | = | 0.3871 × 0.1218 |
| | = | 0.0472 |
| ∴ ∠ BPR | = | 2°42' |

<summary>This page explains why ancient Indian geocentric calculations for the latitudes of Mercury and Venus differ from modern heliocentric values, using a trigonometric proof to show how the observer's position on Earth alters the perceived angle.</summary>

<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, heliocentric, geocentric, Mercury, Venus, celestial latitude, trigonometry, sine, ecliptic</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page concludes the mathematical comparison between ancient Indian and modern astronomical values for planetary latitudes, specifically focusing on Mercury and Venus, and marks the end of the first chapter of the Surya Siddhanta commentary.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

<header>Chapter on Mean Motions</header>

According to modern views, this is the **geocentric** <term>bhukendragat: earth-centered</term> mean **latitude** <term>vikshepa: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> of Mercury, which is 10' <note>original: कला (kala), or minutes of arc</note> greater than the mean latitude recorded in the *Siddhanta Shiromani* <note>A significant 12th-century astronomical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>. The values found in the *Siddhanta Darpana* <note>A 19th-century astronomical work by Samanta Chandra Sekhar</note> align very closely with modern calculations.

Similarly, the mean **heliocentric** <term>ravikendragat: sun-centered</term> latitude of Venus is 3°23'37", and its mean distance from the Sun is 0.7233 (taking the Earth's distance as 1). Therefore, if in Figure 14 <note>referring to the diagram on the previous page</note> we replace the points 'B' and 'Ba' with 'V' and 'V₁' <note>original: शु (Shu) and शू (Shū) for Shukra, the Sanskrit name for Venus</note> to represent the orbit of Venus, the relationship will be as follows:

->0.7233<-
->sine ∠VES = —————— X sine 3°23'37"<-
->1<-
->= 0.7233 X 0.0592<-
->= 0.0428<-
->$\therefore$ ∠VES = 2°7'<-

<note>In the formula above, ∠VES represents the angle of the planet Venus as seen from Earth relative to the Sun.</note>

This result is 11' more than the 2°16' cited in the *Siddhanta Shiromani* and only 1' less than the 2°28' cited in the *Siddhanta Darpana*.

From this, it is evident that according to our ancient teachers, the mean latitudes of Mercury and Venus are only 10 or 11 **minutes** <term>kala: a unit of angular measurement equal to 1/60th of a degree</term> less than modern values. This degree of precision is remarkable considering the observational limitations of that era.

->The Scientific Commentary on the first chapter, titled "On Mean Motions,"<-
->of the **Surya Siddhanta** is now concluded.<-

### Page 79

[ **Verse 59**—The rule for determining the true **declination** <term>kranti: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</term> of a planet. **Verse 60**—The rule for determining the measure of the **day-and-night** <term>ahoratra: a full 24-hour cycle</term> of the planets. **Verse 61**—The method for determining the **sine of the diurnal radius** <term>dyujya: the radius of the small circle on which a planet moves in its daily rotation</term>. **Verses 62–63**—The method for determining the **earth-sine** <term>kshitijya: used in calculating the sun's rising and setting</term> and the **sine of ascensional difference** <term>chara jya: the difference between the actual time of sunrise and the time of sunrise at the equator</term>. **Verse 64**—The rule for determining the duration of day and night from the arc of the ascensional difference. **Verse 65**—The measure of the **lunar mansion** <term>nakshatra: the 27 or 28 divisions of the sky along the ecliptic</term> and the **lunar day** <term>tithi: a 30th part of a lunar month</term>, and the method for knowing which lunar mansion a planet is currently occupying. **Verse 66**—The method for knowing the **yoga** <term>yoga: a specific angular relationship between the sun and moon</term>. **Verse 67**—The method for knowing the lunar day. **Verse 68**—The names of the four fixed **karana** <term>karana: half of a lunar day (tithi)</term> and their respective times. **Verse 69**—How many cycles the seven movable half-lunar days make in a month; one half-lunar day is equal to one *karana*.]

In the first chapter, titled "The Calculation of Mean Positions" <note>original: मध्यमाधिकार (Madhyamadhikara)</note>, the method for determining the locations of planets according to their average or "mean" speed was explained. However, the position of a planet discovered through this method differs greatly from where the planet is actually seen in the sky. To eliminate this discrepancy, it is necessary to perform certain **corrections** <term>samskara: mathematical adjustments to account for orbital eccentricities and varying speeds</term>. This chapter explains how these corrections are carried out. Through these calculations, the position of the planets matches what is actually observed in the clear sky. For this reason, this chapter is named "The Calculation of True Positions" <note>original: स्पष्टाधिकार (Spashtadhikara)</note>.

> ->The invisible forms of time, residing in the zodiacal circles, known as the fast-apogee, the slow-apogee, and the node, are the causes of the planets' motions. (1)<-
> 
> ->Bound by cords of wind and pulled by their left and right hands, the planets are drawn toward them, forward or backward, according to their proximity and direction. (2)<-
> 
> ->The wind named **Pravaha** <term>pravaha: in ancient Indian cosmology, the celestial wind that drives the movement of the heavens</term> impels the planets toward their respective apogees. Thus pulled forward or backward, they attain diverse speeds. (3)<-

> ->If the apogee <note>original: उच्च (uchcha)</note> is situated in the first half of the circle in front of the planet, it pulls the planet forward; if it is in the other half-circle, it pulls the planet backward. (4)<-
> 
> ->Because the planets, being pulled by their apogees, move forward in the zodiac, that correction is called **positive** <note>original: धन (dhana: "wealth" or "plus")</note>, and it is called **negative** <note>original: ऋण (rina: "debt" or "minus")</note> when they are pulled backward. (5)<-

**Translation**—(1) The **fast-apogee** <term>shighrochcha</term>, the **slow-apogee** <term>mandochcha</term>, and the **node** <term>pata</term> are invisible incarnations of Time that revolve upon the ecliptic and are the causes of planetary motion. (2) These forms, using their right and left hands, pull the planets—which are tied to them by ropes made of air—toward themselves. If the form is to the east of the planet, it pulls it east; if to the west, it pulls it west, depending on the distance. (3) The wind called *Pravaha* also pushes these planets toward their apogees. For this reason, planets pulled toward the east or west...

# Surya Siddhanta
### Page 80

Variations in speed occur. (4) If a planet is to the east of its apogee <term>ucca: the point where a planet is furthest from Earth and moves slowest</term> and is not more than 6 signs or 180° away, the apogee pulls the planet eastward from its mean position. However, if it is more than 180° away (placing the apogee to the west of the planet), it pulls the planet toward the west. (5) The **true position** <term>spashta sthana</term> is determined by adding the amount the planet has been pulled eastward from its **mean position** <term>madhyama sthana</term>, or by subtracting the amount it has been lagged toward the west. The corrections that are added are called **positive corrections** <term>dhana sanskara</term>, and the corrections that are subtracted are called **negative corrections** <term>rina sanskara</term>. ||1-5||

### Scientific Commentary
In these five verses, as well as the following verses 6–11, our ancient teachers present their theories regarding "attraction" <note>original: "akarshan"</note>. These theories prove how subtle and precise their observations were. They observed that a planet in its orbit eventually reaches a point where its **true motion** becomes extremely slow. This specific point was identified as the planet's **slow apogee** <note>1</note> <term>mandocca</term>. The position of this apogee is not fixed; rather, it moves at an extremely slow pace. Therefore, it is considered **zodiac-dependent** <term>bhaganashrita</term>, meaning it moves along the **zodiac** <term>rashicakra</term>.

In the zodiac, the ordinary motion of planets is from west to east. When a planet reaches its slow apogee, its speed is at its lowest, making it slower than its average or **mean speed**. Consequently, as the planet moves forward from the apogee, it fails to reach the point where it should have been based on its mean speed for that day, falling behind instead. This creates a discrepancy between the planet's mean position and its true position. This difference increases daily and reaches its maximum when the planet is 90° ahead (to the east) of its apogee. After this, the difference begins to decrease, though the planet remains behind its mean position until it reaches 180° from the apogee. At 180° from the apogee, the mean and true positions of the planet coincide. 

From this, it is natural to conceptualize that when a planet is less than 180° east of its apogee, the apogee "pulls" it from its mean position toward the west (where the apogee is located). Therefore, by applying a **negative correction** to the mean position, the true position of the planet is found. As the planet moves further from the apogee, its true speed increases; thus, it was understood that the "attraction" of the apogee increases or decreases according to proximity.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Chapter Two of the Surya Siddhanta, building on the previous page's discussion of how the "slow apogee" (mandocca) influences planetary speed. ## Chapter on True Positions

When a planet reaches 180° from its <term>mandocca</term> <note>slow apogee: the point in a planet's orbit where it is furthest from Earth and moves at its slowest speed</note>, its speed is at its absolute maximum. This point is known as the planet's <term>nica sthana</term> <note>perigee: the point in an orbit where the planet is closest to Earth</note>. As the planet moves forward from this point, its daily <term>spashta gati</term> <note>true motion: the actual observed speed</note> remains greater than its <term>madhyama gati</term> <note>mean motion: the theoretical average speed</note>. Consequently, the planet travels further than it would be expected to reach by its average speed alone, and it continues to move ahead day by day. Therefore, the **true position** of the planet is determined by applying a <term>dhana sanskara</term> <note>positive correction</note> to its **mean position**.

When the planet moves beyond 180° from the slow apogee, the apogee is located within 180° to the east of the planet. In this position as well, the planet appears to be "pulled" toward the apogee. Because of this, the theory was confirmed that the apogee pulls the planet toward itself: if the apogee is in the east, it pulls the planet eastward; if it is in the west, it pulls the planet westward.

In the provided Figure 15, **U-M-Ni-Mi** represents the path of the Sun. **P** is the center of the <term>prithvi</term> <note>Earth</note>, which is not located at the center of the <term>surya marga</term> <note>the Sun's path or orbit</note>.

For the sake of convenience, we shall refer to a planet by two names: the <term>madhyama graha</term> <note>mean planet</note> and the <term>spashta graha</term> <note>true planet</note>. The difference between them is as follows: the **mean planet** is an imaginary body that is assumed to revolve around the Earth on the <term>rashicakra</term> <note>zodiac</note> at a constant <term>parikrama</term> <note>revolution or average speed</note>, whereas the **true planet** is that...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary motion from Chapter Two of the Surya Siddhanta. Having established the concepts of the slow apogee (mandocca) and the difference between mean and true speeds, the text now introduces the geometric logic for planetary corrections and transitions into the concept of the fast apogee (shighrocca) for the five non-luminary planets, comparing these ancient observations to Newtonian physics.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

A planet is that which is directly observed revolving around the Earth. The <term>madhyama gati</term> <note>mean motion: the theoretical constant average speed</note> of a planet is always uniform; however, the <term>spashta gati</term> <note>true motion: the actual observed speed</note> of a planet keeps increasing and decreasing. The average calculated from the daily true motions is what constitutes the mean motion. Therefore, it is evident that the true motion is sometimes less and sometimes more than the mean motion. When a planet is at its <term>mandocca</term> <note>slow apogee: the point in an orbit furthest from the center of attraction</note>, its true motion is at its slowest. At this specific point, the mean planet and the true planet coincide at the same position. However, moving forward from here, the mean planet—being faster than the true planet at this stage—moves ahead, while the true planet lags behind. In the diagram, **M** and **Ma** represent the positions of the mean Sun, while **S** and **Sa** represent the positions of the true Sun. Therefore, to determine the position of **S** or **Sa**, it is necessary to subtract a value from the position of **M** or **Ma**. When the mean Sun reaches the <term>Nica</term> <note>perigee: the point closest to Earth, 180° from the apogee</note>, or in other words, moves 180° ahead of the slow apogee, the true Sun is also observed at the perigee. At this point, the speed of the true Sun is at its maximum and is much faster than the mean Sun; therefore, moving past the perigee, the true Sun remains ahead of the mean Sun. **Si** and **See** represent the positions of the true Sun, and **Mi** and **Mee** represent the positions of the mean Sun. Here too, the true Sun appears shifted toward the apogee and is ahead of the mean Sun; therefore, to determine its position, it is necessary to add a value to the position of the mean Sun.

The difference between the mean and true positions of the Sun and Moon can be explained by this single concept alone. However, in the case of the five planets—Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn—there is a further variation in their mean and true positions. Consequently, alongside the concept of the <term>mandocca</term> <note>slow apogee</note>, the concept of the <term>shighrocca</term> <note>fast apogee: a correction factor related to the planet's relationship with the Sun</note> was also developed. According to <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> <note>a famous 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, the reasoning for this concept is as follows <insert>1</insert>:—

<margin>1. Siddhanta Shiromani, Ganitadhaya, page 20</margin>
> "When the Sun is ahead of these three planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—the true planets are ahead of the mean planets; that is, they appear pulled toward the Sun. However, when the Sun is behind them, the true planets lag behind the mean planets; that is, they appear pulled back toward the Sun." For this reason, scholars formulated the idea that the fast apogees (*shighrocca*) of these three planets stay with the Sun. It is therefore natural to infer that these planets are pulled by their fast apogees, which remain equal to or alongside the Sun. If this concept had been further expanded, and the Sun itself had been recognized as the fast apogee or the attractor of these planets, then <term>Newton’s Law</term> <note>the law of universal gravitation</note> would have been discovered.

Regarding the concepts of the <term>mandocca</term> and <term>shighrocca</term> positions mentioned above, knowing that the planets are somewhat pulled toward them leads to the natural inference that these positions are...
