<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Surya Siddhanta, moving from the mathematical calculation of planetary speeds to the underlying physical and metaphysical forces—such as the Pravaha wind and the nodes—that the ancients believed governed these motions. It concludes with a historical comparison between Indian astronomical tradition and the European Scientific Revolution.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions</header>

They possess a special power and are also invisible; for this reason, because they are understood to be especially powerful, they are called invisible divine forms. These forms are thought to hold the planets pulled toward themselves by a rope made of invisible wind, and they are also aided by the wind known as <term>Pravaha</term> <note>original: "प्रवह"; the celestial wind believed to drive the motion of the planets in the ancient geocentric model</note>.

The subject of the <term>Pata</term> <note>Node: the point where a planet's orbit intersects the ecliptic</note> has been discussed previously. Whatever was written there regarding the node of the Moon applies to the nodes of other planets as well. When a planet arrives at its north node, it is seen upon the <term>Kranti-vritta</term> <note>Ecliptic: the apparent circular path of the Sun</note>. As it moves forward from there, it becomes northern relative to the ecliptic. It remains north of the ecliptic until it reaches the south node—that is, until it has traveled 180° beyond the north node. In such a state, the north node remains to the west of the planet. For this reason, it is explained in the upcoming 7th verse that a node situated up to 180° to the west of a planet (the north node) pushes the planet toward the north, and a node situated up to 180° to the east pushes it toward the south. This too is invisible and appears to push the planet north or south from the ecliptic. Therefore, a divine power was attributed to this as well. However, the real reason is that the orbits of the Sun and the planets are not in the same plane, which makes the orbit of each planet appear to intersect the Sun's orbit at two points.

In the subsequent verses 9–11, it is explained that those planets which are larger in size, being heavy, are pulled less by their <term>Mandoccas</term> <note>Slow apogees</note>, <term>Shighroccas</term> <note>Fast apogees</note>, and so on, while those that are light are pulled significantly more. This inference is the result of subtle observation and is entirely consistent with the <term>Akarshana Siddhanta</term> <note>The Theory of Attraction or Gravity</note>.

Based on these eight verses of the <term>Surya Siddhanta</term> <note>An ancient and highly influential Sanskrit treatise on astronomy</note>, some scholars claim that Newton cannot be considered the discoverer of the theory of gravity, but rather it was our own ancient masters of astronomy. When considered impartially, it is proven that the inferences our venerable teachers made through direct observation, imagination, and logic were highly commendable given the conditions of that era. They certainly understood that the variation in the speed of planets was due to some force, but it could not then be known exactly how this force operated—whether it existed only in the Earth and the planets' <term>Shighroccas</term>, <term>Mandoccas</term>, and nodes, or in all matter in the universe—nor could its proof be demonstrated through mathematical operations. Newton is the discoverer of this universal law of attraction. There is no doubt that if the study and teaching of astronomy in India had continued in the same manner as it did during the time of <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> <note>A 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, <term>Ganesha Daivajna</term> <note>A 16th-century astronomer</note>, and others—or as it did in European countries like France, Germany, and England during the 16th and 17th centuries in the time of Copernicus, Tycho Brahe, Kepler, Newton, and others—

<meta>This page transitions from the ancient Indian metaphysical explanation of planetary motion (the invisible wind and divine forces) to a comparative analysis with Western classical mechanics. It contrasts the Indian "hypothesis" of attraction with the formalized "theories" of Newton, Copernicus, and Kepler, explaining the latter's laws of planetary motion in simple geometric terms.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...it is possible that the <term>Theory of Attraction</term> <note>Gravity; original: "आकर्षण सिद्धान्त"</note> would have been revealed to our ancient teachers in the same form that Newton later established. In our tradition, the idea of attraction remained only as a <term>Hypothesis</term> <note>original: "कल्पना"; an initial idea or supposition</note>, whereas Newton transformed it into a formal <term>Theory</term> <note>original: "सिद्धान्त"; a proven or established principle</note>.

Since we are considering the reasons for the varying speeds of the planets here, it would not be out of place to briefly explain the principles of Copernicus, Kepler, and Newton regarding planetary motion.

## The Hypothesis of Copernicus

In the book written by Copernicus in 1530 CE (1547 <term>Vikram Samvat</term> <note>The traditional Indian calendar system, which is 57 years ahead of the Gregorian calendar</note>), he demonstrated that if the Earth and other planets are assumed to orbit the Sun, then the observed "crooked" (retrograde) and "straight" (direct) motions of the planets can be easily explained. This is known as the Copernican Method.

## Kepler’s Laws

(1) The orbit in which a planet revolves around the Sun is in the shape of an <term>Ellipse</term> <note>original: "दीर्घवृत्त"; an elongated circle</note>, with the center of the Sun located at one of its <term>Foci</term> <note>original: "नाभि"</note>.

(2) A line connecting the centers of the Sun and any planet sweeps out equal areas in equal intervals of time.

(3) The ratio between the squares of the <term>Orbital Periods</term> <note>original: "भगणकाल"; the time taken to complete one full revolution</note> of two planets is the same as the ratio between the cubes of their mean distances from the Sun.

The following is a brief explanation of the calculations Kepler used to derive these laws.

Everyone experiences the fact that as an object moves further away, it appears to get smaller. This happens because as the distance increases, the angle that the object forms at the eye becomes smaller. Suppose 'N' is the position of the eye and 'A-B' is an object moving away. When it is at position 'A-B', it forms the angle 'A-N-B' at the eye. When it reaches the further position 'A1-B1', it forms the angle 'A1-N-B1', which is smaller than angle 'A-N-B'. For this reason, the same object appears smaller at position 'A1-B1', even though there has been no actual change in its size. (See Figure 16).

<summary>This page compares Indian concepts of "attraction" to Western astronomical developments, detailing Copernicus's heliocentric model and Kepler's three laws of planetary motion. It concludes by explaining the optical principle of perspective used by Kepler to calculate planetary distances.</summary>
<keywords>Newton, Copernicus, Kepler's Laws, Ellipse, Gravity, Orbital Period, Surya Siddhanta, Astronomy, Perspective</keywords>

<header>Chapter on True Motion</header>

<meta>The diagram illustrates the relationship between distance and angular diameter. A viewer at point 'Na' observes two objects of the same physical size at different distances. The closer object (Ka-Kha) subtends a larger angle than the distant object (Kaa-Khaa).</meta>

->Figure 16<-

If the <term>Solar Disk</term> <note>original: "सूर्य बिम्ब"; the visible circular shape of the sun</note> is observed and measured daily, it is found that it does not appear to be the same size every day. When the Sun is at approximately 16° in the <term>Sign of Sagittarius</term> <note>original: "धनु राशि"; the sun reaches this point around January 3rd</note>, its disk appears at its largest. On this day, the measurement of its disk is 32' 35.2" (minutes and seconds of arc). On this same day, its <term>Daily True Motion</term> <note>original: "दैनिक स्पष्ट गति"; the actual observed speed of the sun across the sky</note> is also at its fastest, namely 61' 8.6". Following this, the solar disk gradually appears smaller and its speed decreases. When the Sun is at approximately 16° in the <term>Sign of Gemini</term> <note>original: "मिथुन राशि"—180° away from the first position—the disk is at its smallest, measuring 31' 30.7", and its daily true motion is at its slowest, at 57' 11.5". The reason the disk appears larger or smaller is not that the Sun’s actual size changes, but rather that its distance from the Earth fluctuates. This view is also held by our traditional <term>Siddhantas</term> <note>Indian astronomical treatises</note>. <insert>1</insert>

If the value of the Sun's radius is *s* and the closest distance of the Sun from the Earth is *k*, then the <term>Sine</term> <note>original: "ज्या"</note> of the angle formed at the Earth by the Sun's semi-disk (angular radius) is: **Sine = s / k**

However, on this specific day the Sun's disk is 32' 35.2", therefore the semi-disk (angular radius) will be 16' 17.6".

->Therefore, **Sine 16' 17.6" = s / k**<-

But when an angle is very small, there is no difference between the value of the angle and the value of its sine, provided the angle is expressed in <term>Circular Measure</term> <note>Radians</note> or the sine is calculated according to the Indian method. <insert>2</insert>

# Surya Siddhanta

$\therefore \frac{s}{k} = 16' 17.6'' \text{ or } s = k \times 16' 17.6''$

Similarly, when the <term>Solar Disk</term> <note>original: "बिम्ब" (bimba)</note> is $31' 30.7''$, or the <term>Semi-diameter</term> <note>original: "बिम्बार्द्ध" (bimbarddha)</note> is $15' 45.4''$, then if the Sun’s maximum distance is represented as **'ka'**:

$\frac{s}{ka} = 15' 45.4'' \text{ or } s = ka \times 15' 45.4''$

$\therefore k \times 16' 17.6'' = ka \times 15' 45.4''$

$\text{Or, } \frac{k}{ka} = \frac{15' 45.4''}{16' 17.6''} \qquad \dots (1)$

The position where the Sun appears largest is $180^\circ$ away from the position where it appears smallest. Therefore, the distances $k$ and $ka$ derived above lie on a single straight line. Thus, if in the provided Figure 17, **P** represents the position of the Earth, then **S** and **Sa** will be the positions of the Sun when it appears largest and smallest respectively—that is, when $PS = k$ and $PSa = ka$.

-> S ———————————————————————— Sa <-
-> P   M <-
-> Figure 17 <-

If each side of equation (1) is subtracted from 1:

$1 - \frac{k}{ka} = 1 - \frac{15' 45.4''}{16' 17.6''}$

$\text{or } \frac{ka - k}{ka} = \frac{32.2''}{16' 17.6''} \qquad \dots (2)$

And if 1 is added to each side of equation (1):

$\frac{ka + k}{ka} = \frac{32' 3''}{16' 17.6''} \qquad \dots (3)$

Now, if we divide the corresponding sides of equation (2) by equation (3):

$\frac{ka - k}{ka + k} = \frac{32.2''}{32' 3''} = \frac{32.2''}{1923''} = \text{approximately } \frac{1}{60}$

This relationship demonstrates that **P** <note>the Earth</note> is the <term>Focus</term> <gloss>naabhi</gloss> of that <term>Ellipse</term> <gloss>dirghavritta</gloss> whose major axis is **S-Sa**, whose center is the midpoint **M** of **S-Sa**, and whose <term>Eccentricity</term> <gloss>chyuti</gloss> is $\frac{1}{60}$. This is because the distance from the center of an <term>Ellipse</term> to its <term>Focus</term> is the product of the semi-major axis and the...

<page-num>87</page-num>

# Chapter on True Motion
<note>original: "स्पष्टाधिकार" (spashtadhikara). This chapter explains how to calculate the actual (true) positions of celestial bodies as opposed to their mean or average positions.</note>

By dividing by half of the axis, the value of the <term>Eccentricity</term> <note>original: "च्युति" (chyuti); refers to how much the circular orbit is "off-center" or elongated</note> is obtained <gloss>1</gloss>. There, the distance from the center K to the focus is doubled, and [the sum of] 'ka + ka' represents the length of the <term>Major Axis</term> <note>original: "दीर्घ अक्ष" (deergha aksha); the longest diameter of an ellipse</note>.

In this way, if the distance S-Sa is taken as the <term>Major Axis</term>, P is taken as one <term>Focus</term> <note>original: "नाभि" (nabhi)</note> of it, and <unclear>1/60</unclear> is taken as the <term>Eccentricity</term>, then if an <term>Ellipse</term> <note>original: "दीर्घवृत्त" (deerghavritta)</note> is drawn, the distance of any <term>Radius Vector</term> <note>the line connecting the center of the Earth to the center of the Sun</note> P-Si, which forms an angle *E* <note>original: "इ" (i)</note> with the line S-P, can be determined by this formula <gloss>2</gloss>—

->Figure 18<-
<note>The diagram shows an elliptical orbit where 'P' represents Earth at one focus, and 'Si' represents the Sun's position along the curve.</note>

$$P Si = \frac{MS (1 - ch^2)}{1 + ch \times \text{Cosine } E}$$

Where *ch* <note>the eccentricity</note> = <unclear>1/60</unclear> = .0167 and *MS* is the constant mean distance between the Sun and the Earth.

Therefore, the value of $\frac{1}{P Si}$ changes according to the value of $1 + ch \times \text{Cosine } E$, which is written in brief as follows:

$$\frac{1}{k} \propto 1 + ch \cdot \text{Cosine } E$$

Where *k* is the distance of the Sun from the Earth (<term>Radius Vector</term>). This relationship corresponds exactly with observations <note>original: "वेध" (vedha); astronomical sightings or measurements</note>. Therefore, it is proven that the Sun revolves in an <term>Ellipse</term>—

<page-num>88</page-num>

# Surya Siddhanta

...is placed, and the Earth is at the **focus** <note>original: "नाभि" (nabhi)</note> of this **ellipse** <note>original: "दीर्घवृत्त" (deerghavritta)</note>. It is more accurate to say that the Earth, while revolving around the Sun, creates an elliptical orbit and the Sun’s center remains at the focus of this orbit. 

<note>The author is providing a modern heliocentric correction to the traditionally geocentric Surya Siddhanta text, noting that the mathematics of the ellipse remains the same regardless of which body is considered the center.</note>

The proof of this is given in the journal *Vijnana* <note>Science</note>, Volume 12, pages 75-76, 188-189, and 203 to 207. This is **Kepler’s First Law**.

It has been explained above that the Sun's maximum velocity <note>original: "तीव्रतम गति" (teevratama gati)</note> is 61′ 10″ and at this time its maximum apparent diameter <note>original: "महत्तम बिम्ब" (mahattama bimba)</note> is 32′ 35″; its minimum velocity is 57′ 12″ and at this time its minimum diameter is 31′ 31″. Therefore, it is clear that the difference between the maximum and minimum velocities is equal to $\frac{3' 58''}{59' 11''}$ of the mean velocity, or approximately <term>1/15</term> <note>original: "स्वल्पान्तर" (swalpantara); meaning a very small difference or a close approximation</note>. Furthermore, the difference between the maximum and minimum sizes of the apparent diameter is equal to $\frac{1' 4''}{32' 3''}$ of the mean diameter, or approximately <term>1/30</term>. Therefore, the ratio of the change in the apparent diameter is 1 : 1 + 1/30, and the ratio of the change in the apparent velocity is 1 : 1 + 1/15.

However, 1 + 1/15 = (1 + 1/30)² <gloss>approximately</gloss>
$\therefore$ The ratio of the change in velocity is 1 : (1 + 1/30)².

Whenever observed, it will be found that the angular velocity <note>original: "कोणीय वेग" (koniya vega)</note> of any planet varies according to the square of its apparent diameter. However, the apparent diameter of the Sun varies inversely with the Sun’s distance, as has been explained in the previous pages. Therefore, the angular velocity varies according to the square of the apparent diameter, or inversely according to the square of the **radius vector** <note>original: "कर्ण" (karna); literally 'hypotenuse,' used here to mean the distance between the Earth and Sun</note>. In short:

->Angular Velocity $\propto$ (Apparent Diameter)²<-
->or $\propto \left( \frac{1}{Radius Vector} \right)^2$<-

In Figure 18, **P** is the position of the Earth, **S** is the position of the Sun, and **S-P-Si** is the angle the Sun travels in one day. Similarly, **Sa** is another position of the Sun, and **Sa-P-Si** is the angle the Sun travels in one day. **S**, **Si** or **Sa**, **Si** are so close to each other that the difference between the values of **P-S** and **P-Si** is so small they can be considered equal. Similarly, **P-Sa** and **P-Si** can be considered equal. In such a state, the triangle **P-S-Si** can be considered a sector of a circle whose center is **P** and whose radius is **P-S** or **P-Si**. Therefore:

<summary>This page correlates the varying speed of the Sun with its distance from Earth, demonstrating that angular velocity is inversely proportional to the square of the distance, effectively proving Kepler's Second Law through the framework of the Surya Siddhanta.</summary>
<keywords>Kepler's First Law, angular velocity, apparent diameter, radius vector, ellipse, focus, Surya Siddhanta, Vijnana journal</keywords>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Motions</header>

## Chapter on True Motions

->Figure 16<-

The area of this <term>circular sector</term> <note>original: "वृत्त खंड" (vritta khanda); literally a segment or sector of a circle</note> = ∠ S P Si × $\frac{(PS)^2}{2}$

= <term>Angular velocity</term> <note>original: "कोणीय वेग" (koniya vega)</note> at position S × $\frac{(PS)^2}{2}$

And the area of sector S' P Ci = ∠ S' P Ci × $\frac{(PS')^2}{2}$

= Angular velocity at position S' × $\frac{(PS')^2}{2}$

However, it has already been stated above that:

Angular velocity ∝ $\frac{1}{(\text{radius vector})^2}$ <note>original: "कर्ण" (karna); usually means hypotenuse, but in Indian astronomy refers to the distance between the center and the planet</note> = $k \times \frac{1}{(\text{radius vector})^2}$ where $k$ is a <term>constant</term> <note>original: "अचल राशि" (achala rashi); a quantity that does not change</note>.

<margin>is.</margin>

∴ $\frac{\text{Area of circular sector S P Si}}{\text{Area of circular sector S' P Ci}}$

= $\frac{\text{Angular velocity of S} \times \frac{(PS)^2}{2}}{\text{Angular velocity of S'} \times \frac{(PS')^2}{2}}$

= $\frac{\text{Angular velocity of S} \times (PS)^2}{\text{Angular velocity of S'} \times (PS')^2}$

<note>This mathematical derivation is showing that the area swept by a planet is proportional to its angular velocity multiplied by the square of its distance from the center. By substituting the earlier established rule that angular velocity is inversely proportional to the square of the distance, the author is proving Kepler's Second Law—that a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times.</note>

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof relating angular velocity to the area of a sector, demonstrating that the area swept by a planet (the Sun in this geocentric model) remains constant relative to time.</summary>
<keywords>angular velocity, circular sector, radius vector, mathematical proof, Surya Siddhanta, planetary motion</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof of Kepler’s laws within the context of the Surya Siddhanta. Having established the relationship between angular velocity and the radius vector on page 89, the author now completes the proof for the Second Law and introduces a practical calculation using the Third Law (the Harmonic Law) to determine the distance of Venus from the Sun.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

= $\frac{\text{Angular velocity of S} \times (\text{Radius vector of S})^2}{\text{Angular velocity of S'} \times (\text{Radius vector of S'})^2}$  
---------------------------------------  
= $\frac{k \times \frac{1}{(\text{Radius vector of S})^2} \times (\text{Radius vector of S})^2}{k \times \frac{1}{(\text{Radius vector of S'})^2} \times (\text{Radius vector of S'})^2}$ <note>The author uses 'अ' as a constant (k) to show that the variables cancel out.</note>

$= 1$

This proves that the <term>circular sectors</term> <note>original: "वृत्त खंड" (vritta khanda)</note> S P Si and S' P Ci are both equal. This is **Kepler’s Second Law** <note>Also known as the Law of Equal Areas, which states that a line segment joining a planet and the Sun sweeps out equal areas during equal intervals of time.</note>.

Through **Kepler’s Third Law**, the relationship between the distances of all planets from the Sun can be understood. For example, the <term>orbital periods</term> <note>original: "भगण काल" (bhagana kala); the time it takes to complete one full revolution around the Sun</note> of <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "शुक्र" (Shukra)</note> and <term>Earth</term> <note>original: "पृथ्वी" (Prithvi)</note> are 224.7 days and 365.3 days respectively. Therefore, the ratio of the squares of their orbital periods is:

$= \frac{(365.3)^2}{(224.7)^2} = 2.645$

However, according to Kepler’s Third Law:  
$\frac{(\text{Distance of Earth from Sun})^3}{(\text{Distance of Venus from Sun})^3} = \frac{(365.3)^2}{(224.7)^2} = 2.645$

If the distance from the Sun to the Earth is taken as 1 <note>This is known as 1 Astronomical Unit (AU).</note>, then:  
Distance of Venus from the Sun $= \left(\frac{1}{2.645}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$  
$= (.378)^{\frac{1}{3}}$  
$= \left(\frac{378}{1000}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}} = \frac{1}{10} (378)^{\frac{1}{3}}$  
$= \frac{1}{10} \times (7^3 + 35)^{\frac{1}{3}} = \frac{1}{10} \times 7 \left( 1 + \frac{5}{7^2} \right)^{\frac{1}{3}}$  
$= \frac{7}{10} (1 + .102)^{\frac{1}{3}}$  
$= \frac{7}{10} \{ 1 + \frac{1}{3} \times (.102) + \frac{1}{3} \times \frac{1}{3} (\frac{1}{3} - 1) (.102)^2$  
$+ \frac{1}{3} \times \frac{1}{3} \times (\frac{1}{3} - 1) (\frac{1}{3} - 2) (.102)^3 + \dots \}$ <note>The author is using a binomial expansion to calculate the cube root manually.</note>  
$= \frac{7}{10} (1 + .034 - .00116 + .000066$  
—a very infinitesimal number)  
$= \frac{7}{10} \times 1.0326 = \frac{7.2303}{10} = .723$

<note>The calculated value of 0.723 AU is remarkably close to the modern accepted average distance of Venus from the Sun (approx. 0.7233 AU).</note>

**Vocabulary:** Surya Siddhanta, angular velocity, radius vector (hypotenuse), Kepler’s Law, orbital period, Venus, Earth, infinitesimal number.

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the True Positions of Planets</header>

# Chapter on the True Positions of Planets
<note>original: "Spashtadhikara." In Indian astronomy, this chapter typically deals with calculating the true positions of planets by applying corrections to their mean positions.</note>

Kepler formulated these three laws from minute observations of the planets between 1664–1674 <term>Vikram Samvat</term> <note>The Hindu lunar calendar; these years correspond to 1609–1619 AD</note>. At the time, he was unaware of the specific forces that caused the planets to move according to these rules. For approximately 75 years, the underlying proof or cause of these laws could not be explained. Afterward, Isaac Newton proved that universal gravitation is the root cause of all these phenomena. The three principles upon which Newton established this proof are known as the **Laws of Motion**, and they remain famous under his name:

**First Law**—Unless acted upon by an external force, every object persists in its state of rest or in a state of uniform motion in a straight line.

**Second Law**—The change in motion is proportional to the magnitude of the force applied, and this change occurs in the direction of the straight line in which the force is exerted.

**Third Law**—For every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction. That is, every action produces a reaction that is equal in magnitude but opposite in direction.

These laws are considered self-evident. For more detailed information, one should consult a text on <term>Dynamics</term> <note>original: "Gativijnana"</note>.

Using Kepler’s first and second laws, Newton proved that every planet moves because of a force directed toward the Sun, and that the magnitude of this force is inversely proportional to the square of the distance between the Sun and the planet. From Kepler’s third law, Newton demonstrated how the acceleration of one planet relates to the acceleration of another, and from this, he derived the Principle of Universal Gravitation, which is as follows:

Every particle of <term>matter</term> <note>original: "Dravya"</note> attracts every other particle with a force that varies directly as the product of their masses and inversely as the square of the distance between them.

It must now be proved that if a line is drawn from a fixed point to a moving particle, and that line sweeps out equal areas in equal intervals of time, then the force acting on that particle is directed toward that same fixed point. This can be explained briefly using the principles of <term>Differential Calculus</term> <note>original: "Chalankalan"</note> and Dynamics.

<vocab>Kepler, Newton, Gravitation, Laws of Motion, Dynamics, Matter, Differential Calculus</vocab>

<summary>This page connects Kepler’s observational planetary laws to Newton’s physical laws of motion and gravitation, explaining that gravity is the force governing planetary orbits. It introduces the three Laws of Motion and the Law of Universal Gravitation as the mathematical foundation for modern orbital mechanics.</summary>

<keywords>Kepler's Laws, Isaac Newton, Laws of Motion, Universal Gravitation, Dynamics, Differential Calculus, Indian Astronomy, Spashtadhikara</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sir Isaac Newton’s proofs for planetary motion. The text bridges traditional Indian astronomical terminology with 19th-century Western scientific education.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>Surya-Siddhanta</header>

<note>original: "Surya-Siddhanta." This is a foundational Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy, though this specific text is a later commentary integrating modern physics.</note>

It can be proven, as will be shown later. In this section, it is proven in some detail based on simple mathematics. <gloss>1</gloss>

Suppose that 'S' <note>The Hindi text uses the letter 'स' (Sa), likely standing for "Surya" or Sun/Center.</note> is a fixed point, and a particle of an object, while revolving around 'S', creates a <term>polygon</term> <note>a plane figure with at least three straight sides and angles.</note> K-Kh-G-Gh-Ch <note>The Hindi letters क, ख, ग, घ, च act as labels for the vertices of the shape.</note>. Let us assume that the sides K-Kh, G-Gh, or Gh-Ch are traveled in equal amounts of time, or within 1 <term>pal</term> <note>A traditional Indian unit of time, approximately 24 seconds.</note>. Also, assume that the values of these sides are different, and as long as the particle remains on any one side, its motion remains **uniform**. The <term>areas</term> of the <term>triangles</term> S-K-Kh, S-Kh-G, S-G-Gh, and S-Gh-Ch should also be understood as equal.

Now it is evident that, while revolving around 'S', the particle creates equal areas in equal intervals of time. According to the First Law of Motion, as long as the particle is in the polygon—

# Section on True Motions

As long as [the object] is forming a straight side of the figure, no force is acting upon it and it moves in a straight line with its acquired power. However, the moment it begins to turn from one side to another, some amount of force must certainly act upon it for an instant, so that it may change its previous straight course and transition to another straight path.

Observe the condition at the moment the particle is at point Kh <note>क=K, ख=Kh, ग=G, etc., are used as geometric labels.</note>. If no force were applied at this moment, in the next instant it would travel along path Kh-P in the same line as K-Kh, and the line K-Kh-P would be a straight line where Kh-P and K-Kh are equal, as there would be no change in motion. Connect P to G and S. The base of triangle S-Kh-P is Kh-P, which is equal to K-Kh and lies on the same straight line; therefore, according to <term>geometry</term> <note>original: रेखा-गणित (rekha-ganit)</note>, the areas of both triangles S-K-Kh and S-Kh-P are equal. It was assumed at the beginning that the areas of triangles S-K-Kh, S-Kh-G, etc., are equal. Thus, it is proven that triangles S-Kh-P and S-Kh-G are also equal to each other. Since they share the same base S-Kh, according to geometry, these two triangles lie between parallel lines—that is, the line G-P is parallel to S-Kh. Draw line G-R parallel to Kh-P such that it meets line S-Kh at point R. Then, Kh-P-G-R will be a parallelogram. 

Had no force been applied when the particle was at Kh, it would have reached point P; but because a force was applied, it reached G instead. Therefore, it is clear that the initial velocity of the particle at Kh was Kh-P, and due to the application of force, it changed to Kh-G. Thus, according to the mechanics principle of the <term>‘parallelogram of velocities’</term> <note>original: गति के समानान्तर चतुर्भुज-नियम (gati ke samānāntar chaturbhuj-niyam)</note>, the motion Kh-R was combined with the motion Kh-P due to the applied force. That is, the velocity the particle had at point Kh in the direction of Kh-P was joined by another velocity equal to Kh-R in the direction of Kh-R, causing the particle to reach point G. Therefore, because of this applied force, the object turned toward S. In this same way, it can be proven that the force acting at the vertices G, Gh, and Ch of the polygon also acts in the direction of S.

Now, imagine that this polygon is made of millions of extremely small sides, and the particle revolving around S completes each tiny side in a millionth of a second. It is then evident that the force will act upon that particle toward S millions of times. Therefore, it is proven that the particle follows a <term>curved</term> path towards S in a <term>continuous</term> <note>original: अनवच्छिन्न (anavachhinn)</note> (continuo...

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof showing that a particle moving under a central force (directed toward point S) will sweep out equal areas in equal times, moving from a polygonal path to a continuous curve.</summary>

-> # Surya-Siddhanta <-

...completed due to the force. If the imagination is stretched further and the sides of the polygon become so small that they cannot be bounded by any limit, and their number becomes infinite, even then this logic can be applied. Therefore, it is proven that if any particle moves around a fixed point in such a path that it creates equal areas in equal times, then the force constantly acting upon that particle is in the direction of that fixed point—that is, the fixed point is constantly attracting that particle.

If S <note>original: स (sa)</note> is assumed to be the center of the Sun, and K, Kh, G <note>original: क, ख, ग (ka, kha, ga)</note>, etc., are the positions of a planet, then it is proven by Kepler's Second Law that the force working to hold the planets revolving around the Sun is the attraction of the **Sun** <insert>Sun's</insert> itself. Similarly, planets are also pulling their satellites.

-> *In this way, Rahu—the node—by its own velocity, deflects the Moon and other planets to the North and South, away from the ecliptic, creating a deviation.* [6] <-

-> *When the node is west of the planet but at a distance of less than 6 signs or 180°, it deflects it North from the ecliptic; when it is east of the planet but at a distance of less than 6 signs, it pulls it South from the ecliptic.* [7] <-

**Translation—** (6) The nodes <note>original: पात (pata)</note>, also called Rahu, shift the Moon and other planets North or South from the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the sun's apparent path; original: क्रान्तिवृत्त (kranti-vritta)</note> by their own velocity, equal to the <term>celestial latitude</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic; original: विक्षेप (vikshepa)</note>. (7) When the node is west of the planet but within a distance of less than 6 signs or 180°, it shifts the planet North of the ecliptic. When it is East of the planet but within a distance of 6 signs, it pulls the planet South of the ecliptic.

**Scientific Commentary—** The simple meaning of these two verses is that by observing the positions of the planet and its node, one should understand whether the planet is exactly on the ecliptic or shifted somewhat to the North or South. If both the planet and the node are in the same place, it should be understood that the planet is on the ecliptic. If the planet is ahead of (East of) the node but not more than 180° away, it will be shifted North from the ecliptic. If the planet is behind (West of) the node but not more than 180° away, it will be shifted South from the ecliptic. The reason for this is not an attraction or repulsion by a physical Rahu, but rather that the orbit of any planet is not in the same plane as the ecliptic; therefore, the planet does not always remain on the ecliptic. The two points where the orbit of a planet and the ecliptic appear to meet are called the <term>nodes</term> <note>original: पात (pata)</note>. When the planet is at these two points in its orbit, it is seen on the ecliptic; otherwise, [it is away from] the ecliptic...

# Chapter on True Positions

...is to the North or South according to what was said above. The distance of a planet North or South from the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the sun's apparent path; original: kranti-vritta</note> is called <term>celestial latitude</term> <note>original: vikshepa</note>. This occurs on the circle that passes through the <term>pole of the ecliptic</term> <note>original: kadamba</note> and is at a right angle to the ecliptic.

->For Mercury and Venus, when the node is situated in the same way relative to their fast apogee,<-
->then by the attraction of that fast apogee, they are deflected as stated. [8]<-

**Translation—(8)** When the nodes of Mercury and Venus are positioned relative to their <term>fast apogees</term> <note>the point of swiftest motion or conjunction; original: shighrochcha</note> according to the rules (written in verses 6 and 7), then by the attraction exerted at those fast apogees, the planets are moved North or South from the ecliptic in the same manner.

**Science Commentary—** The rule stated in verses 6 and 7 is only applicable to the Sun, Moon, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn. To determine the positions of the two planets Mercury and Venus, one must observe where and how far their fast apogee nodes are. If the fast apogee node is to the East but less than 180° away, the planet will be North of the ecliptic; if it is to the West but less than 180° away, the planet will be South of the ecliptic.

---

->Because the Sun’s disk is very large, it is pulled only slightly.<-
->Because the Moon’s disk is small, it is pulled much more than that. [9]<-
->Because Mars and the others have small bodies, they are pulled<-
->very far and with great speed by the deities known as the Fast and Slow Apogees. [10]<-
->Therefore, a great addition or subtraction must be made according to their motion.<-
->In this way, they travel in the sky, struck by the winds and drawn by those forces. [11]<-

**Translation—(9)** The Sun’s orb is very large; therefore, it is pulled very little by its apogee. The Moon’s orb is small, so it is pulled significantly. (10) The orbs of planets like Mars are very small; therefore, the deities of their <term>fast apogees</term> <note>original: shighrochcha</note> and <term>slow apogees</term> <note>the point of slowest motion; original: mandochcha</note> pull them very far with great velocity. (11) Consequently, a significant <term>addition</term> <note>positive correction; original: dhana</note> or <term>subtraction</term> <note>negative correction; original: rina</note> must be applied to their positions due to their motion. In this way, these planets travel through the sky, pulled by their fast and slow apogee deities and pushed by the <term>provector wind</term> <note>the cosmic wind believed to drive celestial bodies; original: pravaha-vayu</note>.

**Science Commentary—** Our ancient teachers observed that, compared to the Sun, the Moon deviates much further to the East or West from its mean position, and planets such as Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn are seen much further to the East or West from their mean positions than the Moon is.

# Chapter on True Positions

Therefore, they considered these planetary spheres to be even smaller than the Moon, as they appear to the naked eye, and concluded that because their spheres are so small, they are greatly affected by the gravitational-like pull of the <term>apogees of fast motion</term> <note>original: sighrochchos; the point in an orbit where a planet appears to move fastest, related to the Earth's own motion in modern terms</note> and the <term>apogees of slow motion</term> <note>original: mandochchos; the point of a planet's orbit furthest from the center of attraction, equivalent to the aphelion</note>.

However, the real reasons for planets appearing slightly to the east or west of their <term>mean position</term> <note>the calculated position of a planet assuming a perfectly circular and uniform orbit</note> are the actual shapes of the planetary orbits. The orbits of the planets are in the shape of an <term>ellipse</term> <note>original: deergh-vritt</note>, and the values of their <term>eccentricity</term> <gloss>(deviation from a perfect circle)</gloss> are not all the same. It is primarily because of this that there is a difference between the mean and true positions, not because of the physical size of the planetary spheres. The knowledge of their dimensions will be clear from the table on the previous page, which was taken from page 492 of Robert Ball's <term>‘Spherical Astronomy’</term>. The radius of the Moon is 1,076 miles.

->Retrograde, somewhat retrograde, crooked, slow, slower, and mean;<-
->likewise, faster and fast: these are the eight motions of the planets. (12)<-
->Among these, very fast, fast, slow, slower, and mean<-
->are known as the five direct motions; what is retrograde includes "somewhat retrograde." (13)<-

**Translation**—(12) There are eight types of motions in planets called <term>retrograde</term> <note>backward motion</note>, <term>somewhat retrograde</term>, <term>crooked</term>, <term>slow</term>, <term>slower</term>, <term>mean</term>, <term>faster</term>, and <term>fast</term>. (13) Among these, the very fast, fast, slow, slower, and mean motions are "straight" (direct); that is, when a planet undergoes these motions, it appears to move from west to east in the <term>zodiac</term>. The "somewhat retrograde" and "crooked" motions associated with "retrograde" are called retrograde motion because when a planet undergoes such motions, it appears to move backward from east to west in the zodiac. When a planet has direct motion, it is called <term>margi</term> <note>moving forward</note>, and when it has retrograde motion, it is called <term>vakri</term> <note>moving backward</note>.

**Scientific Commentary**—The reason why these different motions occur in a planet is not explained anywhere in our traditional treatises <note>Siddhantas</note>, because as long as the Earth is considered stationary, the cause cannot be properly explained. However, if the Earth is also assumed to <term>revolve</term> around the Sun like other planets—which has been proven by several experiments—then it can be easily understood how these eight motions appear in any planet; even though, in reality, the planet is continuously revolving around the Sun from west to east. Regarding this, I will quote here what I wrote in the journal ‘Vigyan’ (Science), Volume 13, pages 264-266.

# Surya Siddhanta

If you were to plant a flag in an open field and construct several circular embankments, each two or three feet high, at distances of 4, 7, 10, 15, 52, 85, 162, and 300 furlongs from the flag <note>these distances correspond to the relative distances of the planets from the Sun in astronomical units: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune</note>; and if you stationed a horseman at each circle; and if you yourself stood by the flag and commanded the horsemen to line up in a single straight row and then begin galloping around their respective circles at such a speed that the closest one completes a lap in 88 seconds, the next in 225 seconds, the third in 365 seconds, the fourth in 687 seconds, the fifth in 4,332 seconds, the sixth in 10,756 seconds, the seventh in 510 minutes, and the eighth in 1,003 minutes <note>these times in seconds and minutes represent the orbital periods of the planets in days</note>—then, just as these horsemen would appear to you to be circling in seconds, the planets appear to revolve around the Sun in the solar system in days. The only difference is that while the horsemen move on a single flat plane, the planets also shift slightly to the north and south.

If, instead of standing by the flag, you were to mount the third horse and begin circling at the speed mentioned earlier, the view you would have of the flag and the other horsemen is exactly what we inhabitants of Earth see when observing the planets circling the Sun. Sometimes it appears that a planet is moving forward, sometimes it seems to be falling behind, and at other times it even appears to stand still.

From the example of the horsemen above, you will have understood that if a rider sits on the third horse and orbits the flag, they will observe the same peculiarities in the movement of the horses both inside and outside their path—motions described as <term>“retrograde, somewhat retrograde, and crooked”</term> <note>original: ‘vakrānuvakrā kuṭilā’</note> as well as <term>“fast and faster”</term> <note>original: ‘śīghrā śīghratarā’</note>. This is the same eccentricity that the inhabitants of Earth (the "Earth-horse") observe in the movements of the other planets. To explain this, we must take two examples: one of a planet located between the Earth and the Sun, and another of a planet located outside the Earth’s orbit. For the former, Mercury is shown in Figure 21, and for the latter, Mars is shown in Figure 23.

In Figure 21, the largest circle represents the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>original: rāśi-cakra</note>, upon which the Sun appears to complete one revolution in a year. The point marked as the <term>Vernal Equinox</term> <note>original: vasant-viṣuva; the time when day and night are of equal length</note> is where the Sun appears at the beginning of the spring season. Starting from this point, the Zodiac is divided into twelve parts. Therefore, the sections labeled as Aries, Taurus, and so on, should be understood as <term>Tropical Aries</term> <gloss>original: Sāyaṇa Meṣa; the zodiac sign adjusted for the precession of equinoxes</gloss> and <term>Tropical Taurus</term> <gloss>original: Sāyaṇa Vṛṣa</gloss>. The beginning of Tropical Aries occurs on March 21st or 22nd.

# The Chapter on True Positions
<term>Spashtadhikara: the specific chapter in Indian astronomy (Siddhanta) dealing with the calculation of the "true" positions of celestial bodies as opposed to their "mean" or average positions.</term>

### The Vernal Equinox
<term>Vasanta Vishuva: the spring equinox, occurring around March 21st, when the Sun crosses the celestial equator and day and night are of equal length.</term>

->Figure 21—Earth's paths, its orbit, and other motions<-

The Sidereal <term>Nirayana: a system of astronomy that calculates positions relative to fixed stars</term> month of Aries begins approximately 23 degrees further along from the Tropical <term>Sayana: a system that calculates positions relative to the moving equinoxes</term> Aries. This usually falls on the 13th or 14th of April <note>this refers to the difference caused by the precession of the equinoxes, known in Sanskrit as Ayanamsha</note>. In the zodiac, the Sun appears to move through the signs, traveling from Aries to Taurus, Taurus to Gemini, and so on.

In the zodiac diagram, the smaller circle represents the Earth's orbit <term>Bhukaksha</term>. The Earth moves along this path, completing one full revolution around the Sun (marked as 'S'), which is located at the center, in the span of one year. To an observer living on the Sun, the Earth would also appear to be traveling through the signs of Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, and the rest. It is because of this actual motion of the Earth that the Sun appears to us to be the one moving. In Figure 21, this movement is shown starting from the point 'P' and...

<page-num>100</page-num>
# Surya-Siddhanta
<note>original: "सूर्य-सिद्धान्त" — The "Sun-Treatise," one of the foundational texts of Indian astronomy, dating back at least to the 6th century CE.</note>

The Earth moves in a direction opposite to that of a clock's hands (counter-clockwise) through points 2, 3, 4, and so on. The direction of this travel can be identified by the direction of the arrow. Because the Earth's daily motion is uneven <note>meaning the Earth does not move at a perfectly constant speed throughout the year due to its elliptical orbit</note>, the points on the Earth's orbit <term>Bhukaksha: the 'room' or path of the Earth</term> are shown at unequal intervals.

The smallest circle represents the orbit of the planet **Mercury** <term>Budha: the planet Mercury, associated in Sanskrit with the word for 'intellect' or 'wisdom'</term>. Let us assume that Mercury begins its journey from point B₁ and travels counter-clockwise through points 2, 3, 4, and so on; to inhabitants of the Sun, it would appear to move through the <term>Rashi: the twelve signs of the zodiac, such as Aries, Taurus, and Gemini</term> signs of Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc. In the diagram, B₁ is marked at the position Mercury occupies when the **Earth** <term>Prithvi</term> is at point P₁. When Mercury moves to point 2, the Earth also moves to point 2 in its own orbit. When Mercury moves from point 2 to point 3, the Earth similarly moves from point 2 to point 3. This same logic should be applied to the other points; for instance, when Mercury is at point 7 in its orbit, the Earth is also at point 7 in its orbit, and so on.

If one wishes to see in which direction Mercury will appear on the **zodiac** <term>Rashi-chakra: the 'wheel of signs' or the celestial belt containing the twelve zodiac constellations</term> when viewed from Earth, one must draw a line connecting the points where Mercury and the Earth are located at that moment and extend that line until it reaches the zodiac. The place where this line touches the zodiac is the position of Mercury. For the sake of visual clarity in the diagram, the solid lines connecting the Earth and Mercury are not shown, but they are represented by dashed lines extending from Mercury to the zodiac.

For example, when the Earth is at P₁ and Mercury is at B₁, the line connecting P₁ and B₁ reaches point 1 on the zodiac; this means that to those living on Earth, Mercury will appear at point 1 of the zodiac, or at the end of the sign of **Aquarius** <term>Kumbha: the 'water-pot' sign of the zodiac</term>. When the Earth reaches P₂, Mercury reaches B₂, and it appears at point 2 on the zodiac, or within the sign of **Pisces** <term>Meena: the 'fish' sign of the zodiac</term>. From point P₃ (that is, when the Earth is at point 3 in its orbit), Mercury is at B₃ and will appear at point 3 on the zodiac, at the end of Pisces. When Mercury is at point 4 in its orbit, the Earth will also be at point 4, and Mercury will appear to Earth's inhabitants at point 4 on the zodiac—which is only slightly ahead of point 3.

When Mercury moved from 1 to 2 in its orbit, the Earth also moved from 1 to 2, and we observed Mercury moving from Aquarius into Pisces on the zodiac. When Mercury moved from 2 to 3 in its orbit, the Earth moved from 2 to 3, and to the inhabitants here, Mercury appeared to move further within the sign of Pisces from point 2 to 3. However, this time, Mercury did not advance as far across the zodiac as it did previously; in other words, the speed of Mercury...

<page-num>101</page-num>
# Chapter on True Planetary Motions
<note>original: "Spashtadhikara" — the section of the text dealing with the "true" or "corrected" positions of planets as they appear in the sky, accounting for their varying speeds.</note>

Mercury's speed has already slowed down from its previous state. In moving from point 3 to 4, Mercury's position in the <term>Rashi-chakra: the 'Wheel of Signs' or the Zodiac</term> has advanced very little; therefore, it would be no exaggeration to say that Mercury’s motion is almost non-existent. In this condition, Mercury appears to be somewhat stationary. When Mercury moves from 4 to 5 in its own orbit, and the Earth also moves from 4 to 5 in its orbit, for the inhabitants of Earth, Mercury will appear to go backward in the zodiac from 4 to 5. This means Mercury has become <term>Vakri: 'crooked' or retrograde; the appearance of a planet moving backward against the stars</term>. 

When Mercury moves from 5 to 6 in its orbit, the Earth also moves from 5 to 6, and to us, Mercury will appear to move backward in the zodiac from 5 to 6, traveling from the sign of <term>Meena: Pisces</term> into <term>Kumbha: Aquarius</term>, but its speed will appear very rapid. Here too, Mercury is said to be retrograde, even though it is moving in its usual sequence within its own orbit. As Mercury moves from 6 to 7, it appears to move backward in the zodiac from 6 to 7. However, while moving from 8 to 9 in its orbit, it will appear to move forward in the zodiac from 8 to 9—meaning Mercury’s motion will become <term>Margi: 'on the path' or direct motion</term>—though it will remain very slow. 

Now it should be clear that although from the perspective of the Sun, both Mercury and Earth appear to move in the same direction, to the inhabitants of Earth, Mercury appears to advance from 1 to 4 and move backward from 4 to 7. When it moves forward, it is called direct, and when it moves backward, it becomes retrograde. Even when it is direct, its speed does not appear uniform; rather, sometimes it seems to increase rapidly, sometimes it slows down, and sometimes it appears to stand still. And when it is retrograde, the speed also appears fast, faster, slow, slower, or almost stationary.

There is one more point worth knowing here. When the Earth is at point P¹ and Mercury is at B¹, the line connecting the Sun and the Earth reaches the end of <term>Makara: Capricorn</term>—meaning the Sun appears to be in Capricorn—but Mercury appears at the end of Aquarius. Therefore, Mercury stays to the east of the Sun and is visible in the west after sunset. From P², the Sun appears at the beginning of the sign of Aquarius and Mercury at the beginning of Pisces. From P³, the Sun appears to have advanced a bit further into Aquarius, but Mercury appears to have reached the end of Pisces; it is near this point that the distance between the Sun and Mercury is at its greatest. In such a state, if the line connecting the Earth and Mercury were extended, it would touch Mercury's orbit <note>meaning the line is tangent to the orbit</note>, and the angle it makes with the line connecting the Earth and the Sun would be at its maximum. This is called the **Greatest elongation** <note>original: "Mahattam Antar"</note> of the Sun and Mercury, and this distance occurs to the east of the Sun. A few days after this greatest elongation, Mercury's motion becomes retrograde and the distance...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mercury's orbital mechanics. While the previous page (101) focused on the visual phenomenon of retrograde motion and how Mercury appears to move backward in the zodiac, this page (102) explains the specific conditions of Mercury's conjunctions with the Sun—both "Inferior" and "Superior"—and how its distance from Earth affects its visibility from our perspective.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
->**Surya Siddhanta**<-
<note>The "Sun-Tradition" or "Sun-System," one of the most significant Sanskrit treatises on Indian astronomy.</note>

The distance begins to decrease, and as it diminishes, Mercury comes between the Earth and the Sun—that is, the difference in their positions becomes zero. In this state, Mercury rises and sets simultaneously with the Sun. This is known as Mercury’s <term>Inferior Conjunction</term> <note>original: "Bhitari Yuti" — literally 'inner union'; this occurs when Mercury passes between the Earth and the Sun.</note>. When the distance between Mercury and the Sun becomes approximately 12°, Mercury can no longer be seen with the naked eye because it is so close to the Sun that its light is overwhelmed by the Sun's brilliance. Therefore, it is said that Mercury "sets in the West," because Mercury disappears while being visible in the western sky. Shortly before the time of the <term>Inferior Conjunction</term>, while in <term>Retrograde</term> <note>original: "Vakri" — 'crooked' or backward motion</note> motion, Mercury moves toward the right hand and the Sun toward the left; therefore, Mercury moves away from the Sun very quickly. This means that only a few days after setting in the West, it moves to the West of the Sun and begins to appear in the East, rising before sunrise. At that point, it is said that Mercury has "risen in the East." When Mercury reaches a distance of 12° West of the Sun, it becomes visible again. Only then is its "rising" recognized; its motion also changes from <term>Retrograde</term> back to <term>Direct</term> <note>original: "Margi" — 'on the path' or forward motion</note> within a few days.

After becoming <term>Direct</term> in this manner, Mercury gradually moves further away from the Sun, and when it reaches the area between points 7 and 8 in its orbit, its distance from the Sun becomes greatest. Then, Mercury begins to approach the Sun again, and within a month and a half, it becomes so close to the Sun that it cannot be seen by the eye. According to the *Surya Siddhanta*, the planet is considered to have "set" when the difference in distance is 14°. When the difference between Mercury and the Sun becomes zero, both rise above the horizon together. In this condition, a <term>Superior Conjunction</term> <note>original: "Bahari Yuti" — literally 'outer union'; this occurs when Mercury is on the opposite side of the Sun from the Earth.</note> occurs. During a <term>Superior Conjunction</term>, Mercury remains in <term>Direct</term> motion.

During the time of <term>Superior Conjunction</term>, both Mercury and the Sun appear to move toward the left; therefore, it takes more days for Mercury to distance itself from the Sun. This means that when Mercury sets in the East, it remains invisible for a longer duration than it does during the Western setting period, and it takes longer to rise in the West.

It has been written that Mercury sets in the West when the distance between the Sun and Mercury becomes less than 12°, and it sets in the East when the distance between the two becomes less than 14°. The reason for this is that during an <term>Inferior Conjunction</term>, Mercury is very close to the Earth; therefore, its <term>Disk</term> <note>original: "Bimba" — the apparent circular area of a celestial body</note> appears larger, and it remains visible until it reaches a distance of 12° from the Sun. However, during a <term>Superior Conjunction</term>, Mercury is even further away than the Sun itself; therefore, its <term>Disk</term> appears smaller <meta>Text cuts off at bottom of page</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Computation of True Planetary Positions</header>

it becomes visible, and when its distance reaches 14°, it disappears from view. <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "Shukra"</note> also appears at its largest during its <term>Inferior Conjunction</term> <note>original: "Bhitari Yuti"; when the planet is between the Earth and the Sun</note> and at its smallest during its <term>Superior Conjunction</term> <note>original: "Bahari Yuti"; when the Sun is between the Earth and the planet</note>. From this, it must be proven that planets do not simply "go away" somewhere after they set; rather, they become so close to the Sun that they become invisible to the naked eye. However, through a <term>telescope</term> <note>original: "Durbin"</note>, they can be seen no matter how close they are to the Sun.

The orbits of both planets, <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Budha"</note> and Venus, are within the Earth's orbit; therefore, what has been said regarding Mercury also applies to Venus. The only difference is that Venus’s orbit is larger than Mercury’s, which is why Venus comes extremely close to the Earth during its inferior conjunction. When viewed through a telescope, both Mercury and Venus exhibit <term>phases</term> <note>original: "Kalaye"</note> just like the Moon. During a superior conjunction, both planets appear as full circles because at that time, the entire illuminated <term>disk</term> <note>original: "Bimba"</note> faces us. As the planet moves to the side, we no longer see the full illuminated portion, and day by day, the disk appears more "broken" or partial. However, we receive more light because the distance is decreasing; thus, even a "broken" planet provides more light because it is closer. At the time of inferior conjunction, the planet’s illuminated side faces the Sun, so we receive no light from the planet at all, and it appears in the telescope like a black spot. The phases of Venus are shown in Figure 22.

In Figure 23, only that part of the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>original: "Rashi-chakra"</note> is shown where <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Mangala"</note> appears to go <term>Retrograde</term> <note>original: "Vakri"; appearing to move backward</note> and then <term>Direct</term> <note>original: "Margi"; moving in its normal forward direction</note>. 'S' (the Sun) is at the center. The Earth in its orbit and Mars in its orbit revolve around the Sun in such a way that they appear (from the Sun) to move through the Zodiac from <term>Aries</term> to <term>Taurus</term>, and from Taurus to <term>Gemini</term>. To a person situated on the Sun, no planet would appear to be retrograde; he would see all planets revolving in the same direction. However, to the inhabitants of Earth, Mars appears to be moving direct, then fast-moving, then slow-moving, then stationary, then retrograde, then slow-moving again, and finally direct. One revolution of Mars is completed in 686 days; therefore, it completes a 10° arc of its revolution in <gloss>686 x 10 / 360</gloss> days, or about 19 days. In that same amount of time, the Earth moves approximately 19°, because the Earth's revolution is completed in 365 days—meaning it moves roughly 1° per day.

Suppose the Earth is at point 1 in its orbit and Mars is also at point 1 in its orbit; then, to the inhabitants of Earth, Mars [appears] at point 1 in the Zodiac...

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric explanation of planetary motion, specifically focusing on Mars. The previous page established the initial positions of Earth and Mars in their respective orbits to explain how their relative speeds create shifts in perspective.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions <note>original: "Spashtadhikara"; the section of an astronomical treatise dealing with calculating the true, observed positions of planets rather than their mean or average positions.</note></header>

...will reach point 3, <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Mangal"</note> will also reach point 3 in its orbit, and it will appear that in the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>original: "Rashi Chakra"</note> it has moved only slightly forward past point 2. Here, Mars will seem nearly stationary for a few days, because within 18 days it has moved very little from point 2 to 3 in the Zodiac. When Earth and Mars both reach point 4 in their respective orbits, Mars will appear at point 4 in the Zodiac—that is, it will seem to have moved backward. This is what we call <term>Retrograde</term> <note>original: "Vakri"</note>, even though Mars’s actual path in its own orbit remains straight and forward. At point 4, the Earth is positioned directly between Mars and the Sun; the Sun is to the Earth’s right and Mars is to its left. Thus, the distance between them becomes 6 signs <note>The Zodiac is divided into 12 "signs" (Rashis) of 30° each; 6 signs equals 180°.</note> or 180°. This state is called Mars being **in opposition** to the Sun. When the Sun sets, Mars rises in the east, and when the Sun rises, Mars sets in the west. In this position, Mars is at its closest point to Earth; therefore, its **disc** <note>original: "Bimba"; the visible circular face of a celestial body.</note> appears very large, and many details of the planet can be seen through a telescope at that time.

When Earth and Mars are at point 5 in their respective orbits, we see Mars at point 5 in the Zodiac, still appearing to have moved backward. When both planets reach point 6 in their orbits, Mars appears at point 6 in the Zodiac, having shifted slightly forward again. Here, too, Mars appears nearly stationary for a while before it is seen to move forward once more.

When the Sun is positioned between the Earth and Mars—that is, when Earth is at point 4 and Mars is at point 'M' <note>referring to a diagram not pictured here</note>—then the distance of Mars from Earth is at its greatest. This state is called the **conjunction** <note>original: "Yuti"</note> of Mars and the Sun. In this condition, the disc of Mars appears very small.

It is evident from both diagrams that the **inner planets** <note>original: "Inferior planets"; those closer to the Sun than Earth, like Mercury and Venus.</note> appear to be <term>Retrograde</term> when they are nearing an <term>Inferior Conjunction</term> <note>When the planet passes between the Earth and the Sun.</note>, and they remain retrograde during that conjunction. However, the **outer planets** <note>original: "Superior planets"; those further from the Sun than Earth, like Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> become retrograde when they are at a distance of approximately 6 signs or 180° from the Sun, and they remain retrograde while they are directly **in opposition**. The inner planets are "set" <note>original: "Ast"; meaning they are invisible because they are too close to the Sun's glare, a state called combustion.</note> during both the inferior and superior conjunctions of every revolution; that is, due to being near the Sun, they cannot be seen with the naked eye in the Sun’s intense light. But the outer planets have only one conjunction per revolution, and it is only then that they are said to be "set."

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the mechanics of planetary motion. While the previous page provided a geometric explanation for the retrograde motion of Mars, this page transitions into the necessity of mathematical corrections to ensure that calculations align with actual visual observations.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>Sūryasiddhānta <note>The "Sun Canon," one of the most important ancient Indian treatises on astronomy.</note></header>

In this way, it is evident that by assuming the Earth is in motion, it becomes very easy to understand the peculiar movements of the planets. If the Earth is considered stationary, it cannot be explained in any way why the <term>retrograde motion</term> <note>original: "vakri gati"; the apparent backward movement of a planet in the sky.</note> of the planets occurs.

->Now, I shall reverently describe the rules for finding the true positions of the planets,<-
->so that they may always correspond with observation according to their various motions. ||14||<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "तत्तद्गतिवशान्नित्यं यथा दृक्तुल्यतां ग्रहाः । प्रयान्ति तत्प्रवक्ष्यामि स्फुटीकरणमादरात् ॥१४॥"</note>

**Translation (14)—** Under the influence of these various motions, I shall respectfully explain the rules for <term>finding the true positions</term> <note>original: "sphuṭīkaraṇa"</note> (knowing them through mathematics) so that the planets reach a state of <term>observational accuracy</term> <note>original: "dṛktulyatā"; literally "equality with sight," meaning the calculated position matches where the planet is actually seen in the sky.</note>—that is, they appear exactly where they are calculated to be at the time of observation.

**Scientific Commentary —** This verse is of great importance. It proves that our ancient teachers calculated the true positions of planets specifically so that there would be no discrepancy between mathematics and direct observation. For this purpose, the most minute rules were created in the <term>Chapter on True Positions</term> <note>original: "Spaṣṭādhikāra"</note>. However, as I have already mentioned in the commentary on the 6th verse of the Chapter on Mean Positions, whether the instruments are coarse or refined, some amount of <term>experimental error</term> <note>original: "prayogātmaka aśuddhi"</note> always remains during observation. Therefore, over a long period, a difference arises which requires periodic correction. This is called the **Bīja correction** <note>original: "Bīja Saṃskāra"; literally "seed-correction," an adjustment made to astronomical constants to account for the gradual accumulation of errors over centuries.</note>.

For example, suppose a watch loses one second every day. In 60 days, it will be 1 minute behind, and in a year, it will be 6 minutes behind. However, in daily use, we would still say the watch is very accurate, because a difference of 1 minute in 60 days or one second per day is negligible. But if this difference continues to accumulate and the watch is never adjusted, over many years the difference will become so large that it will be impossible to ignore, and a correction must be made. 

Just as a daily difference of one second in a watch can take on a massive form over time, similarly, even a difference of one <term>pala</term> <note>A traditional unit of time; 1 pala equals 24 seconds.</note> in the <term>orbital periods</term> <note>original: "bhagaṇakāla"</note> of the Sun, Moon, and other planets becomes very large over hundreds of years. This is why the Bīja correction must be applied. The custom of making corrections from time to time was accepted by our ancient teachers, whose citations I will provide below. However, for some time now, there has been a disagreement on this subject. One side argues that there is no right to comment upon or correct the <term>Sacred Texts</term> <note>original: "Ārṣa Granthas"; texts believed to be authored by divine seers (Rishis).</note>, and that whatever is in them must be accepted as it is. The other side argues that making corrections is entirely appropriate. Below, I provide the arguments for both sides as far as I have found them:

<vocab>Sūryasiddhānta, Dṛktulyatā (observational accuracy), Sphuṭīkaraṇa (calculation of true position), Vijñāna Bhāṣya (Scientific Commentary), Bīja Saṃskāra (Bīja correction), Bhagaṇakāla (orbital period), Ārṣa Grantha (Sacred/Ancient texts), Vedha (observation/sighting)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the term "dṛktulyatā" (observational accuracy) introduced at the end of the previous page. While the previous page suggested that calculations should match what is seen in the sky, this page presents a complex debate among traditional Indian astronomers about whether this "accuracy" refers to physical sight or a spiritual, "unseen" accuracy required for religious rituals.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions</header>

# Chapter on True Positions

Pandit Indranarayan Dwivedi, a resident of Prayag and recipient of the title *Jyotish Bhushan* <note>original: "Jewel of Astronomy"</note>, provides this commentary alongside his translation of this same verse—

> “Many people here interpret ‘observational accuracy’ (<term>dṛktulyataḥ</term>) as meaning visible calculation; however, this is a misconception. From examining the preceding and following context, it becomes clearly known that ‘observational accuracy’ here refers to a calculation where the planet is seen at its true calculated position according to the ‘unseen’ vision. Otherwise, visible planets could never be proven according to this calculation, because the specific corrections currently derived for visible planets would have to have been constant—a fact well-known to those who understand <term>spherics</term> <note>original: "gola vidyā"; the science of the celestial sphere</note>.”¹

The essence of this passage is perhaps that the rules provided in this text for calculating the true positions of planets do not result in the same positions seen through direct observation (<term>vedha</term>). Therefore, "observational accuracy" does not mean direct visual observation, but rather an "unseen observation" which the Sages <note>original: "Rishis"</note> knew through their power of yoga <note>original: "yogabala"</note>.

The astronomer Pandit Girijaprasad Dwivedi, who is currently the headmaster of the Naval Kishore School in Lucknow and a proponent of this view, writes very clearly in the ‘Prabha’ Hindi commentary on pages 6 and 7 of his *Siddhanta Shiromani Goladhyaya* <note>A famous 12th-century astronomical text; this refers to the section on the "Sphere."</note>²:—

> “...Mathematics is of two kinds based on the distinction between the ‘seen’ (<term>dṛṣṭa</term>) and the ‘unseen’ (<term>adṛṣṭa</term>). Regarding this, it is said: ‘Perform the calculation according to the words of the Sages for the sake of attaining spiritual <note>unseen</note> fruits. For mathematics meant for visible purposes always arises from visual observation.’ Likewise, ‘For attaining spiritual results, only that which is spoken in the original Sun Canon <note>original: "nirvīja-arka"; the "Seedless Sun," referring to the original text before modern correction factors were added</note> should be used.’ Following these verses shown by the Great Sages, as stated by Kamalākara in the *Tattva-viveka*, only the ‘true’ planets calculated this way are suitable for making astrological predictions, not those corrected with modernly available observations. By specifying the ‘original Sun Canon,’ modern corrections are rejected. If non-Sage-authored mathematics is accepted in matters of religious results, chaos ensues in the timing of many Vedic and Smriti rituals. Therefore, wise men who value religion should examine everything and follow the ‘thornless path.’ Planets arising from those specific modern corrections are only useful for ‘seen’ mathematics, such as eclipses, rising and setting, and so on. Those who pride themselves on ‘seen’ mathematics often argue for the uprooting of ‘unseen’ mathematics. However, life is managed only by accepting both, and not by accepting just one; the details of this are found elsewhere.

> “Mathematics is of two types: Seen and Unseen. ‘Seen’ is that which is observed with the eyes, such as eclipses, rising and setting, planetary conjunctions, and the elevation of the Moon’s horns. ‘Unseen’ is that which does not come into view, such as the conjunction of lunar dates (<term>tithi-yoga</term>), etc. The religious result of an eclipse occurs only upon seeing it. But the spiritual result of fasts and festivals occurs without seeing. The prediction of results belongs only to the Sages...”

---

1. *Surya Siddhanta*, published by the Hindi Sahitya Sammelan, Prayag, page 35.
2. Published in Lucknow at the Naval Kishore Press, 1911 AD.

<summary>This page explores a scholarly debate regarding "dṛktulyatā," arguing that traditional astronomical calculations are intended for spiritual and ritual accuracy ("unseen") rather than mere physical observation ("seen").</summary>
<keywords>dṛktulyatā, Indranarayan Dwivedi, Girijaprasad Dwivedi, seen vs unseen mathematics, Surya Siddhanta, ritual timing, astronomical corrections</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding "dṛktulyatā" (observational accuracy). It specifically addresses the tension between traditional Sage-authored calculations and modern, instrument-based corrections, arguing that different mathematical systems apply to physical events versus spiritual rituals.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

...comes from the experience-proven words of the Sages. Whatever results are written according to the positions of the planets, man will only be able to know those. No one besides the Sages can imagine or know these results.

> "In the Sage-authored texts <note>original: "Arsha Granthas"</note>, the method for calculating the true positions of planets is the same method that is appropriate for predicting outcomes <note>original: "phaladesha"</note>. This is because the timings for Vedic rituals <term>Shrauta</term> and traditional religious rites <term>Smarta</term> are determined based on those specific planetary positions. Therefore, it is proper to establish religious systems and conduct practices using the lunar dates (<term>tithi</term>) and other markers derived from that traditional mathematics.

> "In modern times, scholars from Europe have established many new corrections <note>original: "samskaras"</note> using precise instruments, and by applying them to the planets, they calculate 'micro-accurate' true planetary positions. Following this precise method, many modern experts in English mathematics now calculate true planetary positions in various almanacs <term>Panchangas</term>, using them to determine lunar dates and organize religious life accordingly. However, this is entirely improper and creates an obstacle to faith. When a fast such as <term>Ekadashi</term> <note>the eleventh day of the lunar fortnight, a major fasting day</note> is determined according to the Sages' mathematics, it would be impossible for it to coincide with the time calculated by this new 'precise' mathematics. In this way, by contradicting the words of the Sages, religion will be thrown into disorder. The words of the Sages can only be fulfilled by following their own methods. From this, it is clear that for religious arrangements, it is proper to rely only on the mathematics spoken by the Sages.

> "These new observation-proven corrections are referred to in ancient texts by the name <term>bija</term> <note>literally "seed"; a corrective factor applied to planetary formulas to account for the passage of time</note>. The ancients also derived these through direct observation <note>original: "vedha"</note>. However, they utilized this 'seed' correction only for the knowledge of the precise timing of 'visible mathematics' <term>Drishta-ganita</term> <note>calculations regarding things that can be seen, like eclipses</note>. They did not insert it into 'unseen mathematics' <term>Adrishta-ganita</term> <note>calculations for spiritual or ritual potency</note>, as is done today. Therefore, the modern European corrections are only appropriate for visible calculations. There is no harm in using them for that purpose, as the ancient masters themselves organized the system in this way."

For example — 
> "For the success of unseen results, one should use the 'seedless' <note>original: "nirbija"; the original formula without later corrections</note> words of the Sun. For calculations intended for visible phenomena, the calculation should always be made so that it matches observation."

This means that for 'unseen mathematics' <term>Adrishta-ganita</term>, one should rely solely on the 'seedless' mathematics spoken by the Sun in the *Surya Siddhanta*. But for 'visible mathematics' <term>Drishta-ganita</term>, one should always calculate using whatever method brings the mathematics into harmony with the actual sky.

"Thus, through impartiality and religious wisdom, it is proven that every thoughtful person should accept 'visible' and 'unseen' mathematics according to the aforementioned rules..."

<summary>This page argues for a dual-system approach to astronomy: using ancient, uncorrected formulas for religious rituals (Adrishta) while allowing for modern, corrected calculations for visible events like eclipses (Drishta).</summary>
<keywords>Surya Siddhanta, Arsha texts, Panchanga, Ekadashi, Bija corrections, Drishta-ganita, Adrishta-ganita, religious vs observational astronomy</keywords>
