<meta>This page continues the debate from the previous section regarding the tension between traditional planetary calculations and observational accuracy. It specifically addresses the views of the 17th-century astronomer Kamala Kar and the practical use of astronomical tables in creating almanacs for religious rituals.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions</header>

...is necessary. To follow only what is perceived by the senses everywhere, like the **Charvakas** <note>a school of ancient Indian materialists who rejected everything except direct sensory perception</note>, is highly improper and a denial of the truth.”

In this long excerpt, the Sanskrit verse provided as evidence belongs to Acharya Kamala Kar’s *Siddhanta Tattva Viveka*, which was written in 1560 Shaka (1795 Vikram / 1638 CE). In this text, Acharya Kamala Kar has occasionally taken an improper bias toward the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>a foundational Sanskrit text on astronomy</note>, the evidence for which is found in the words of Mahamahopadhyaya Sudhakar Dwivedi:

> “In this work, the author has, as far as possible, defended the views of the *Surya Siddhanta* and refuted the likes of Bhaskara and Munishvara <note>famous astronomers who often favored observational corrections</note>. Because of his great desire to criticize others, his refutation of Bhaskara’s equation of time <term>udayantara</term> and other corrections does not suit this scholar, who was otherwise peerless in the study of spheres and mathematics and skilled in many conceptualizations.” <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original Sanskrit: “अत्र यावच्छक्यं सूर्यसिद्धान्तमत मण्डनं भास्कर मुनीश्वरादीनां खडनं च कृतं ग्रन्थ कृता। बहुत परदूषणाभिलाषेणान्यथैव भास्कर कृतोदयान्तर कर्मादि खण्डनमस्य गोले गणितेचाद्वितीय पण्डितस्यानेक कल्पनाकुशलस्य न शोभते।”</note>

I do not know if there are any other ancient opinions on this side of the argument. If any noble soul would be kind enough to inform me, I would be deeply grateful and would accept the information with thanks. Furthermore, I desire to know if anyone has actually accepted Acharya Kamala Kar’s rule—that planetary positions “without seed corrections” <note>original: *nirbijakokta*; referring to traditional calculations that have not been updated to match modern observations</note> should be used for religious practices. This is because, for hundreds of years before him, astronomical manuals <term>Karana Granthas</term> such as the *Makaranda Sarini* and *Grahalaghava* have been used to create almanacs <term>Panchangas</term>, and these texts have already applied “seed corrections” <term>Bija Samskara</term> <note>mathematical corrections applied to ancient formulas to bring them back into alignment with observed reality</note>. Some evidence for this is provided below:

> (1) “There are several astronomical manuals, but none are as popular in study and teaching as the *Grahalaghava*. Almanacs are created in many regions based on it, and following these, everyone performs their Vedic <term>Shrauta</term> and traditional <term>Smarta</term> rituals without hesitation. This is a manual of the Solar school <note>Saura Paksha</note>. Although the author has occasionally relied on the schools of Aryabhata and Brahma, the Solar school has held prominence for a very long time. The mathematics of the Aryabhata and Brahma schools had already become disrupted by the time of Acharya Varahamihira (427 Shaka / 505 CE). In some places, almanacs of the Brahma school are still in use—such as the ‘Chandu’ almanac started by the astrologer Chandu of Jodhpur—but they are not considered authoritative because they lack Sage-authored roots <note>original: *anarshamulaka*</note>.” <gloss>2</gloss>

---

1. *Ganaka Tarangini*, page 68.
2. Written by Pandit Girija Prasad Dwivedi in the magazine *Madhuri*, Kartik issue, 1923 (1680 Vikram), page 505.

The text highlights the tension between adhering to the "uncorrected" traditional Solar school and the practical need for accuracy in ritual timing.</summary>

<page-num>११०</page-num>
-># The *Surya-Siddhanta*<-

In the *Makaranda Sarini* <note>a famous set of astronomical tables used for calculating planetary positions, composed by Makaranda in 1478 CE</note>, this passage serves as evidence regarding the subject of <term>Bija correction</term> <note>Bija refers to the necessary adjustments made to ancient astronomical constants to ensure they match current observations</note>.

(2) “...The thousandth part of the elapsed Kali Yuga <note>the current world age in the Hindu cycle of time</note> (1000) is 4; 42, 46, which is the positive correction for Saturn. Two-thirds of this (1; 35, 15) added results in a positive correction for the apogee of Mercury (6; 17, 1). Subtracting one-third of Saturn's correction results in a negative correction for Jupiter (3; 8, 31). Saturn's correction is a negative correction for the apogee of Venus (4; 42, 46). The apogee of Mercury, refined by the Bija...” <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original Sanskrit: “कलिगतस्य सहस्रांशों १००० शादि ४ । ४२ । ४६ शनि बीज धन ॥ एतद्द्व्यंशे १ । ३५ । १५ सहितं जातं बुधोच्च धनं ६ । १७ । १ शनिबीज व्यंशेन रहितं जातं ३ । ८ । ३१ ऋणंगुरोः शनिबीजं शुक्रोच्च ऋणं ४। ४२ । ४६ बीज संस्कृतं बुधोच्चं...”</note>

The famous astronomer Shankar Balkrishna Dixit writes in his Marathi work, *History of Indian Astronomy*, on pages 184 and 257:

(3) “In the *Makaranda* text, there is a Bija correction for the planets as stated in the *Surya-Siddhanta*”...; “The author of *Makaranda* has applied Bija corrections to the *Surya-Siddhanta*; I have already written about this previously.” <note>original Marathi: “मकरंदग्रंथांत सूर्यसिद्धांतोक्त ग्रहादिकांस बीज संस्कार आहे”...; “मकरंदकाराने सूर्यसिद्धांतास बीजसंस्कार दिला आहे, त्या विषयीं पूर्वी लिहिलेंच आहे”</note>

It is proven from these passages that for hundreds of years, all the <term>Panchangs</term> <note>traditional Hindu liturgical calendars or almanacs</note> created according to the *Makaranda Sarini* or the *Grahalaghava* incorporate revisions based on Bija corrections. Therefore, in my opinion, Kamala Kar’s statement has never been accepted in practice.

Regarding this verse by Acharya Vashistha: “Thus, in brief, the science revealed by Mandavya has been stated. In every age, the positions of the Sun, Moon, and others will become *deteriorated* (*visrasti*)” <note>original Sanskrit: “इत्थं माण्डव्य संक्षेपा-दुक्तं शास्त्रमयोदितं । विस्त्रस्ती रविचन्द्राद्यैर्भविष्यति युगे युगे”</note>, Kamala Kar has interpreted the word *visrasti* <note>meaning slipped, decayed, or deteriorated</note> as *vistriti* <note>meaning expanded or detailed</note>, thereby changing the entire meaning of the verse. However, this is entirely unscientific, misleading, and contrary to ancient scientific methods; it was written solely to support his own partisan view.

Now, I am writing what has been said in support of the other side for nearly one and a half thousand years, which will prove how our ancient astronomers viewed the sacred texts of astronomy and what arguments they recorded in favor of revising them from time to time.

At the time when the Solar Being <note>an emanation of the Sun God</note> began to instruct Mayasura in the *Surya-Siddhanta*, he said:

> “This is that same primeval science which the Sun God previously spoke. Only the difference of time occurs here due to the change of the ages.”
> <note>original Sanskrit: ‘ शास्त्रमाद्यं तदेवेदं यत्पूर्वं प्राह भास्करः । युगानां परिवर्तन कालभेदोत्र केवलं ॥”</note>

This is the 9th verse of the *Madhyamadhikara* <note>the chapter on the mean motions of the planets</note>, which has already been explained.

Later, when the sages received the teaching of astronomy from Mayasura, Mayasura first recounted everything he had learned from the Solar Being, and at the end, he gave instructions on the *Bijop-nayanadhyaya* <note>the chapter on the application of Bija corrections</note> in 21 verses, the reason for which was stated as follows:

1. *Makaranda Sarini*, page 36, Bombay edition.

While the previous page cited the Makaranda Sarini and Shankar Balkrishna Dixit to prove the practical necessity of these corrections, this page moves into the Spashtadhikara (Chapter on True Positions) to discuss the philosophical and traditional origins of the Bija method, addressing arguments regarding whether these sections were later interpolations.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Planetary Positions</header>

# Chapter on True Planetary Positions

> “The mean position is derived from the proportion of the cycle; the true position is derived from the arc of the mean circle. Over time, this no longer matches visual observation, therefore the <term>Bija correction</term> <note>the process of adjusting astronomical constants to align with current observations</note> is explained. || 5 ||
> The <term>Bija</term> is the ultimate, clearly manifested secret of the entire astronomical treatise. It grants the successful fulfillment of duties such as journeys, marriage, and other auspicious acts. || 21 ||” 
<note>original Sanskrit: "चक्रानुपातजोमध्यो मध्यवृत्तांशजः स्फुटः । कालेन दृक्समो न स्यात् ततो बीजक्रियोच्यते ॥ ५ ॥ बीजं निःशेष सिद्धान्त रहस्य परमं स्फुटं । यात्रापाणिग्रहादीनां कार्याणां शुभसिद्धिदम् ॥ २१ ॥"</note>

This means that as time passes, <term>observational equivalence</term> <note>Drik-tulyata: the state where calculated planetary positions match what is actually seen in the sky</note> is lost; for this reason, the method of <term>Bija correction</term> is provided. Only through the true planetary positions refined by <term>Bija correction</term> do journeys, weddings, and other auspicious works become fruitful.

However, it is a matter of regret that scholars of the traditionalist school consider this chapter to be an interpolation <note>an addition made by a later author rather than the original composer</note>. In my view, it is certainly an interpolation if one considers only what the <term>Sun-born Man</term> <note>Suryansha Purusha: the celestial being who revealed the Surya-Siddhanta</note> initially spoke, because this is the <term>Bija correction</term> of <term>Mayasura</term> <note>the figure who received the revelation of the Surya-Siddhanta</note> and not of the <term>Sun-born Man</term> himself. But if it is accepted that the <term>Surya-Siddhanta</term> currently in use is the one received by the Sages exactly as <term>Mayasura</term> related it to them, then there remains no need to consider this an interpolation. The reality is that the prevailing <term>Surya-Siddhanta</term> is indeed that which was taught by <term>Mayasura</term> to the Sages. The chapter on the application of Bija was placed at the end so it would remain clear what instructions <term>Mayasura</term> received from the <term>Sun-born Man</term> and what <term>Mayasura</term> added from his own experience.

Regarding <term>observational equivalence</term>, <term>Brahmagupta</term> <note>a prominent 7th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> wrote in 628 CE (Saka 550):

> “Because of the daily discrepancies in planets, lunar days, half-days, constellations, days, and months—who would touch such a base calculation even with their foot during eclipses or planetary conjunctions? || 57 ||
> 
> When the astronomical system fails, a wise man should daily recognize this through observation and make such an effort that calculation and observation always reach unity.”
<note>original Sanskrit: "प्रतिदिवस विसंवादाद् ग्रह तिथि करणर्क्ष दिवसमासानाम् । ग्रहणग्रह-योगादिषु पादं पादेन कः स्पृशति ॥ ५७ ॥ तन्त्वभ्रंशे प्रतिदिवसमेवं विज्ञाय धीमता यत्नः । कार्यस्तस्मिन् यस्मिन् दृग्गणितैक्यं सदा भवति ।।"</note>

In the commentary on these two verses, Mahamahopadhyaya <term>Sudhakar Dwivedi</term> <note>a renowned 19th-century scholar of Sanskrit and mathematics</note> writes:

> “Due to the daily disagreement and visual contradiction in the planets, lunar days, half-days, constellations, days, and months, as well as in eclipses and planetary conjunctions, who would even touch a 'low-limb'—meaning a poor astronomical calculation—with their foot? Just as the foot is the lowest part of the body, a calculation that does not match observation is the lowest form of calculation and is unworthy of contact. As the proverb says: 'It is better to avoid mud from a distance than to have to wash it off.' 
> 
> When the system falls into error and there is a contradiction between its calculations and visual observation, a wise man should understand the daily true positions through observation and make an effort in that system through <term>Bija</term> and other methods so that...”
<note>original Sanskrit: "ग्रह-तिथि-करण-ऋक्ष-दिवस मासानां तथा ग्रहण-ग्रह योगादिषु च प्रतिदिवस विसंवादात् प्रत्यहं दृग्विरोधात् पादं करणाधमं कः पादेनापि स्पृशति... तन्त्रभ्रंशे सति तदीयतन्त्रगणनया दृग्विरोधे सति एवं पूर्वोक्तं प्रतिदिनं स्पष्टीकरणाद्यं वेधादिना विज्ञाय तस्मिन् तन्त्रे बीजादिना तथा यत्नः कार्यो यथा"</note>

<summary>This page argues that Bija corrections are essential for keeping astronomical calculations aligned with reality (observational equivalence), citing both the Surya-Siddhanta and the 7th-century astronomer Brahmagupta. It addresses the scholarly debate on whether these correction chapters were later additions or original parts of the wisdom passed from Mayasura to the Sages.</summary>

<keywords>Bija correction, Spashtadhikara, observational equivalence, Surya-Siddhanta, Mayasura, Brahmagupta, Sudhakar Dwivedi, Hindu astronomy, planetary calculation</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for astronomical corrections (Bija) by citing historical authorities such as Brahmagupta and Bhaskaracharya. It transitions from the previous discussion on whether these chapters are interpolations to proving that classical astronomers actively advocated for aligning calculation with observation.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

"...observational agreement is achieved. Thus, whichever system consistently achieves agreement between calculation and observation is the system that should be honored." <note>original Sanskrit: "दृग्गणितैक्यं भवति । एवं यस्मिन् तन्त्रे सदा दृग्गणितैक्यं भवति तदेव तन्त्रमादरणीय- मिति ।"</note>¹

In the passage above, along with planets and conjunctions, words like <term>Tithi</term> <note>a lunar day</note>, <term>Karana</term> <note>half of a lunar day</note>, and <term>Riksha</term> <note>a nakshatra or lunar mansion</note> are also mentioned. This makes it clear that even for what Pandit Girija Prasad has called "the unseen" <note>spiritual or merit-based results of rituals</note>, the rule of <term>observational agreement</term> <note>Drik-ganitaikya: the alignment of calculated data with visual reality</note> applies, and the necessity of performing <term>Bija corrections</term> <note>mathematical adjustments to astronomical constants</note> has been stated. Therefore, the great astronomer Brahmagupta did not consider performing corrections to achieve observational accuracy to be contrary to the scriptures or the words of the ancient seers. Indeed, he only forcefully refuted that which he considered truly contrary to the scientific spirit of the scriptures.

The famous <term>Bhaskaracharya</term> <note>a 12th-century mathematician and astronomer, author of the Siddhanta Shiromani</note> wrote in 1072 Saka (1207 Vikram Samvat) [1150 CE]:

> "In journeys, weddings, festivals, and horoscopy, the results are accurate only when the planetary positions themselves are accurate. For this reason, the method for clarifying the positions of the heavenly wanderers is said to be that which achieves agreement between calculation and observation." <note>original Sanskrit: "यात्रा विवाहोत्सवजातकादौ खेटै: स्फुटैरेव फलस्फुटत्वं । स्यात् प्रोच्यते तेन नभश्चराणां स्फुटक्रिया दृग्गणितैक्य कृद्या ॥"</note>²

This means that for activities such as journeys, weddings, festivals, and the casting of birth charts, using clarified planetary positions yields more accurate results. The only "pure" method of clarifying a planet's position is one that results in <term>observational agreement</term>.

Evidence has been provided above regarding how highly the <term>Grahalaghava</term> <note>a popular 16th-century astronomical manual</note> is regarded today. In 1418 Saka (1553 Vikram Samvat) [1496 CE], Acharya Keshava—the father of Acharya Ganesha Daivajna, who authored the *Grahalaghava*—wrote the following in his work *Graha-Kautuka* regarding the need to revise ancient texts:

> "Even in the planetary calculations of the Brahma, Aryabhata, and Surya systems, a great difference is seen in the positions of Mercury and Venus. In the slow-moving sky, a visible difference of five degrees is seen in the conjunctions of stars and planets, as well as in their risings and settings... Similarly, there are differences in the initial positions <note>kshepa</note> and differences in the annual motions. Thus, over a long period, the difference will become very large. This is because even in the Brahma and other systems, a great difference is observed in the number of revolutions and the solar days; hence, over much time, a great discrepancy is inevitable... Therefore, in the future, when great differences arise, skillful calculators should observe the current events such as the conjunction of stars and planets and their risings and settings, and then perform planetary calculations by adjusting the number of revolutions and other constants. Alternatively, one should create simplified manuals by determining the initial positions and annual motions for that specific time... Thus, at the time of a lunar eclipse, I determined the mean position of the Moon from the end of the <term>Tithi</term> <note>the exact moment the lunar day ends</note> using the reverse method, because at that point there is no increase or decrease in the <term>Phala</term> <note>the correction or 'equation' applied to mean positions</note>. Similarly, I calculated the Moon's apogee from the end of the eclipse <term>Tithi</term> using the reverse method at the center of the orbit, because there the correction is at its maximum."

---

1. *Tantra-Pariksha-Adhyaya*, pages 166-170, *Brahmasphuta Siddhanta* edited by Mahamahopadhyaya Sudhakara Dvivedi.
2. *Siddhanta Shiromani*, *Ganitadhayaya*, page 56.

<summary>This page argues that historical Indian astronomers like Brahmagupta, Bhaskaracharya, and Keshavacharya prioritized observational accuracy (Drik-ganitaikya) over rigid adherence to outdated calculations, justifying the use of Bija corrections to align math with the physical sky.</summary>
<keywords>Drik-ganitaikya, Bija correction, Brahmagupta, Bhaskaracharya, Keshavacharya, Graha-Kautuka, planetary observation, lunar eclipse, astronomical constants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for astronomical corrections by providing specific historical examples of how practitioners adjusted their calculations. It moves from general principles to the specific technical adjustments made by Ganesha Daivajna in the 16th century, citing his work Grahalaghava and its commentary by Mallari to prove that ritual timing must rely on observed planetary positions.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>
# Chapter on True Positions

"In that context, the Moon was observed to be five <term>Kalas</term> <note>minutes of arc</note> less than the position calculated by the <term>Surya-paksha</term> <note>the school or system of the Surya Siddhanta</note>. Its <term>Uchcha</term> <note>apogee or highest point</note> followed the <term>Brahma-paksha</term> <note>the system of the Brahma Siddhanta</note>. The Sun showed only a slight difference across all systems, so the <term>Saura</term> <note>Solar system</note> value was adopted. Other planets, the positions of stars, planetary conjunctions, and their risings and settings were calculated by observing current events. Presently, Mars and Jupiter align with the Brahma-paksha. Mercury follows the Brahma system. Venus is midway between the Brahma and <term>Argha</term> systems. Saturn was observed to be five degrees more than what the three systems calculated. Observing these current occurrences, planetary calculations were performed using simplified methods." <note>original Sanskrit: "तत्र चंद्रः सूर्यपक्षात्पंचकलोनो दृष्टः । उच्चं ब्रह्मपक्षश्रितं । सूर्यः सर्वपक्षेपीषदन्तरः स सौरो गृहीतः । अन्ये ग्रहा नक्षत्र ग्रहयोगास्तोदयोदिभिर्वर्तमानघटनामवलोक्य साधिताः । ... एवं वर्तमान घटनामवलोक्य लघुकर्मणा ग्रह गणितं कृतं ।"</note>¹

This long excerpt clearly illustrates how one should modify the <term>Bhagana</term> <note>revolution cycles</note> of the Sun, Moon, and other planets by observing current celestial events through <term>Vedha</term> <note>astronomical observation</note>. Instructions are also given to continue this practice in the future. In this passage, there is an explicit mention of the *Surya Siddhanta*. Based on these observations and <term>Bija</term> <note>mathematical corrections</note> established by his father, the teacher <term>Ganesha Daivajna</term> <note>a prominent 16th-century Indian astronomer</note> composed the *Grahalaghava* <note>a manual for astronomical calculation</note>. In the 16th verse of its chapter on mean positions, written in 1442 Shaka (1577 Vikram Samvat) [1520 CE], he states:

> "The Solar [correction], the Moon's apogee, the Moon itself minus nine minutes, Jupiter according to the <term>Arya</term> system, Mars, Rahu, and Mercury according to the <term>Kendra</term> <note>anomaly</note> of the Brahma system, and Saturn with an added degree—these, along with the Venusian anomaly following the non-Aryan <note>likely referring to Islamic or other foreign tables</note> middle system, achieve <term>observational agreement</term>. With these perfected values, one should declare the timing of eclipses, religious duties, political matters, and auspicious ceremonies." <note>original Sanskrit: "सौरोंकोऽपि विधूच्चमङ्क कलिकोनाब्जो गुरुस्त्वार्यजो... सिद्धैस्तैरहपर्व धर्म नय सत्कार्यादिकं त्वादिशेत् ॥"</note>²

This makes it evident that Ganesha orders that all auspicious works—such as festivals and religious duties—must be determined through <term>Drik-ganitaikya</term> <note>the unification of calculation and observation</note>, and not by the "seedless" <note>un-corrected or obsolete</note> *Surya Siddhanta*.

In his commentary on this, <term>Mallari</term> writes in 1547 Shaka (1682 Vikram Samvat) [1625 CE]: "...calculated from those systems, these planets achieve observational agreement... In this book, one should declare eclipses, religious duties, political ethics, and auspicious works using these perfected planets. 'Eclipses and religious duties' refers to the performance of sacrifices, the <term>Ekadashi</term> <note>the 11th day of the lunar fortnight</note> fast, and so on. 'Ethics' refers to political conduct and the administration of justice. 'Auspicious works' includes the thread ceremony, weddings, and more. The intent is that these should only be declared using the <term>Tithis</term> <note>lunar days</note> derived from these texts. The reasoning is: in whatever era a particular calculation achieves observational agreement, that alone should be accepted because it matches reality." <note>original Sanskrit: "...इति तेभ्यः पक्षेभ्यः साधिता इमे ग्रहाः दृशितुल्यतां दृग्गणितैक्यं यान्ति... यतो यस्मिन् यस्मिन् काले यद्यद् दृग्गणितैक्यकृत्तदेवग्राह्यं घटमानत्वात् ।"</note>³

Furthermore, Mallari states: "A planet calculated from the <term>Ahargana</term> <note>total count of days elapsed since an epoch</note> is a 'mean' planet; however, when looking at the sky through instruments, the planet is not seen at that exact position. A slight difference is observed because daily motion is inconsistent. Thus, by observing the planets daily with the <term>Gola</term> <note>armillary sphere</note> or the <term>Chakra-yantra</term> <note>circle instrument</note>, the differences between the 'mean' planet generated from the day-count and the 'true' observed planet were calculated." <note>original Sanskrit: "अहर्गणात्साधितो यो ग्रहः स मध्यमो यतो यन्त्र-वेधेनाकाशे विलोकयमाने तावान् ग्रहो न दृष्टः... मध्यम ग्रह वेधित स्पष्टग्रहयोरन्तराणि साधितानि ।"</note>⁴

It cites the commentator Mallari and the astronomer Ganesha Daivajna to prove that even ancient masters recognized that infinitesimal errors in measuring seconds of arc accumulate over time, requiring periodic correction of the "mean motion" of planets.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
# Surya Siddhanta

Mallari Ji writes in another place: “In this way, having calculated the planetary movements until the completion of a planetary revolution, the mean motion was accepted as the average of the maximum and minimum speeds. This is difficult to achieve with perfect precision because the subtlest units—fractions of <term>Vikala</term> <note>seconds of arc</note>—are imperceptible. Thus, an approximate value was accepted. After some time had passed, sages like Vasistha and others observed a difference in the speeds. Later scholars saw this as well... At this current time, these positions are visible to the eyes; similarly, in the future, astronomers should perform planetary <term>Vedha</term> <note>observation</note> using instruments like the <term>Nalika-bandha</term> <note>a tube-like sighting device</note>, identify the discrepancies, and then formulate the planetary calculations. This has also been stated by the Author <note>Ganesha Daivajna</note> at the conclusion of the book.”^1 
<note>original Sanskrit: "एवं ग्रहभगणभोगपर्यन्तं ग्रहगतीरानीथ तासु मध्ये या परमाधिका गतिर्याच परमाल्पा तयोर्योगाधं मध्यगतिरेवाङ्गी कृता । सा दुःसाध्या सूक्ष्माणां विकला कोट्यंशादीनामलक्ष्यत्वात्... ग्रहकरणानि कार्याणीत्यग्रे ग्रन्थ समाप्तावाचार्येणोप्युक्तमस्ति ।"</note>

In this passage, to support the logic Mallari Ji has employed, he has provided citations from Varaha-mihira, Vasistha, the *Surya Siddhanta*, and the *Brahma-gupta Siddhanta*. I have omitted them here because I have already presented them previously. To achieve <term>Drik-tulyata</term> <note>observational parity; when calculation matches sight</note>, a test can only be conducted through observation. Therefore, a rule can only be considered pure or accurate when there is harmony between mathematics and observation. Mallari Ji’s point is absolutely correct <note>literally "16 annas," a phrase meaning 100% or entirely</note> that in the values obtained through observation, some level of approximation remains "because fractions of seconds of arc are imperceptible," for which one must periodically make corrections based on observing current events.

The reader may be weary of these many long citations; therefore, I will not provide excerpts from Ganesha Daivajna's book, the *Brihat-tithi-chintamani*. However, that work briefly discusses the observations of Brahmacharya, Vasistha, Kashyapa, Mayasura, Aryabhata, Durgasimha Mihira, Brahmagupta, Keshava, and others. It explains why discrepancies arose between them, why there was a need to create new texts at those times, and how corrections should be made when the need arises in the future. Nevertheless, I cannot resist providing one final verse from it, which is as follows:

->“If somehow these calculations become loose <note>inaccurate</note> during the time of observation, then one should repeatedly observe the conjunctions of the Moon, planets, and stars. Using the light of intelligence obtained from a pure and great teacher, one should move the centers of correction according to sound logic.”<-^2
<note>original Sanskrit: "कथमपि यदिदं चेद्‌दृग्मूरिकाले श्लथंस्यान्, मुहुरपि परिलक्ष्येन्दु ग्रहाद्य क्षयोगम् ॥ सदमलगुरुतुल्यप्राप्तबुद्धिप्रकाशैः कथित सदुपपत्त्या शुद्धिकेन्द्रै प्रचाल्ये ॥"</note>

After reading these excerpts, who could deny that our ancient teachers and scientists, through rational arguments, demonstrated the necessity that observational...

<meta>This page continues the argument for periodic astronomical corrections, transitioning from the general necessity of observation (Vedha) discussed on page 114 to the specific mathematical methods of the 'Spashtadhikara' (Chapter on Planetary Correction). It introduces the trigonometric methods used by ancient Indian astronomers to calculate planetary positions.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

# Chapter on True Planetary Correction

To maintain accuracy, it is necessary to revise the <term>Siddhanta</term> <note>traditional astronomical treatises</note> from time to time. Only with these corrections should the <term>Tithi</term> <note>lunar day</note>, <term>Yoga</term> <note>angular relationship between Sun and Moon</note>, <term>Karana</term> <note>half a Tithi</note>, and <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>lunar mansion</note> be determined for performing all worldly duties. Even a modern "expert in English mathematics" could not find cause to say more in support of his own position than what the ancient teachers have already stated.

> The eighth part of the total minutes in a <term>Rashi</term> is called the first <term>Jya</term>.
> Divide this by itself, subtract the quotient from it, and add the result to itself to obtain the second Jya. || 15 ||
> By dividing all subsequent Sine-sums by the first Jya and adding the results to the difference of previous segments, the twenty-four Sine-sums are derived in succession. || 16 ||
<note>original Sanskrit: "राशिलिप्ताष्टमो भाग: प्रथमं ज्यार्धमुच्यते । तत्तद्विभक्त लब्धोनमिश्रितं तद् द्वितीयकम् ॥१५॥ आद्यनैवं क्रमात्पिण्डान्भक्तवा लब्धोनितैर्युतैः । खण्डकैस्स्युश्चतुर्विंशज्ज्यार्धपिण्डाः क्रमादमी ॥१६॥"</note>

**Translation** — (15) The total number of <term>Kalas</term> <note>minutes of arc</note> in one <term>Rashi</term> <note>zodiac sign of 30 degrees</note> divided by eight is called the first '<term>Jya</term>' <note>Sine</note>. If you divide this by itself and subtract the quotient from the original value, then add the remainder back to the first Jya, the second Jya is calculated. (16) In this same manner, by dividing all the accumulated Sine-sums by the first Jya, subtracting those quotients from the segments, and adding the result to the previous Sine-sum, you find the next Sine-sum. In this sequence, the twenty-four <term>Jya-pindas</term> <note>Sine values</note> are established.

**Science Commentary** — A discussion on what <term>Jya</term> <note>Sine</note> is and how its value is derived through <term>Geometry</term> was provided in the commentary on verse 60 of the <term>Chapter on Mean Motion</term>. One should refer to the second diagram provided under that verse. In verse 15 above, the word '<term>Jyardha</term>' <note>half-chord</note> is used instead of 'Jya'; one should not be confused by this. Both are considered to have the same meaning. The term 'Jyardha' is used because the simplest way to find the 'Jya' of any angle (AOB) is to construct a <term>Sector</term> where the central angle is double the desired angle, then draw the <term>Jeeva</term> <note>chord</note> of that arc and halve it. This half of the chord (Jyardha) is the Sine of the desired angle. Therefore, Jyardha and Jya are synonymous (see Figure 24).

This verse also reveals that the ancient masters saw no difference between the <term>Dhanu</term> <note>arc</note> and the <term>Jya</term> <note>Sine chord</note> for the 8th part of a Rashi—that is, 3¾ degrees or 225 minutes. Following this, the method for finding the Sines of angles that are double, triple, or quadruple of 3¾ degrees is explained. Briefly, in the language of <term>Algebra</term>, the method can be written as follows: —

# Surya Siddhanta

(Geometric diagram showing the derivation of sine values. Point **A** is connected to **U** and **Uu**. A horizontal line from **A** passes through chord **U-Uu** at point **I** and ends at the arc at point **Aa**.)

->Figure 24<-

If p = 3 $\frac{3}{4}$ <term>degrees</term> <note>original: अंश (Ansh)</note> = 225' <note>The ' symbol denotes minutes of arc; 225 minutes is 1/8th of a zodiac sign (30 degrees)</note>
Then <term>Jya</term> <note>The Indian Sine function, representing a semi-chord</note> p = 225'

Jya 7 $\frac{1}{2}$ degrees = Jya 2p = Jya p + Jya p - $\frac{\text{Jya p}}{\text{Jya p}}$ = 225' + 225' - 1'
= 449' ;

Jya 11 $\frac{1}{4}$ degrees = Jya 3p = Jya 2p + Jya p - $\frac{\text{Jya p + Jya 2 p}}{\text{Jya p}}$
= 449' + 225' - 3 = 671' ;

Jya 15° = Jya 4p = Jya 3p + Jya p - $\frac{\text{Jya p + Jya 2 p + Jya 3 p}}{\text{Jya p}}$
= 671' + 225' - (1 + 2 + 3) = 890' ;

In the same manner, Jya (s + 1) p
= Jya (s p) + Jya p - $\frac{\text{Jya p + Jya 2 p + ... Jya (s p)}}{\text{Jya p}}$

The <term>derivation</term> <note>original: उपपत्ति (Upapatti)</note> of this, according to <term>Mahamahopadhyaya</term> <note>an honorary title for a "great, great teacher"</note> Bapudeva Shastri,$^1$ is as follows:—

Suppose, Jya p - Jya 0 = $T_1$,
Jya 2p - Jya p = $T_2$,
Jya 3p - Jya 2p = $T_3$,

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the True Places of the Planets</header>

# Chapter on the True Places of the Planets
<note>original: स्पष्टाधिकार (Spashtadhikara)</note>

-> <term>Sine</term> <note>original: ज्या (Jya)</note> n p — Sine (n — 1) p = $T_n$ <-

-> and Sine (n + 1) p — Sine n p = $T_{n+1}$ <-

Then, $T_1$ — $T_2$ = 2 Sine p — Sine 2 p
= 2 Sine p — 2 Sine p <term>Cosine</term> p* <note>original: कोज्या (Kojya)</note>
= 2 Sine p ( 1 — Cosine p )
= 2 Sine p × <term>Versed Sine</term> p<sup>× ×</sup> (1) <note>original: उज्ज्या (Ujjya)</note>

$T_2$ — $T_3$ = 2 Sine 2 p — Sine p — Sine 3 p
= 2 Sine 2 p — Sine p — (3 Sine p — 4 Sine<sup>3</sup> p)
= 2 Sine 2 p — 4 Sine p + 4 Sine<sup>3</sup> p
= 2 Sine 2 p — 4 Sine p ( 1 — Sine<sup>2</sup> p)
= 2 Sine 2 p — 4 Sine p × Cosine<sup>2</sup> p
= 2 Sine 2 p — 2 Sine p × Cosine p × 2 Cosine p
= 2 Sine 2 p — 2 Sine 2 p × Cosine p
= 2 Sine 2 p ( 1 — Cosine p )
= 2 Sine 2 p × Versed Sine p (2)

$T_3$ — $T_4$ = 2 Sine 3 p — Sine 2 p — Sine 4 p
= 2 Sine 3 p — (Sine 2 p + Sine 4 p)
= 2 Sine 3 p — 2 Sine 3 p × Cosine p<sup>× × ×</sup>
= 2 Sine 3 p (1 — Cosine p)
= 2 Sine 3 p × Versed Sine p (3)

In the same manner, $T_n$ — $T_{n+1}$ = 2 Sine np — Sine (n — 1) p — Sine (n + 1) p
= 2 Sine np — {Sine (n — 1) p + Sine (n + 1) p}
= 2 Sine np — 2 Sine np Cosine p
= 2 Sine np ( 1 — Cosine p )
= 2 Sine np × Versed Sine p (n)

---

* Cosine <note>original: कोज्या (Kojya)</note> = <term>Kotijya</term> <note>original: कोटिज्या</note> = cosine
<sup>× ×</sup> Versed Sine <note>original: उज्ज्या (Ujjya)</note> = <term>Utkramajya</term> <note>original: उत्क्रमज्या</note> = versed sine = (1 — cosine)
<sup>× × ×</sup> See Hall and Knight's Trigonometry page 113.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof for the trigonometric sine tables. While the previous page established the relationship between differences in sines, this page sums those equations to derive a general recursive formula. It also begins the list of specific Sine values for the twenty-four 225-minute arcs of a quadrant, encoded in Sanskrit verse.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

Now, by adding the equal sides of the equations (1), (2), (3)...(n):

$T_1$ — $T_{n+1}$ = 2 <term>Versed Sine</term> <note>original: उज्या (Ujya)</note> p (Sine p + Sine 2p + ... Sine np)

However, $T_1$ — $T_{n+1}$ = Sine p + Sine np — Sine (n + 1) p

$\therefore$ Sine p + Sine np — Sine (n + 1) p = 2 Versed Sine p
                          × (Sine p + Sine 2p + ... Sine np)

$\therefore$ Sine (n + 1) p = Sine np + Sine p — 2 Versed Sine p
                          (Sine p + Sine 2p + ... Sine np)

Here, p = $3^\circ 45'$ = 225' <note>The standard arc segment used in ancient Indian trigonometry.</note>

$\therefore$ 2 Versed Sine p = 2 Versed Sine 225' = 2 (1 — <term>Cosine</term> <note>original: कोज्या (Kojya)</note> 225')
            = 2 (1 — .9978) = 2 × .0011 = $\frac{44}{10000}$ = $\frac{1}{227}$
            = $\frac{1}{225}$ <note>The text notes this is "with a slight difference" (स्वल्पान्तर से), simplifying the fraction for manual calculation.</note>

$\therefore$ Sine (n + 1) p = Sine np + Sine p — $\frac{1}{225} \times$
                          (Sine p + Sine 2p + ... Sine np)

> Elements-twins, then marks-oceans-Vedas; form-earth-mountains-seasons;
> Void-marks-eight; five-void-arrows; arrows-earth-qualities-moon. ॥17॥
> <note>This verse uses the 'Bhutasankhya' system where words represent numbers: e.g., 'Vedas' = 4, 'Twins' = 2. Read right-to-left or in groups, they provide the Sine values: 225, 449, 671, 890, 1105, 1315.</note>
> Void-moon-beyond-fortitude; qualities-holes-void-fire-creepers. ॥18॥
> Sages-six-twins-eyes; moon-fire-made-twins. ॥19॥
> Five-eight-senses-eyes-earth; elephants-fire-mountains-horses. ॥20॥
> Holes-five-eight-twins; wealth-mountains-marks-twins likewise.
> Made-eight-void-fires; mountains-mountains-fire-fires. ॥21॥
> Six-five-eyes-qualities; moon-eyes-fire-fires.
> Twins-mountains-fire-fires; holes-void-oceans-fires. ॥22॥
> Form-fire-essence-qualities; wealth-three-made-fires.
> Subtracting in reverse order from the radius, these are the <term>Versed Sines</term>. <note>original: उत्क्रमज्यार्धपिण्डकाः (Utkramajyardhapindakah)</note> ॥23॥

**Translation of the values** — (17) 225, 449, 671, 890, 1105, 1315; (18) 1520, 1716, 1910, 2093; (19) 2267, 2431, 2585, 2728; (20) 2859, 2978, 3089, 3185; (21) 3276, 3359, 3431, 3489; (22) 3535, 3570, 3593, 3600.

<note>These numbers represent the Sines of multiples of $3^\circ 45'$ in a circle with radius 3438.</note>

<page-num>116</page-num>
# Chapter on True Positions
<note>original: Spashtadhikara</note>

2678, 3084, 3177; (21) 3256, 3321, 3372, 3406; (22) 3431, 3438. These values in <term>minutes of arc</term><note>original: कला (Kala)</note> correspond respectively to $3\frac{3}{4}^\circ$, $7\frac{1}{2}^\circ$, $11\frac{1}{4}^\circ$, $15^\circ$ and so on. They are the <term>Sines</term><note>original: ज्या (Jya)</note> of the twenty-four segments of a right angle. If you subtract these in reverse order from the <term>Radius</term><note>original: त्रिज्या (Trijya)</note> value of 3438, you will find the <term>Versed Sines</term><note>original: उत्क्रम ज्या (Utkram-jya)</note> of the twenty-four segments of a right angle. Their values are also provided in the following five verses.

**Scientific Commentary —** There does not seem to be a need to say much more on this matter. Following these next five verses, the values of these Sines and Versed Sines will be compared with values calculated using modern methods.

The rule written to determine the value of the Versed Sine is very simple and fundamental. If the final Sine value, 3438, is subtracted from 3438, zero remains, which is the Versed Sine of zero degrees. If 3431 is subtracted, 7 remains, which is the Versed Sine of 225 minutes ($3^\circ 45'$). This can be understood through geometry as follows—In Figure 24, if Angle $U A B$ is an angle of 225 minutes, then the arc $U B$ is 225', the line $U I$ is 225' (approximately), the Sine $A I$ is 3431, and the distance $I B$ is 7. This value of $I B$ is the Versed Sine of the angle $U A I$. In this same way, the Sines and Versed Sines of other <term>segments</term><note>original: पिण्ड (Pinda)</note> can be known.

->Sages, holes-twins, tastes-sixes, lords of sages;<-
->Two-eights, form-six-twos, oceans-arrows-fires. ॥23॥<-
->Void-seasons-Vedas, nines-eights-aims, directions-elephants-aims-elephants;<-
->Mountains-void-void-moons, form-mountains-Shankaras. ॥24॥<-
->Arrows-atoms-fires, serpents-eyes-arrows-moons;<-
->Nines-form-mountains, elephants-one-nines-moons. ॥25॥<-
->Qualities-Ashvins-form-eyes, fires-fires-qualities-Ashvins;<-
->Vasus-oceans-aims-twins, horses-mountains-Ashvins. ॥26॥<-
->Nines-eights-nines-eyes, fires-one-twins-fires;<-
->Eights-fires-oceans-qualities, the Versed Sine half-segments. ॥27॥<-
<note>These verses use 'Bhuta-sankhya,' a system where words represent numbers (e.g., 'Sages' = 7, 'Twins' = 2, 'Vedas' = 4). Numbers are usually read from right to left to form the full value.</note>

**Translation —** (23) 7, 26 <note>original: २६ (Note: The verse suggests 29, but the commentary lists 26)</note>, 66, 117, 182, 261, 354; (24) 460, 576, 710, 853, 1007, 1171; (25) 1345, 1528, 1716, 1818; (26) 2123, 2333, 2548, 2767; (27) 2986, 3213, and 3438 minutes are, in order, the segments of the Versed Sines.

# Chapter on True Planetary Positions
<note>Sanskrit: *Spashtadhikara*. This chapter explains how to calculate the actual (true) positions of planets in the sky, as opposed to their mean or average positions.</note>

**Terminology Reference:**

<term>Spashtadhikara</term><note>The specific chapter of an astronomical text dealing with the 'true' (spashta) positions of celestial bodies.</note>
<term>Jya</term><note>Sine; in this context, it refers to the R-Sine, where the radius is 3438.</note>
<term>Ansha</term><note>Degree; a unit of angular measurement.</note>
<term>Kala</term><note>Minute of arc; there are 60 minutes in one degree.</note>
<term>Kojya</term><note>Cosine; literally the 'sine of the complement'. In Indian mathematics, the cosine is found by taking the sine of the angle subtracted from 90 degrees.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a detailed table of trigonometric values from the Surya Siddhanta, comparing ancient Indian calculations of Sines and Versed Sines with modern values for angles up to 90 degrees. It demonstrates the high level of accuracy achieved by early Indian astronomers using a radius of 3438 minutes.</summary>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues from the table of trigonometric values on page 121, moving from the data itself into a scientific commentary on how these ratios are applied in Indian astronomy, specifically regarding the calculation of planetary declination.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>Surya Siddhanta</header>

**Scientific Commentary**—In the *Surya Siddhanta*, only these specific ratios of <term>Trigonometry</term><note>Sanskrit: *Trikonamiti*; the mathematical study of the relationships between the sides and angles of triangles.</note> are provided. To determine the <term>Cosine</term><note>Sanskrit: *Kotijya*.</note> from these, the following rule is employed: if the <term>Sine</term><note>Sanskrit: *Jya*.</note> of an angle is known, then the value of the Cosine of that angle is the same as the Sine of its complement (the angle subtracted from 90°). In other words, the Sine of any angle is equal to the Cosine of its complementary angle. The value of the <term>Tangent</term><note>Sanskrit: *Sparsh rekha*; literally "touching line."</note> is not given explicitly as it is in modern mathematics, but it is used indirectly by dividing the Sine of an angle by its Cosine.

If an angle's value falls between the specific intervals given in the previously mentioned tables, the method for determining its Sine, Cosine, or <term>Versed Sine</term><note>Sanskrit: *Utkramajya*.</note> using the <term>Rule of Three</term><note>Sanskrit: *Trairashika*; a mathematical method for finding a fourth value from three known values based on proportion (linear interpolation).</note> is explained in the subsequent verses 31–34. Similarly, these verses also describe the method for finding the "bow" (the angle or arc) if the value of the Sine is already known.

The great mathematician Bhaskaracharya has provided even more refined and precise methods for determining the Sine and Cosine.

Various synonyms for Sine are used, such as <term>Kramajya</term>, <term>Bhujajya</term>, <term>Bahujya</term>, and <term>Ardhajya</term>. For the Cosine, the term <term>Lambajya</term> is also frequently used.

->The sine of the maximum declination is one thousand three hundred and ninety-seven.<-
->Multiply that by the sine [of the longitude], divide by the radius; the arc of the result is the declination. [Verse 28]<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "Paramāpakramajyā tu saptarandhraguṇendavaḥ | tadguṇā jyā trijavāptā taccāpaṃ krāntiriṣyate ||28||"</note>

**Translation**—(28) The value of the <term>Sine of maximum declination</term><note>Sanskrit: *Kranti Jya*. The declination is the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator.</note> is 1397 minutes. By multiplying this by the Sine (of the planet's longitude) and dividing the result by the <term>Radius</term><note>Sanskrit: *Trijya*; the standard radius used in these calculations is 3438 minutes of arc.</note>, the resulting value is the Sine of the declination; the arc corresponding to that Sine is the value of the declination itself.

---

->Figure 25<-
<note>This diagram illustrates the geometric relationship between the ecliptic and the celestial equator to calculate the declination (Kranti) of a point. The vertices व (Va), स (Sa), and प (Pa) likely represent the equinox, the planet's position, and the projection on the equator.</note>

<summary>This page explains the trigonometric principles of the Surya Siddhanta, specifically the relationship between Sine and Cosine, and provides the formula for calculating a planet's declination using the Sine of its longitude and the maximum declination.</summary>
<keywords>Trigonometry, Sine (Jya), Cosine (Kotijya), Declination (Kranti), Rule of Three (Trairashika), Surya Siddhanta, Bhaskaracharya, Indian Mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the scientific commentary on trigonometric ratios, specifically transitioning from general Sine definitions to the practical calculation of planetary declination using spherical triangles.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Computation of True Positions</header>

**Scientific Commentary**—In this verse, it is demonstrated how the <term>Sine</term><note>Sanskrit: *Jya*.</note> is applied in practice. Furthermore, the rule governing the relationship between the sides and angles of a <term>right-angled spherical triangle</term> is also explained. The value of the Sine of the <term>maximum declination</term><note>Sanskrit: *Paramakranti*; the greatest distance the Sun reaches from the celestial equator, known as the obliquity of the ecliptic.</note> is stated to be 1387 minutes. From this, it is understood that the value of the maximum declination is 24°; this is because the Sine of 24° calculated using the aforementioned method results in 1387 minutes, although by precise modern calculation, it is actually the Sine of 23° 58' 31''.

In the provided Figure 25, V (the <term>Vernal Equinox</term><note>Sanskrit: *Vasant-Sampat*.</note>) to S is a segment of the <term>Ecliptic</term><note>Sanskrit: *Krantivritta*; the Sun's apparent path.</note> and V to P is a segment of the <term>Celestial Equator</term><note>Sanskrit: *Vishuvadvritta*.</note>. S-P is a segment of a secondary to the equator (a declination circle)—that is, a segment of a circle that passes through the celestial pole and forms a right angle at point P on the celestial equator. The angle S-V-P is the angle between the ecliptic and the equator (the <term>obliquity of the ecliptic</term>), which according to the verse is 24°. The distance of point S from the Vernal Equinox (V-S) is the <term>celestial longitude</term><note>Sanskrit: *Bhoga-ansh*.</note> of point S on the ecliptic. The distance of point S from the equator (S-P), where the angle S-P-V is a right angle, is called the <term>declination</term><note>Sanskrit: *Kranti*.</note> of point S. This is also referred to as the <term>Apakrama</term>. According to the rule provided:

-> Sine (V S) × 1387 <-
-> ———————— = Sine (S P) <-
-> 3438 <-

-> Or: Sine (V S) × Sine (S V P) <-
-> ———————————— = Sine (S P) <-
-> Radius <-

If the <term>Radius</term><note>Sanskrit: *Trijya*.</note> is taken as 1 instead of 3438, as is the modern custom, then the value 0.4067 must be used in place of 1387 minutes. This simplifies the multiplication and division, resulting in the following formula:

-> Sine (V S) × Sine (S V P) = Sine (S P) <-

With some minor differences, this is known today as one of "Napier’s Rules," which was published by the mathematician named **Napier** from Edinburgh in 1614 AD (or 1671 Vikram Samvat) in his work *A Description of the Admirable Table of Logarithms* <note>original Latin: "Mirifici Logarithmorum Canonis Descriptio"</note>. To remember Napier’s rules, the following device is used:—

---

*See Todhunter and Leathem’s *Spherical Trigonometry*, printed in 1861, page 50.

<summary>This page explains the calculation of planetary declination using spherical trigonometry, specifically demonstrating that the Indian Sine of 1387 minutes corresponds to an obliquity of 24 degrees. It also draws a historical parallel between these ancient methods and Napier's Rules of spherical trigonometry published in the 17th century.</summary>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spherical trigonometry used in the Surya-Siddhanta commentary, moving from the practical application of Sines to the formal geometric definitions of "circular parts" and Napier's Rules for right-angled spherical triangles.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>Surya-Siddhanta</header>

Excluding the right angle of any right-angled spherical triangle, the two sides that form the right angle, the complement of the hypotenuse, and the complements of the other two angles are called the **circular parts** <note>Sanskrit: *Gol Khand*.</note> of the triangle. In this way, any right-angled spherical triangle has five circular parts. These five parts are arranged around a circle in the same sequence as they appear in the triangle. Suppose A-I-U <note>Original labels use Hindi vowels: अ (A), इ (I), उ (U).</note> is a

->Figure 26<-

spherical triangle. A, I, and U are the vertices <note>Points where the sides meet.</note> at which the sides I-A and U-A; A-I and U-I; and A-U and I-U meet. The angles U-A-I, A-I-U, and A-U-I are abbreviated using the letters A, I, and U. Similarly, the side I-U opposite angle A is represented by 'Ā', the side A-U opposite angle I is represented by 'Ī', and the side A-I opposite angle U is represented by 'Ū'. <note>The author uses a clever mnemonic: short vowels represent angles, while their corresponding long vowels represent the opposite sides.</note> The general rule is that the angles of the triangle are expressed with short vowels and their opposite sides are expressed with the corresponding long vowels. The sides of a spherical triangle are also measured in angular values <note>In spherical geometry, sides are arcs of great circles and are measured in degrees/minutes rather than linear units.</note>. If I is a right angle, then this triangle is called a right-angled spherical triangle. In that case, the side Ī opposite to it is called the <term>hypotenuse</term><note>Sanskrit: *Karna*.</note>. [See Figure 26]

In Napier's rule, excluding the right angle of the right-angled spherical triangle, the two adjacent sides Ā and Ū, the complement of angle A ($\frac{\pi}{2} - A$), the complement of the hypotenuse Ī ($\frac{\pi}{2} - Ī$), and the complement of angle U ($\frac{\pi}{2} - U$), these circular parts are written in a diagram as follows [See Figure 27]

<term>Right-angled spherical triangle</term><note>A triangle on the surface of a sphere where one of the angles is 90 degrees.</note>, <term>Circular parts</term><note>The five specific elements of a right-angled spherical triangle used in Napier's analogies.</note>, <term>Napier’s Rules</term><note>A set of formulas created by John Napier (1550–1617) to easily solve for any part of a right-angled spherical triangle.</note>, <term>Hypotenuse</term><note>The side opposite the right angle.</note>, <term>Complement</term><note>The value required to complete a 90-degree angle; mathematically, 90° minus the given angle or side.</note>, <term>Surya-Siddhanta</term><note>One of the most significant ancient Indian treatises on astronomy.</note>

<summary>This page defines the "circular parts" of a right-angled spherical triangle and establishes a naming convention using Sanskrit/Hindi vowels for vertices and sides. It introduces Napier’s Rules, which are used to solve astronomical calculations involving the celestial sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spherical trigonometry used in the Surya-Siddhanta commentary. It moves from defining the "circular parts" of a right-angled spherical triangle to explaining Napier’s Rules (the rules of the circular parts) and how they relate to the calculation of planetary positions through the concept of anomalies.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the True Positions of Planets</header>

->Figure 27<-

Choose any one of these five and designate it as the **middle part** <note>Sanskrit: *madhya khanḍa*</note>. The two parts located on either side of the chosen middle part are called the **adjacent parts** <note>Sanskrit: *āsanna khanḍa*</note>; the remaining two parts are called the **opposite parts** <note>Sanskrit: *sanmukha khanḍa*</note>. Now, Napier’s rules can be written as follows:—

(1) Sine of the middle part = Product of the tangents of the adjacent parts.
(2) Sine of the middle part = Product of the cosines of the opposite parts.

This second rule was utilized in the verse below at least a thousand years before Napier <note>John Napier (1550–1617) is credited with these rules in Western history, but the author notes their presence in ancient Indian astronomical texts like the Surya-Siddhanta.</note>.

> Subtract the mean position of the planet from its slow apogee, and likewise subtract it from its fast apogee.
> The remainder is the anomaly; from this, the quadrants, and then the Sine and Cosine are determined. || 29 ||
> In an odd quadrant, the Sine is from the traversed part and the Cosine from the part to be traversed.
> In an even quadrant, the Sine is from the part to be traversed and the Cosine from the traversed part. || 30 ||
<note>original Sanskrit:
ग्रहं संशोधय मन्दोच्चात् तथा शीघ्राद्विशोध्य च ।
शेषं केन्द्रं पदैस्तस्माद् भुज ज्या कोटिरेव च ॥ २९ ॥
गताद्भुजज्या विषमे गम्यात्कोटि: पदे भवेत् ।
समे तु गम्याद्बाहुज्या कोटिज्या तु गताद् भवेत् ॥ ३० ॥</note>

**Translation** — (29) By subtracting the mean position of a planet from the positions of its <term>slow apogee</term><note>Sanskrit: *Mandocca*; the point in a planet's orbit furthest from Earth where its motion appears slowest.</note> and its <term>fast apogee</term><note>Sanskrit: *Shighrocca*; associated with the planet's conjunction with the Sun.</note>, the remainders are respectively called the <term>slow anomaly</term><note>Sanskrit: *Manda Kendra*.</note> and the <term>fast anomaly</term><note>Sanskrit: *Shighra Kendra*.</note>. From these, determine the <term>quadrant</term><note>Sanskrit: *Pada*; the 90-degree divisions of the circle.</note> and, knowing the quadrant, find the **Sine** <note>Sanskrit: *Bhuja-jya*</note> and...

<summary>This page explains Napier's Rules for right-angled spherical triangles and asserts their ancient Indian origin. It introduces the calculation of planetary anomalies (Manda and Shighra Kendra) used to determine true planetary positions.</summary>
<keywords>Napier's Rules, spherical trigonometry, Manda Kendra, Shighra Kendra, Sine, Cosine, Surya-Siddhanta, planetary anomalies, apogee</keywords>
